<meta>This page serves as the author's preface, establishing the personal and academic origins of the translation of the Digenes Akrites epic.</meta>

# PREFACE

MANY years ago my friend Petros Petrides, himself born at Nigde in Cappadocia <note>A historical region in central Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey</note>, used to tell me about the stories of Digenes, which were to inspire his own dramatic symphony first performed in Athens in May 1940. When at last I started to read for myself this heroic poem of *Twyborn the Borderer* <note>A literal translation of "Digenes Akrites": "Digenes" meaning "of two races" and "Akrites" meaning "border-guard"</note>, who guarded the marches of the Byzantine Empire in the tenth or eleventh century, I was alone in the country, and found my way through the text as best I could; if some of the notes now published sound like an ingenuous soliloquy, it is from that early period they must have survived—*reader, hold these things excused by their own time; I was an exile* <note>original: "excusata suo tempore, lector, habe; exsul eram." A poignant reference to the author's isolation or displacement during the work's composition</note>. Later on I was back in London, and there enjoyed the advice and encouragement of Norman Baynes, of F. H. Marshall, and of Romilly Jenkins; somehow or other my translation and a few lectures were on paper in 1938; and not long afterwards, thanks perhaps largely to their continued interest, I found myself living in College at Oxford and on the same staircase as my friend R. M. Dawkins. Many were now the anxious days and black nights in the course of which we talked about nearly everything in the world. I do not remember that in fact we ever talked about the Warden of the Marches. But Dawkins had such a lively knowledge of the land and the people and the language of Greece, and such an affection for them all, ancient, medieval, and modern, that his talk and his example were a university; and this is the place in which I must say how much I owe consciously and unconsciously to his learning and judgement.

It was some years later, and I was no longer living in Oxford, when the Delegates of the Clarendon Press <note>The prestigious publishing house of Oxford University</note> gave me one of the pleasantest surprises of my life by undertaking the publication of this work. By this time most of my books had had to go into store; and as I had been brought up to fill every margin with notes—not to mention several cardboard boxes full of half-sheets of paper—many of my cherished references had now become inaccessible. Perhaps that does not matter as much as I thought it did. But it has taken a long time to reconstitute the commentary and do all that was necessary; and I am immensely grateful to the readers and experts of the Press whose patience and skill have been at my service on every page.

<summary>The author recounts the long development of his translation of the Digenes Akrites epic, acknowledging the influence of Greek friends and prominent Byzantine scholars during the pre-war and wartime years.</summary>
<keywords>Digenes Akrites, Byzantine epic, Cappadocia, Oxford University, Petros Petrides, R. M. Dawkins, Clarendon Press, literary history</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial preface, detailing the specific manuscript sources and previous scholarship used to establish the current text of Digenes Akritas.</meta>

# PREFACE

The Greek text here printed is that of the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>A 14th-century manuscript found in the Basilian monastery of Grottaferrata, Italy; it is considered the most "poetic" version of the epic.</note> of *Digenes* edited by Emile Legrand and published in Paris (by Maisonneuve) in 1892. A few passages to fill up gaps in the narrative have been borrowed from the Trebizond manuscript, edited by Constantine Sathas and Legrand and published in Paris in 1875; and from the Andros manuscript, edited by A. Mêliarakes and published in Athens in 1881, these passages amounting to 117 and 26 lines respectively. A second edition of Legrand’s text *revised and corrected* <note>original: "revue et corrigée"</note> was issued in 1902, but all the errors I have noticed appear in both editions. Legrand can never be sufficiently honoured for his great services to the study of post-classical Greek, and above all for his two large collections of *Monuments to serve the study of the Neo-Hellenic language* <note>original: "Monuments pour servir à l’étude de la langue néo-hellénique"</note>, but much of his work contains small inaccuracies. Some light may be thrown on these by an affectionate tribute he pays to his wife in the preface to the third edition (Paris, 1900) of the Cretan pastoral *The Fair Shepherdess*. He there tells us that in order to lighten his labours she had learned to read and write the Greek characters and so for many years had copied out all his Greek texts for him:

> ‘my wife transcribed with an exactness all the more surprising (which may seem paradoxical) because she knew the language less.’ <note>original: "ma femme transcrivait avec une exactitude d’autant plus surprenante (ce qui peut sembler paradoxal) qu’elle savait moins la langue."</note>

Of the slips in his Grottaferrata text and *critical apparatus* <term>apparatus criticus: the organized body of notes at the bottom of a page that lists variant readings or corrections from different manuscripts</term> a few erratic punctuations and accents have been silently corrected. Otherwise his text and apparatus have been exactly reprinted and any emendations I have been obliged to make are recorded only in my own notes.

In his introduction Legrand recalls that on the publication in 1875, edited by Constantine Sathas and himself, of the Trebizond manuscript—the first to be discovered—the interest excited had led to the publication of the Oxford manuscript, which was included by S. P. Lampros in his *Greek Romances in Verse* <note>original: "Romans grecs en vers"</note> (Paris, 1880), and of the Andros manuscript, edited by Mêliarakes (Athens, 1881). Lampros in his introduction (pp. xc—ci) describes the manuscript which he had seen in the autumn of 1879 in the library of the Greek monastery at Grottaferrata near Frascati. It is a manuscript of the fourteenth century written on 79 leaves of paper (5 1/2 $\times$ 8 1/4 inches—14 $\times$ 21 cm.) of which the first 73 contain the text here printed.

Joseph Müller of the University of Turin had already called attention to this manuscript, and had himself made a copy of it which he had shown to Legrand and had subsequently presented to the German scholar Wilhelm Wagner (editor of *Medieval Greek*...

<summary>The editor explains the textual history of the Digenes Akritas epic, identifying the Grottaferrata manuscript as the primary source while acknowledging the pioneering but occasionally flawed work of Emile Legrand.</summary>
<keywords>Digenes Akritas, Grottaferrata manuscript, Emile Legrand, Byzantine epic, textual criticism, Constantine Sathas, S. P. Lampros, manuscript history</keywords>

# PREFACE

Texts, London, 1870; *Greek Songs of the Middle Ages* <note>original: "Carmina Graeca Medii Aevi"</note>, Leipzig, 1874; and *Three Greek Poems* <note>original: "Trois poèmes grecs"</note>, Berlin, 1881). After Wagner’s premature death in 1880 this copy had been returned to Müller and has never been heard of again.

Meanwhile Nicolas Polites in Athens, fired by the description given by Lampros, had asked the librarian at Grottaferrata <note>A Greek monastery near Rome, famous for its collection of Byzantine manuscripts.</note> to have the manuscript copied for him; and Legrand through the French Ministry of Foreign Affairs had asked for a loan of the precious volume which in due course reached him in Paris. Lampros and Polites withdrew in his favour, and Legrand with his friend Jean Psichari read over together the copy which he had made, and certified its accuracy, in May 1887.

The most important thing about my translation is that it follows the Greek line for line, so that the original can always be referred to without delay. This has involved a good deal of compression but few, if any, sacrifices. On the rare occasions on which some Greek word has had to be omitted the fact has been recorded in the notes. This does not apply to particles <note>Small, often untranslatable words used in Greek to indicate tone or to balance the rhythm of a verse.</note> which the Greek author uses almost indiscriminately in order to fill up his line. In order to empty the English line I have omitted 'fors', 'howevers', and 'yets' by the bushel, and 'buts' by the load; thus not only lightening the metre but improving the style. In English a minimum of such words is a condition of good writing, as a minimum of gestures is a condition of good speaking.

Secondly, this translation has been kept as literal as possible. First finished in December 1938, it had to be copied out. In the first ten weeks of 1939 it was not only copied out but completely rewritten from beginning to end, because almost every line as it came up for approval was changed so as to give a more literal representation of the Greek, even if it produced less tidy metre and less conventional English. One result of this insistence on literalness has been an occasional loosening of the metre; so that instead of five feet and ten syllables some lines (e.g. 3314) have three beats each followed by a varying number of unaccented syllables.

Thirdly, I have never tried to improve the Greek original. (Well, hardly ever.) Clumsy Greek has often been turned into clumsy English. I have resisted the temptation to conceal silliness or bathos <note>A sudden shift from a serious or elevated tone to a common or ridiculous one.</note>,

<summary>The author describes the 19th-century scholarly efforts to copy the Grottaferrata manuscript and outlines a translation philosophy prioritizing literal accuracy and line-for-line correspondence over poetic polish.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolas Polites, Spyridon Lampros, Grottaferrata, Émile Legrand, Jean Psichari, translation methodology, literalism, Greek particles, Byzantine poetry, manuscript history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translator's explanation of his stylistic choices, specifically focusing on the tension between natural speech and the artificiality of literary language.</meta>

# PREFACE

vi

and to make the English line more poetical than the Greek line which it is supposed to represent.

Generally speaking I have used a basis of ordinary conversational English; the fundamental rule for the structure of this groundwork being that there shall be no inversion of the natural order of words. To reinforce this natural order there has been an avoidance of conventionally poetical usages. I have never used the second person singular <note>The use of "thee" and "thou."</note>, except where God is addressed. The heroine, when she is referred to, perhaps with some mystical significance, as *the Girl* <note>original: "Korê" — a Greek term often referring to Persephone or a young woman of noble birth.</note>, is called the Girl rather than the Maiden or the Virgin.

On the other hand, although based on natural speech the diction is one definitely removed from that of common talk. It is what Aristotle in his *Poetics* calls *changed* <note>original: "exêllagmenê" — language that is modified or elevated above the commonplace.</note>; it is changed over from the customary *speech* <note>original: "dialektos" — the everyday language or vernacular.</note>, the conversation of ordinary life. This translation in fact uses a written language and not a spoken language. And why not? Does anybody suppose that the Greek was not an artificial language? It is only as a result of contemporary fashions, English as well as Greek, that one has to defend the perfectly natural division of the written from the spoken language. This division connotes the artificiality of the written language. But all writing is an artifice and it can never be anything else. The words commonly used in streets and fields are often better than others, but they are not in themselves patterns laid up in heaven.

Having laid down the ground, a written language free from inversions and poetical conventions, based on ordinary speech but clearly divided from it, we proceed to colour it with reminiscences of the Bible, of Shakespeare, of Milton, and of the Ballads; inevitable reminiscences, not because they are beautiful English, and recall beauty by association even when they have it not in the immediate use; but because these exactly represent the Greek of the ancient classics, which the editor <note>original: "redactor" — one who prepares a text for publication by revising or combining multiple sources.</note> of this version was attempting to revive, with painful but with loving artifice, in the middle of the eleventh century. His is certainly an artificial language. There is only one characteristic of his style which he shares with the good conversationalist and the good letter-writer: that he often begins a sentence without knowing how he is going to end it.

This is not the place to examine other characteristics of his style, notably his mixture both in vocabulary and in syntax of elements from every period, from Homer, from the Greek Old Testament <note>original: "Septuagint" — the earliest Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible.</note>, and from

<summary>The translator defends his use of an "artificial" written language that blends natural word order with literary echoes of the Bible and Shakespeare to mirror the 11th-century Greek style. He emphasizes that all writing is an artifice and justifies his specific word choices, such as using "the Girl" for Korê.</summary>
<keywords>translation theory, Aristotle's Poetics, literary artifice, Korê, redactor, linguistic style, 11th-century Greek, Septuagint</keywords>

# PREFACE

contemporary folk-song. This eclecticism <note>The practice of deriving ideas or style from a broad and diverse range of sources.</note> shocks the grammarians, and it shocks the Athenian demotics <note>Those who advocate for the use of "Demotic" or "popular" Greek as opposed to the formal, archaic style.</note>; but it does not shock anyone who has a sense and a love of the Greek language as a whole, to whom no part is unclean. It does not shock me, and I have sometimes even attempted to reproduce it in English. In passages where the Greek is peculiar, and possibly corrupt <note>A term used by scholars to describe a text that has been altered or damaged through centuries of copying, making the original meaning difficult to discern.</note>, or the words rare or remarkable, or the meaning doubtful, the original was sometimes quoted in the notes, without comment, so as to call attention to the fact that further elucidation was required. But many of these mere exclamation marks were afterwards left out in order to save space.

The lines of this edition are numbered on two systems. The numbers in the margins of the right-hand pages, that is of the English version, include not only Grottaferrata <note>The Grottaferrata manuscript is one of the primary sources for the epic of Digenes Akritas.</note> but also the passages borrowed from the Trebizond and Andros versions to fill up gaps in the narrative. This numeration, running from 1 to 3850, is used in the notes and in the Index of Greek Words. The Greek text of the Grottaferrata and other versions on the left-hand pages have each book numbered separately—(GRO Book I, 337 lines; Book II, 300 lines; Book III, 343 lines; Book IV, 1093 lines; Book V, 289 lines; Book VI, 845 lines; Book VII, 229 lines; Book VIII, 313 lines; TRE and AND, Books I to X) ; this numeration given on the left-hand pages is to be associated with the number of the book in Roman figures given at the top of the page, and both the line-numbers and the Roman book-numbers must be used for reference to the Greek text. (Note, however, that the TREbizond and ANDros versions, each divided into ten books, are not numbered by separate books but straight through from beginning to end—3182 and 4778 lines respectively.)

->J. N. M.<-

# INTRODUCTION

## 1. DISCOVERY

In the middle of last century nothing was known of the Byzantine epic of Digenes Akrites <note>The name "Digenes" means "Two-Blood," referring to his mixed Arab and Greek parentage; "Akrites" refers to the "Akritai," the frontiersmen who guarded the borders of the Byzantine Empire.</note>; but the atmosphere had been prepared by the publication of several ballads of what is now called the Akritic Cycle (a name first used by Legrand in 1874). The discussion of these—(in particular a paper by Büdinger who had used the headline ‘A Greek Medieval Popular Epic’, although the Song in question, *The Sons of Andronikos*, was only seventy lines in length)—had opened the way for further revelations of an heroic age of medieval Greece.

(i) The discovery was made at Trebizond <note>A historic Greek city on the Black Sea coast, now Trabzon in modern Turkey.</note>. Manuscripts could not be photographed at Trebizond, and after considerable correspondence the precious work was sent to Paris by post; and in 1875 Sathas and Legrand published *The Exploits of Digenes Akritas—a Byzantine epic of the tenth century . . . according to the unique manuscript of Trebizond* <note>original: "Les Exploits de Digénis Akritas—épopée byzantine du dixième siècle . . . d’après le manuscrit unique de Trébizonde"</note>. The manuscript is said to be not earlier than the sixteenth century. There are several gaps in it. The poem is divided into ten books of which the first and the last are missing altogether; there remain 3,182 lines of it.

This manuscript was afterwards returned to the hands of Savvas Ioannides, the Trebizond schoolmaster, author of a statistical history of Trebizond, who in May 1868 had received it from a monk at the monastery of Soumelá. (For the present state of the monastery of Soumelá see D. Talbot Rice in *Byzantion*, v. 72 ff.) He published another edition of it at Constantinople in 1887,¹ and then deposited it in the library of the Philological Society <note>original: "Filologikos Syllogos"</note>. All the archives of this institution are said to have been removed to Ankara <note>original: "Angora"</note> by the Turkish Government and the fate of the Digenes manuscript is unknown; but in the opinion of Kyriakides (conveyed in a private letter 7 April 1936) it must, for the present at least, be regarded as lost.

(ii) Before the learned world had had time to digest this, several

<note>¹ *Medieval Epic / from the Trebizond manuscript / Basil Digenes Akrites / The Cappadocian / edited with commentary / by / Savvas Ioannides / . . . in Constantinople / . . . 1887.* <note>original: "Epos Mesaiônikon / ek tou kheirografou Trapezountos / O Basileios Digenes Akritês / O Kappadokês / ypomnêmatisthen ekdidotai / ypo / Sabba Iôannidou / . . . en Kônstantinoupolei / . . . 1887."</note></note>

The text is fairly correct and is supplemented by about 700 lines from the Oxford <term>OXF: The Oxford manuscript version of the poem</term> and Andros <term>AND: The manuscript version found on the island of Andros</term> versions. Ioannides regards Digenes as a fully historical figure of the Cappadocian aristocracy who lived from 936 to 969 against a background slightly adjusted from the chronicle of Kedrenos <note>George Kedrenos was an 11th-century Byzantine historian.</note>.

<summary>This page details the 19th-century rediscovery of the Digenes Akrites epic, beginning with the now-lost Trebizond manuscript found at the Soumelá monastery. It establishes the scholarly context of the "Akritic Cycle" and the early editions produced by Legrand, Sathas, and Ioannides.</summary>

<keywords>Digenes Akrites, Akritic Cycle, Trebizond manuscript, Savvas Ioannides, Soumelá Monastery, Byzantine epic, medieval Greek literature, manuscript history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction's survey of the various manuscript versions of the Digenes Akritas epic, following the discussion of the Trebizond manuscript.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

other manuscripts were discovered; beginning with the version found in Andros in 1878 and published by Mêliarakes in 1881, a manuscript of the sixteenth century practically complete in 4,778 lines, conveniently filling the gaps in the Trebizond manuscript <note>The Trebizond manuscript was the first discovered version of the epic, but it was heavily damaged.</note> which it closely follows. The Mêliarakes edition was reprinted in 1920. This Andros manuscript is now in the National Library at Athens, and is referred to by Kalonaros as the Athens version (which he prints first in his collection).

The Mêliarakes introduction refers to a Greek newspaper of 23 November 1880, reporting that Legrand had discovered in Constantinople, and proposed to publish, another version, the oldest known, written in iambics <note>A metrical form in poetry consisting of short and long syllables.</note> and containing the name of the poet. At that time (towards the end of 1880), Legrand must have known of the existence of the Grottaferrata and Oxford manuscripts besides the Andros manuscript, which was still in the press; he may also have heard about the description of other versions in an unpublished Athos manuscript of the monk Dapontes just then discovered (see below). Legrand, discussing his hopes of one or all of these in a conversation with reference to his own edition of the Trebizond manuscript, may have originated this newspaper paragraph of which nothing more seems to be known.

Another short version, in prose, said to have been discovered in Constantinople by Dr. Mordtmann, is mentioned in Legrand's preface to Grottaferrata (pp. xi, xxii—<note>original: "signalée par le Dr. Mordtmann à Sabbas Ioannidis"</note> 'pointed out by Dr. Mordtmann to Sabbas Ioannidis'); and is also referred to hopefully by Polites (*Concerning the National Epic* <note>original: "Peri tou Ethnikou Epous"</note>, p. 5); by Hesseling in his introduction to ESC <note>The "Eskorial" or "Escorial" version of the manuscript.</note> (*Folklore* <note>original: "Laografia"</note>, iii. 551); and by Ioannides himself in the introduction to his Constantinople edition of 1887.

(iii) Next came a manuscript of the fourteenth century discovered at the Greek monastery of Grottaferrata near Frascati in 1879, which was published by Legrand in 1892 (reprinted 1902).

(iv) The incomplete 'Madrid' version of only 1,867 lines was discovered by Krumbacher in the Escorial library in 1904 and was published by Hesseling in 1912.

(v) A rhymed version was published by Lampros in 1880 from a manuscript at Lincoln College, Oxford, which had the advantage of being signed by the writer, a monk of Chios, by name Ignatios Petritzes, who finished his task on 25 November 1670.

(vi) A prose version, written by Meletios Vlastos of Chios in 1632,

It details the scholarly efforts of late 19th-century researchers to reconstruct the poem from these diverse sources.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly introduction regarding the various manuscript versions of the Digenes Akritas epic. It specifically discusses the 15th-century reworkings, the identity of the redactor Eustathios, and begins an analysis of the Escorial (ESC) version's relationship to folk ballads.</meta>

# VERSIONS

The fifteenth-century scribe seems to have been reworking a more scholarly text that was nearly identical to the <term>Trebizond (TRE)</term> <note>A manuscript found in the Sumela Monastery near Trebizond, considered a key source for the poem.</note> manuscript, often adopting its lines exactly as they were written. (For more on how rhyme was introduced into the text, see the work of <term>Krumbacher</term>, page 700.) 

Perhaps this romantic-minded monk was <term>Eustathios</term>, who is mentioned in the opening lines as writing the story for a dear young friend named <term>Manuel</term>. Surprisingly, some have believed that these lines actually reveal the name of the original author of the *<term>Digenes</term>* <note>Digenes Akritas, the legendary hero of the Byzantine borderlands.</note>. A Greek scholar, <term>A. Hatzês</term>, attempted—without much success—to prove through linguistic analysis that the *<term>Digenes</term>* was written by <term>Eustathios</term> (also known as <term>Eumathios</term>) <term>Makrembolites</term>, who authored the <term>Byzantine</term> prose romance *<term>Hysmine and Hysminias</term>*. (See the *<term>Byzantine-Modern Greek Yearbook</term>* <note>original title: "Byz. Neugr. Jahrb."</note> volume ix (1931), pages 256 and following; and for <term>Grégoire</term>’s critique and the list of <term>Hatzês</term>’s works, see *<term>Byzantion</term>*, volume vi, page 482.)

It should have been clear to any observer that the heading of the <term>Andros (AND)</term> <note>A manuscript discovered on the island of Andros.</note> version does not mean <term>Eustathios</term> was the original creator. <term>Petritzes</term>, the scribe of the <term>Oxford (OXF)</term> <note>A version of the poem held in the Bodleian Library at Oxford.</note> manuscript, uses even more explicit language—saying <gloss>I have arranged and composed it</gloss> <note>original Greek: "esyntaxa kai ’synthesa to"</note>—and yet we know he was merely the one who turned it into verse. <term>Eustathios</term> may simply have been the editor who added the rhymes and other romantic flourishes. As for the author of *<term>Hysmine and Hysminias</term>*, he exists in a completely different literary world of over-refined and artificial style.

## IV

The <term>Escorial (ESC)</term> <note>A manuscript kept at the Escorial library in Spain, notable for its more "folk" or "popular" tone.</note> version is extremely incomplete. It begins at roughly the same point as the <term>Trebizond (TRE)</term> version but omits many incidents and countless small details; many of its episodes and lines are even out of sequence. It is written in a style that is very common and "popular," though not consistently so, with a <term>Cretan</term> dialectal influence. These features, along with many repetitions and confusing passages, give it a striking—if superficial—resemblance to some of the <term>Akritic ballads</term> <note>Folk songs passed down through oral tradition about the frontier warrior Digenes.</note>. 

It has become a common opinion, first proposed by <term>N. G. Polites</term>, that these <term>Ballads</term> are more "poetic" than the <term>Epic</term> poem itself because they are filled with magic and narrative confusion. Consequently, the scholar <term>Kyriakides</term> and others claim that a "breath of life" flows through the messy structure of the <term>Escorial (ESC)</term> version because it feels so much like the <term>Ballads</term>. Some have even argued that, if we assume the <term>Epic</term> was originally put together from scattered <term>Ballads</term> or <term>Lays</term> <note>Short narrative poems intended to be sung.</note>, the fact that the <term>Escorial (ESC)</term> version most resembles the surviving ballads proves it is closer to the original source and therefore older than all other versions. A similar argument was once made in October 1941 by <term>Grégoire</term> to support the <term>Russian</term> version of the story. However, this argument ignores the possibility that these qualities...

# VERSIONS
### xxiii

In numerous unimportant details, he seems to follow the TRF manuscript <note>TRF refers to the Trapezuntine version of the Digenes Akrites epic.</note> rather than the AND <note>The Andros version.</note> and in a few details to have been following a version which differed from both. (See, for example, the journal *Folklore* <note>original: "Laografia"</note>, volume ix, page 350, compared with TRE 1128, 1133—lines which are different in the AND version; page 358, where the name of the Saracen Soudales is omitted but details are inserted which are not in the AND or TRE versions (where there is a gap in the text), or the GRO version <note>The Grottaferrata version, often considered the most "literary."</note> (where the Saracen is omitted altogether), but which are in the ESC version <note>The Escorial version.</note> 930, 931; page 359, where the detail of the girl’s father and her two brothers pursuing does not agree with TRE 1267 or AND 2075 or the OXF version 1957 (where the three pursuers are her three brothers), or with ESC 969 (five brothers) but with GRO iv. 610, iv. 657; PAS page 361, the twelve eunuchs, who are to be found in GRO iv. 925 but in no other version; page 366, a detail which is in no other version about the confusion in the house of Haplorrabdes when his wife fell ill; page 406, the doctors feel his pulse; and a few other details and misunderstandings.)

The writer gives his name as **MELETIOS VLASTOS** and the date 1632. The editor, on insufficient linguistic grounds, says that he was a native of Chios. A Cretan monk of this name is said to have been one of the teachers of Cyril Loukares <note>Loukares (1572–1638) was the Patriarch of Constantinople known for his leanings toward Reformed theology.</note>. Why did Meletios want to turn the story of Digenes, of which he had in his hands a perfectly good version, into the...

...meter, which has become almost universal in Greek popular poetry. (See my note in F. H. Marshall’s *Three Cretan Plays* (Oxford, 1929), page 2, and add references to Wagner, *Mediaeval Greek Texts* (1870), pages ii–viii; Schmitt, *The Chronicle of Morea* (1904), pages xxxiii–xxxvi; W. P. Ker, *The Dark Ages* (1904, new edition 1955), pages 343, 344; Heisenberg, *Dialects and Colloquial Language in Modern Greek* <note>original: "Dialekte und Umgangssprache im Neugriechischen"</note> (Munich, 1918), pages 44–55; and Ducange, under the entry <term>political verse</term> <note>original: "politikoi stichoi"; a 15-syllable iambic meter common in Byzantine and Modern Greek folk songs.</note>.) This meter, although it is now of course accentual <note>Meaning the rhythm is based on word stress rather than vowel length.</note>, seems to be the same as the ancient (quantitative) iambic tetrameter catalectic which is to be found fairly often in the tragedians as well as in Aristophanes. See, for example, Aristophanes, *Plutus* 288, *The Clouds* <note>original: "Nubes"</note> 1433, which are both quantitative and accentual; *The Clouds* 1399, which is quantitative but cannot be read accentually. Aeschylus, *The Persians* <note>original: "Persae"</note> 155, and Sophocles *Oedipus the King* <note>original: "O.T." (Oedipus Tyrannus)</note> 1524, are examples of quantitative trochaic tetrameters catalectic which can be read accentually as iambic tetrameters catalectic, and many more could no doubt be found; even Homeric hexameters can sometimes be read as accentual <term>fifteeners</term> <note>Another name for the 15-syllable "political verse."</note>, though usually without a <term>caesura</term> <note>A natural pause or break in a line of poetry.</note>: e.g. *Odyssey* ix. 106, *Iliad* vii. 59. The beginnings of the accentual fifteener are obscure, but Professor D. S. Robertson has shown that it can be discovered in Procopius’ *Secret History* <note>original: "Anecdota"</note> xv. 34 by substituting for the discreet <term>so-and-so</term> <note>original: "ho deina"</note> the vocative of a proper name such as *Theodore*. This would take it back to, say, A.D. 550. In English it is less common than in Greek, and is seldom heard except in ballads like ‘In Scarlet town where I was born there was a fair maid dwellin’’, or better still in the song of the London Apprentices in *The Knight of the Burning Pestle*:

> The rumbling rivers now do warm for little boys to paddle:
> The sturdy steed now goes to grass and up they hang his saddle.

<summary>This page analyzes the 1632 Meletios Vlastos version of the Digenes Akrites epic, comparing its narrative details to other manuscripts, and traces the origins of the Greek "political verse" (fifteener) meter back to ancient quantitative verse and early Byzantine prose.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the literary development of the Digenes Akrites epic, transitioning from comparisons with other national epics to a detailed analysis of the Greek 'Akritic' folk ballads.</meta>

->xxvii<-
### VERSIONS

...a method of composition once supposed to have produced the *Iliad* and the *Odyssey*, and the French *Song of Roland* (just as Lönnrott in fact produced the Finnish *Kalevala* in modern times (1835-49) from traditional lays collected by Topelius in 1822).

Here, then, we say as we approach the Greek songs of the Akritic Cycle <note>The Akritic Cycle refers to a body of Byzantine folk songs centered on the "Akritai," the frontiersmen or border guards who protected the edges of the Empire.</note>, here is a wonderful chance to study in their raw state the materials of an epic poem. In these ballads we might expect to find, distorted perhaps by oral tradition but still easily recognizable, many of the episodes of *Digenes*. We are surprised to discover an entirely different world: a world of supernatural feats, magic weapons, and talking birds; in which Digenes is only one of a number of heroes we have never heard of before—Andronikos, Porfyrios, Armouropoulos, Konstantas, Theophylaktos, Xantinos—and is not by any means the most popular. *Digenes* indeed hardly appears at all except in the series of ballads describing his death,¹ foretold by talking birds, his iron death-bed, and his wrestling with Death <note>original: "Charon," who in Greek folklore is the personification of Death rather than just a ferryman</note> who comes to fetch him. This connected series and one or two which seem to refer to his carrying off, in entirely different circumstances, of Evdokia, are the only ones that can be fitted into the story of our Digenes at all; and a great number are commonly called ‘Akritic’ only because of their obvious antiquity. Some of the other heroes have been plausibly identified by Grégoire as historical figures, emperors or generals or pretenders; or as the eponymous heroes of imperial <term>themes</term> <note>Themes (themata) were the administrative and military districts of the middle Byzantine Empire.</note> or regiments. But he has had less success in rationalizing the incidents.

A son of Andronikos born in captivity, his mother having been carried off before his birth, escapes from the Saracens <note>A term used in the Middle Ages for Arabs or Muslims.</note> and goes in search of his father and brothers. (In this episode it is worth noting that the hero is <term>twice-born</term> <note>original: "digenes" — meaning "of two races" or "of two origins"</note>, in another sense, as being the son of a Christian father Andronikos as well as the putative son of the Emir at whose court he is born and brought up (see Passow 482, Kyriakides, *Digenes Akrites*, pp. 35 ff., and Legrand, *Popular Greek Songs* <note>original: "Chansons pop. grecques"</note> (1874), no. 87, especially lines 7, 8).) A gargantuan Porfyres falls in love with the king’s daughter and no chains are strong enough to hold him. The equally monstrous Xantinos liberates his son who has—

***

¹ *Folklore* <note>original title: "Laografia"</note>, i (1910), pp. 169-274. Polites has here edited 72 songs including 14 which are only slightly, and in some versions, contaminated with Akritic matter; but not including, he says (p. 171), innumerable wrestling matches of an unnamed man with Death <note>original: "Charon"</note>.

<summary>This page explores the differences between the formal Digenes Akrites epic and the "Akritic Cycle" of folk ballads, noting that the ballads present a more mythical world filled with various heroes and supernatural elements.</summary>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the textual history and versions of the Digenes Akrites epic, specifically focusing on the debated provenance of certain manuscripts and the relationship between folk ballads and the formal epic structure.</meta>

### VERSIONS

The text was first published by Zampelios in 1859. He claimed the manuscript was given to him by Brunet de Presle, who had received it from the unpublished collection of the historian Claude Fauriel. Fauriel, in turn, was said to have copied it from a manuscript held in the National Library of France. However, this account is flatly denied by the scholar Legrand. Legrand asserts that Brunet de Presle, while a student, actually transcribed the text from the oral dictation of Professor Hase (see Zampelios, *Whence the Common Word "I Sing"?* <note>original: "Pothen hê Koinê Lexis Tragoudô?"</note> (1859), p. 37; Legrand, *Popular Greek Songs* <note>original: "Chansons pop. grecques"</note>, p. 183). Büdinger, who reprinted the version provided by Zampelios, mentions his own failed attempts to locate the alleged original manuscript in Paris (*A Medieval Greek Folk Epic* <note>original: "Ein mittelgriechisches Volksepos"</note> (Leipzig, 1866), p. 3). Further discussion can be found in Wagner, who also reprinted the text (*Medieval Greek Texts* (1870), pp. x, xiii n. 34, xxii). Legrand suggests that his own version was derived from the same Brunet de Presle manuscript that Zampelios had used and subsequently "improved" or edited.

None of the Akritic <note>The "Akritic" songs are a cycle of folk ballads celebrating the lives of the "Akritai," the frontier guards of the Byzantine Empire.</note> ballads or fragments can be classified as "Folk-chronicles" or "Historical Ballads." That specific category is better represented by the Cretan historical songs of the 18th century, such as the famous *Daskalogiannes* <note>A celebrated ballad recounting the rebellion and sacrifice of "Teacher John," a Cretan leader against Ottoman rule.</note>. One might expect the creator of the Digenes epic to have drawn material from historical ballads rather than romantic ones, yet this is not the case. The curious absence of any clearly recognizable plot points from the epic within the surviving Akritic ballads is somewhat mirrored in English folk literature. *The Gest of Robin Hood* is a short epic of 1,824 lines divided into eight sections (or "fyttes"). It was first published in the mid-16th century and is believed to have been composed by a poet of "thoroughly congenial spirit" using ballads that had been circulating for a century prior. Interestingly, not a single one of the original ballads used to construct that epic has survived in its individual form, and some parts of the narrative may have been invented by the compiler themselves (*Child’s Popular Ballads*, edited by Sargent and Kittredge, p. 225).

---

¹ Zampelios notes he had special permission from Fauriel’s widow to copy the document; see *Whence the Common Word "I Sing"?* <note>original: "Pothen hê Koinê Lexis Tragoudô?"</note> (p. 37, footnote).

² It is worth noting that neither the ballads nor the epic typically portray Digenes <note>The hero Digenes Akrites, whose name means "Twice-born Borderer."</note> as a slayer of dragons. However, the scholar Henri Grégoire identifies Digenes on several pottery fragments, believed to date to the 13th century, which were discovered during excavations of the ancient Athenian marketplace (the Agora). See the journals *Byzantion*, vol. xv (1940-1); *Hesperia*, vol. x (Jan.-March 1941); and the book *Digenes Akrites* (New York, 1942), pp. 3-5. I am deeply grateful to Professor J. M. Hussey and Dr. Alison Frantz for sharing their knowledge of these fragments. The figures on the pottery appear to be typical representations of Saint George and may be compared to the fresco of Saint George and the Dragon in Stratford-on-Avon, which serves as the frontispiece for E. INTRODUCTION
xxx

### 3. STORY

Once upon a time (so begins the first book, which is found only in the Andros <note>AND refers to the Andros manuscript version of the text.</note> and Pashley <note>PAS refers to the Pashley manuscript version of the text.</note> manuscripts) there lived in Cappadocia <note>A region in central Turkey, then part of the Byzantine frontier.</note> in the Roman Empire (Romania <note>The term "Romania" was used by the Byzantines to refer to the lands of the Eastern Roman Empire.</note>) a certain prince called Andronikos, or Aaron, of the Doukas family. He and his wife Anna (of the Magastrean or Kinnamos family) had five sons, but they wanted a daughter. (The "wanted daughter" is a very rare theme in oriental folk-lore.) So they prayed and in due time a little girl was born to them, a lovely child called Eirene <note>The name Eirene means "Peace" in Greek.</note>. The soothsayers foretold that she should be carried off by an (Arabian) Emir <note>A title for a Muslim military commander or local ruler.</note>—who would, however, become a Christian—so when she was seven years old she was put in a palace in a garden attended by a company of guards and nursemaids, so that she should never think of love. When she was twelve years old and more beautiful than the moon she saw in the palace a picture of Love <note>The Greek god Eros, often depicted as Cupid.</note> painted in the likeness of a little boy shooting with a bow, and one of her maids told her that this was the strong and terrible one, armed with arrows and fire, the conqueror, the slave-driver, bearing inkhorn and paper to enroll his victims.¹ She only laughed and said she was not afraid of his arrows or his lions or his fire or his ink and paper. But that night Love himself appeared to her in a dream and she was terrified and begged him to have mercy on her and had to run to her maids to be comforted. From this point (Andros manuscript, line 261) the writer breaks into rhyming octosyllables <note>A poetic meter consisting of eight syllables per line.</note> in attempting to describe her loveliness and her nobility. The last thirty lines of the book can hardly be earlier than the fifteenth century and must have been added by one of the later transcribers. The Pashley manuscript says only that "her heart was on fire."

Then one day when her father was away on an expedition she went out with her maids and her nurses into the country for a picnic. Now at this time there was a great Emir called Mousour who had been made prince of Syria: and one day when he came raiding into Romania <note>The Byzantine Empire.</note> he happened to come to the place where the picnic was going on and he carried off the girl and her young companions.

When her five brothers heard of it and told their mother she charged them on pain of a mother’s curse² to go after the Emir and bring back their sister or not to return alive. They arm and go in pursuit, and Constantine, who is either her eldest brother (Andros manuscript...

---

¹ Compare the painting of Love in Eustathius’s *The Story of Hysmine and Hysminias*, book 2, chapter 7. <note>Eustathios Makrembolites was a 12th-century Byzantine novelist.</note>
² The Escorial manuscript <note>ESC refers to the version found in the Escorial Library in Spain.</note> opens at this point: Escorial line 2 corresponds to Andros line 324.

320; PAS <note>The Pashley manuscript version.</note> p. 320; TREB <note>The Trebizond manuscript version.</note> 303; OXF <note>The Oxford manuscript version.</note> 741) or her twin (GRO <note>The Grottaferrata manuscript version.</note> i. 132; SPE <note>The Spetses manuscript version.</note> p. 307), fights the emir while the others look on.

The Emir is outfought, surrenders, gives them his ring, and allows them to search the camp for their sister. They cannot find her, and a helpful Arab suggests that she may have been killed with a lot of other girls whose bodies have been thrown into the ditch. There is a search among the mutilated bodies of her companions. (TRE <note>The Trebizond manuscript version.</note> begins at this point.) Finally they return to the Emir and threaten him. He asks them who they are, and when they ask him about his own family he replies proudly, ‘I am the son of Chrysoherpes and Spathia (or Panthia, GRO i. 284). My father is dead, so I was brought up by my Arabian uncles as a Mohammedan <note>A historical term for a Muslim.</note>. My grandfather was Ambron and my uncle Karoes. I have conquered all Syria and beyond and have never been defeated, but now I surrender to your sister’s beauty. She is alive and unharmed, and if I may marry her, I am willing to become a Christian and come over with all my people into Rômania <note>The Byzantine Empire.</note>.’

(These genealogies must be noticed.<sup>1</sup> The study of the historical vestiges in the poem really begins with the identification of Chrysoherpes, father of the Emir Mousour, with Chrysocheir <note>Literally "Golden Hand" in Greek.</note> a leader of the Paulician <note>A Christian dualist sect often at odds with the Byzantine state.</note> (Christian) heretics, who in the ninth century rebelled against the empire, ravaged Asia Minor, and compelled Basil I the Macedonian <note>Byzantine Emperor, reigned 867–886.</note> to sue for peace, but in a later expedition in 873 was surrounded and beheaded (see below, p. xxxii). ‘In their hostile raids’, says Gibbon <note>Edward Gibbon, author of *The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire*.</note> (vol. vi, p. 126) ‘the disciples of St. Paul were allied with those of Mahomet’; and after the defeat of Chrysocheir, ‘the glory of the Paulicians faded and withered;<sup>2</sup> but the spirit of independence survived in the mountains; the Paulicians defended above a century their religion and liberty, infested the Roman limits, and maintained their perpetual alliance with the enemies of the empire and the gospel’. In those words Gibbon has recorded the circumstances which produced the background of the present poem. He has even defined by that phrase ‘infesting the Roman limits’ the name or rather the profession of our hero. For AKRITÊS <note>A term for the frontiersmen or border guards of the Byzantine Empire.</note> means

a BORDERER, a <term>miles limitaneus</term> <note>original: "miles limitaneus"; Latin for a soldier stationed on the border districts</note>, a frontier guard, or Warden of the Marches: but the office had been so notably illustrated by the pacifying achievements of Digenes or one of his prototypes that already in the twelfth century it had become almost a proper name and the poet Ptôhoprodromos <note>A 12th-century Byzantine poet whose name literally means "Poor Prodromos"</note> tried to flatter the emperor Manuel Komnênos by addressing him as ‘a second Akritês’ and again used the phrase much as one might say ‘a new Achilles’; and as the Grottaferrata manuscript (line 1036) refers to ‘the Emperor Basil the Blessed, the mighty Borderer’ (see Mavrofrydes, *Selection of Monuments* (1866), pp. 45, 65, for the two Prodromic verses). Neither Mavrofrydes nor Koraês (*Miscellanies*, vol. i. p. 256) knew the meaning of the word *akritês*; and Legrand when he first quoted the two verses in his introduction to the ballads which he was the first to call the Akritic Cycle (*Greek Folk Songs* (1874), p. 184) still thought it was a proper name. (See Krumbâcher, p. 832, for quotation from Constantine Porphyrogenitus, *On Ceremonies* <note>original: "De Cerimoniis"; a Latin title for a Greek work describing Byzantine court protocols</note> (Bonn edition, p. 489), describing duties of the <term>akritai</term> <note>The border guards of the Byzantine Empire</note>.)

So after making peace they all returned to Roman territory <note>The Byzantine Empire, which considered itself the continuation of Rome</note>, and there at the castle the *Stratêgissa* <note>The wife of the General or Strategos</note>, the General’s wife, welcomed her sons returning with their sister ‘as a mother that rejoiceth over her children’ (a quotation from the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> version of the Psalms: Psalm 112:9 in the Greek; King James Version, Psalm 113:9). The Emir was baptized and married the princess Eirene and in due time a child was born who was called Basil Digenes Akrites.

All versions report the baptism, the marriage, and the birth of the child very briefly, and at this point give no explanation of the child’s names. It is only later that we learn that he was called **Digenes** because he was born of two races <note>From the Greek "di-" (two) and "genos" (race/birth)</note>, a happy union of Roman and Arab, and **Akrites** because his task was to bring peace to the border between the two. (See the Trebizond manuscript line 835 onwards; Grottaferrata manuscript book iv. line 50 onwards; Andros manuscript line 1357 onwards.)

The Emir’s mother, hearing what had happened, wrote to him from Syria a letter of bitter reproaches. She reminded him of the achievements of his father Chrysocherpes <note>Historically identified as Chrysocheir, the leader of the Paulician heretics in the 9th century</note>, who had defeated many Roman generals, and when at last he was surrounded and offered an honourable and even a glorious peace, who had preferred to be cut to pieces rather than surrender. And his uncle Mousour of Tarsus, what raids he had made as far as Ankyra <note>Modern-day Ankara</note> and Smyrna <note>Modern-day Izmir</note>! (Grottaferrata manuscript book ii. line 78, where compare Procopius, *On Buildings*, book ii. chapter ix. section 10, for ‘Pentakomia near the Euphrates’). She tells him of the danger she herself is in as a

<meta>This page continues the summary of the narrative transition where the Emir’s mother in Syria attempts to recall him, fearing he has abandoned his faith and family for a Byzantine bride.</meta>

<page-num>xxxiii</page-num>

-># STORY<-

...the result of her son letting himself be seduced from glory by a pretty eater of pork <note>A derogatory term used by the Emir's Muslim mother for the Christian Eirene, referencing dietary differences</note>. (She even reminds him rather surprisingly—the reference is omitted in the later versions—of the wives of his harem and his children left behind in Syria. In the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>A 14th-century version of the epic poem</note>, book 3, line 127, these women and their children actually go to meet him on his return to Edessa.) She insists he must return at once and may bring the girl with him if he likes.

She sends the letter by swift horsemen who camp outside the Doukas family castle at a place called **Whitestone** (*Leukopetra* <note>original: "Leukopetra"; Greek for White Stone</note> in the Trebizond manuscript line 230 and Andros manuscript line 680; *Asprê Petra* <note>original: "Asprê Petra"; another Greek variant for White Stone</note> in the Oxford manuscript line 635; but *Lakkopetra* <note>original: "Lakkopetra"; likely meaning Hollow Stone</note> in the Grottaferrata manuscript book 2, line 101, and *Halkopetron* <note>original: "Halkopetron"; Bronze Stone</note> in the Escorial manuscript line 321—evidently a mistake of memory confusing it with *Halkourgia* <note>original: "Halkourgia"; Copper-works</note> mentioned in Grottaferrata book 5, line 238). When the Emir receives his mother's letter he tells Eirene, who is quite willing, though distressed, to go away with him.$^1$ There follows a rather complicated quarrel which is developed with much originality in the Escorial manuscript, line 405 and following.

However, all ended happily. Eirene declared that she had not told her brothers anything and then she ran and explained to them that the Emir only wanted to go to Syria to see his mother, because she had written to him, and would then return again; she reminded them that they themselves not long ago had been afraid of a mother's curse. So there was a general reconciliation and the Emir set off with all their good wishes for a speedy return. But he went, rather unexpectedly after what had been said, without Eirene. The incident illustrates the sensitivity of brothers about their sisters' 'honor' in the Greek world: the scholar Psichari has a pungent note on the selfish tyranny of Greek brothers and quotes an even more pungent proverb (Psichari, *Some Works* <note>original: "Quelques Travaux"; a collection of scholarly papers by Jean Psichari</note> (Paris, 1930), p. 45).

The Emir sings to his men on the journey to cheer them on, for he makes them travel fast, covering three stages in one day in his longing to return to his love. He reminds them how they had been in a desperate battle together and how—at **Mellokopia** (Grottaferrata manuscript book 3, line 67; *Mylokopodi* in the Andros version line 956; the passage is missing in the Trebizond version; this is the modern Malakopi in Cappadocia <note>A region in central Turkey</note>, south of the Halys River$^2$)—he had cut a way for them through the Roman <note>The Byzantine forces, who referred to themselves as Romans</note> forces that had surrounded them.

This battle is identified by the historian Grégoire with that in which Omar of—

***

$^1$ There is a similar episode in the romance *Imperios and Margarona* <note>A popular medieval Greek chivalric tale</note>, line 455 and following. Imperios proposes to his bride that they should go off secretly to visit his father, without the knowledge of her parents.
$^2$ For the modern village see R. M. Dawkins, *Modern Greek in Asia Minor* (1916), pp. 23 and following.

<summary>This page details the narrative conflict sparked by a letter from the Emir's mother, leading to his temporary return to Syria and a discussion of cultural themes like sibling honor and historical battle locations.</summary>

<keywords>Emir, Eirene, Doukas castle, Whitestone, Syria, Edessa, Cappadocia, honor, Byzantine-Arab relations, Digenes Akrites manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s analysis of the historical and legendary elements of the poem, specifically the Emir’s journey back to his home in Edessa and his mother's attempts to keep him within the Islamic faith by citing local miracles and relics.</meta>

<page-num>xxxiv</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION

Melitene <note>A significant city on the Byzantine-Arab frontier, modern-day Malatya, Turkey</note> was defeated and killed by the Roman General Petronas, as described in the histories of Genesius and the Continuation of Theophanes <note>Two primary historical sources for 10th-century Byzantine history</note>; it took place in 863, but not at Malakopi (see Bury, *Eastern Rom. Emp.*, p. 283). After comparing the accounts of this Byzantine victory with the passages in the Grottaferrata manuscript and in the Escorial manuscript (pages 500 and following), he <note>Referring to the scholar Henri Grégoire</note> claims, not without reason, that the "historical" elements in the poem are simply borrowed from the chroniclers (*Byzantion*, volume v. pages 334 and following).

On the road through the mountains the Emir kills a lion and takes its teeth and the claws of its right foot as a present for his little boy, Basil. No river crossing is described but they soon arrive at Edessa, the capital of Osroëne in Mesopotamia (called **The Castle of Edessa** <note>original: "tou Rouhâ to Kastron"; appearing in the Trebizond and Andros manuscripts</note>; or **The Castle of Rahab** <note>original: "to Rahab to Kastron"; appearing in the Grottaferrata manuscript</note>), and there after dinner he preaches the Christian faith to his mother with such success that she and all her household are immediately converted; and after loading up apparently most of the treasure of Edessa they all return to Cappadocia to be baptized. (Edessa took its Greek name from the ancient capital of Macedonia. From the Arabic name, *Er-Ruha*, was developed the Greek adjectival name of the province of Osroëne).

In the Grottaferrata manuscript, however (book iii, lines 135-57), but not in the other versions, the Emir's mother anticipates his preaching by challenging him again about the cause of his absence. Had he ever seen, she asks, in **the land of the Romans** <note>original: "Rômania"; the term used by Byzantines to refer to their own Empire</note> any wonders to compare with the miracles which are performed at the tomb of the Prophet <note>Referring to Muhammad's tomb in Medina</note>, the light that there descends by night from on high, the bears and lions, the wolves and sheep together, one not hurting the other, that there bow down together during the prayer? "And have we not," she continues, "the Kerchief of Naaman <note>A legendary reference likely confusing the biblical Naaman with local traditions of the Mandylion</note> who was king among the Assyrians and for his virtues was found worthy of miracles? How could you overstep all these, my son, and even despise the greatest honors when all thought you would be lord of Egypt, standing in the way of your own destiny, all for the sake of this Greek girl?"

Now, says Grégoire, what is this Kerchief, or Towel, of Naaman? It must be the **Sacred Image of Edessa** <note>Known as the Mandylion, a cloth believed to bear the miraculous imprint of the face of Jesus</note>—"one of the most precious of Christian relics . . . an authentic portrait of Christ, the Towel on which He had dried his face, leaving on it the Impression which he had sent to King Abgar of Edessa" (Runciman, *Romanus Lecap.* p. 145). But this relic was surrendered by the Arabs of Edessa to the victorious Kourkouas <note>John Kourkouas, a famous Byzantine general</note> and triumphantly carried to Constantinople...

<summary>This page discusses the historical influences on the poem, specifically the Emir's conversion of his mother in Edessa and her mention of Islamic miracles and the "Kerchief of Naaman," identified by scholars as the Mandylion of Edessa.</summary>

<keywords>Melitene, Petronas, Edessa, Osroëne, Mesopotamia, Mandylion, Kerchief of Naaman, King Abgar, Henri Grégoire, conversion, Byzantine history</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s analysis of the historical and legendary elements of the poem, specifically focusing on the scholar Henri Grégoire’s theories regarding the dating of the manuscript based on the movement of holy relics from Edessa.</meta>

<page-num>xxxv</page-num>
<header>STORY</header>

...in 944. Therefore, says Grégoire <note>Henri Grégoire, a prominent 20th-century scholar of Byzantine epic poetry</note>, the poem which refers to its beneficent presence at Edessa must have been written before that year. Digenes (presumably he means the hypothetical orthodox anti-Paulician revision he has been talking about) must have been written between 930 and 944. But if we refuse to be bluffed or hypnotized we will find it difficult to suppose that a poet writing in the immediate neighbourhood, with a good knowledge of the Septuagint <note>The ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> and of local tradition, could possibly confuse—just because they were both lepers—Naaman the Syrian lord healed by Elisha with Abgar the King of Edessa to whom Christ was said to have sent firstly a Letter and secondly "the perfect impression of his Face on a Linen" (Gibbon, v. 265). This Holy Image (or *impression* <note>original: "ekmageion"</note>, as it was called) was certainly brought from Edessa to Constantinople in 944, after the victorious advance of Kourkouas <note>John Kourkouas, a brilliant Byzantine general of the 10th century</note> to the Euphrates. But much the more famous of the two treasures of Edessa, the Letter and the Image, both associated with the Christ-Abgar story, was the Letter. Only the Letter is mentioned by Saint Sylvia in her *Pilgrimage to the Holy Places* <note>original: "Peregrinatio ad Loca Sancta"</note> who visited Edessa in the fourth century. Both Letter and Image are referred to on the capture of Edessa by the Arabs in 637 (Gibbon, v. 471). The Image seems to have been a later discovery. The more famous relic of the two, the Letter, did not leave Edessa in 944, but remained there until the town was again captured by Maniakes <note>George Maniakes, the famous 11th-century Byzantine general</note> in 1031. Of course *kerchief* <note>original: "mantēli"</note> might be a mistake for *letter* <note>original: "mantato"</note>. But we should still wonder why Naaman should have been written for Abgar. If the name Naaman is retained, as I think it must be, either Letter or Towel would make sense, for both a Letter and a Towel (i.e. the towel on which Naaman dried himself after bathing in Jordan) would suit the Elisha-Naaman story (2 Kings v; Septuagint version, 4 Kings v); and Edessa was such a well-known clearing-house of religious legend (cf. Hasluck, *Christianity and Islam*, p. 37) that there may have been many relics there we have not heard of.

On the Emir’s return to Cappadocia with his mother and her household there is a scene of reunion, and when he embraces his Eirene they faint for joy in the fashion of the later romances. Then the little boy Digenes was brought in and the Emir took him in his arms and said, "My little hawk, and when will you spread your wings and go after the partridges?" (Grottaferrata manuscript, book iii. line 307.) And so the third book ends in a scene of happy family life among the Cappadocian nobility.

<summary>This page evaluates the historical dating of the Digenes Akrites poem, specifically disputing Henri Grégoire's timeline by analyzing the legends of King Abgar's relics and the biblical figure Naaman. It concludes with the Emir's sentimental reunion with his family and the introduction of his young son, the future hero Digenes.</summary>

<keywords>Edessa, King Abgar, Naaman, Elisha, Holy Mandylion, Henri Grégoire, relics, John Kourkouas, George Maniakes, Cappadocia, Digenes Akrites, Grottaferrata manuscript</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s narrative summary of the epic poem, moving from the historical dating of the text to the specific plot details and character origins found in the primary manuscript versions.</meta>

<page-num>xxxvi</page-num>
<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

The fourth book in the three oldest and most complete versions <note>These refer to the major surviving manuscripts of the poem: GRO (Grottaferrata), TRE (Trebizond), and AND (Andros)</note> opens with a summary of the life and exploits of the Emir <note>The hero's father, a high-ranking Muslim leader who converted to Christianity for love</note> which would be a suitable introduction to the Tale of Digenes if it were only just beginning. In the course of this introduction we are told explicitly for the first time why Basil was called Digenes and why he was called Akrites. "To these a child was born and from his birth he was named Basil; and he is also called Digenes <note>original: "Digenes"; meaning "of two races" or "twice-born," referring to his mixed parentage</note> as from his parents, being a <term>gentile</term> <note>original: "ethnikos"; meaning a non-Christian or foreigner, in this case, Arab</note> by his father and by his mother a Roman <note>In this period, "Roman" (Rhomaion) referred to the Greek-speaking citizens of the Byzantine Empire</note>; and becoming terrible, as the tale shall show, he is named Akrites the <term>Borderer</term> <note>original: "Akrites"; from "akra," meaning the edges or borders of the empire</note> as having subdued the Borders." (GRO iv. 50–53; TRE 825–34, a slightly different version; AND 1356–64 again slightly different.)

When at last the hero does begin to grow up he grows up very quickly. For three years he learned letters. Then he learned arms, the uses of spear and sword, the arts of running and wrestling. When he was twelve years old he asked if he might go with his father and his uncle to the chase. The Emir said he was too young but he insisted; and next morning he rode off into the forest with his father and his uncle Constantine. He kills two bears, and catches a running deer on foot and kills it with his hands. When a lion springs at him he draws his sword and kills it with one stroke. Afterwards they go to a spring in the forest and wash him and give him clean clothes of silk and gold; and a curious piece of folklore has survived in the account in the Grottaferrata manuscript (GRO iv, 217, 218), though not in the Trebizond (TRE 990 ff.) or Andros (AND 1530 ff.) versions. The boy was anxious to ride home to his mother: so they mounted him on a white horse, its mane plaited with turquoises and little bells of gold, with a saddlecloth of rose and green silk, and a bridle embroidered with gold and pearls. The horse was spirited but he let it play to his own desire and sat in the saddle "like a full-blown rose" (TRE 1019, AND 1557), or "like an apple on a tree" (GRO iv. 245). So he grew up strong and beautiful, and as the Emir his father grew old he left all adventuring to his son.

One day when he became a man Digenes rode off with his company and in one of the passes succeeded in finding a band of the <term>Brigands</term> <note>original: "Apelatai"; literally "cattle-raiders," these were lawless frontiersmen often at odds with the authorities</note> whom he had been longing to meet.

This is the first appearance on the scene of a class of freebooters or highwaymen who with their enemies the <term>Border Guards</term> <note>original: "Akritai"</note> or Wardens play a great part in the action. In fact all the action of the poem is a conflict between the Border Guards and the Brigands. The name by which they are called,

<summary>This page explains the origins of the hero's names, Basil "Digenes" (of two races) and "Akrites" (borderer), and describes his miraculous childhood strength and his first encounter with the outlaw Brigands.</summary>
<keywords>Digenes Akrites, Basil, Emir, border guards, brigands, Apelatai, Byzantine epic, Grottaferrata manuscript, Trebizond manuscript</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction’s exploration of the terminology and narrative structure of the epic, moving from the naming of the hero to the specific groups he encounters and the variations between manuscript versions.</meta>

<page-num>xxxvii</page-num>
<header>STORY</header>

**reivers** <note>original: *apelatai*; a term for border bandits or cattle-rustlers</note>, has given occasion for many notes, although it is fairly common in Byzantine literature (e.g. *Belthandros and Chrysantza* 104; see Krumbacher, p. 832; Lampros, *Greek Romances*, glossary; and Sophokles, under the relevant entries) and has even given its name **reiver-mace** <note>original: *apelatiki*</note> to the mace or club which seems to have been their characteristic weapon (see Ducange and Meursius, under the relevant entries). It is the exact equivalent of the Latin, and obsolete English, **cattle-thief** <note>original: *abactor*</note>, and means simply drivers-away, reivers, rustlers, cattle-drivers, or horse-thieves. St. Theodore, in whose name Digenes <note>The hero of the epic</note> vows to his lady in the Escorial manuscript 891 and the Andros manuscript 1921, is there called ‘the great Reiver.’¹ But nearly all commentators before Krumbacher, including Lampros, Legrand, Gidel, and Rambaud, took the word *reiver* in a passive sense and explained it as meaning one who had been driven away, that is an ‘outlaw’; and Ioannides (in his Constantinople edition of the Trebizond manuscript, p. 49) suggests that it means one who is not any man’s dependant <note>original: *a privative + pelatês*; using the "a-" prefix to mean "not" a dependant</note>.

Digenes finds their water-carrier and by him is guided to their chief, the old brigand Philopappos, who is lying in his tent on a heap of wild beasts’ skins, and tells him that he wants to join the band. The old man answered and said (Andros manuscript 1609–20): ‘Young man, if you think yourself good enough to be a Reiver take this club and go on guard: and if you can fast for fifteen days, and let no sleep close your eyes, and then go off and kill lions and bring me their skins; and after that go and watch again, and when the princes go by with a caravan and the bride and bridegroom with them, if you can take the bride from the midst of them all and bring her here to me, then indeed you may call yourself a Reiver.’ Digenes answers that he had done all that when he was a little boy: now he can overtake a hare running uphill, or catch a low-flying partridge in his hands. The Reivers invite him to dinner, and when they are boasting he challenges them, all of them, to a bout with quarter staves, disables the whole company and presents their weapons to old Philopappos saying, ‘If you don’t like it I will do the same to you.’ And so he rejoined his young men and they rode home.

This disconnected episode, well developed in the Escorial manuscript (lines 622–701), is omitted by the Grottaferrata version which passes directly from the Hunting

# INTRODUCTION

xxxviii

to the Courtship. The omission is not an unmixed advantage. For the incident not only represents a preliminary challenge from the youthful hero to the forces of lawlessness and the standards of violence (it is perhaps not by accident that Digenes <note>The "Twice-Born" hero, son of an Arab Emir and a Greek noblewoman</note> in his reply to Philopappos suggests that the skill of the athlete is better than the violence of the brigand); it also serves a purpose in the narrative of representing the years of adolescent adventure which must have elapsed between the boy’s first hunting and the young man’s lovemaking. In fact, there is little doubt that there is a gap <note>original: lacuna; a missing section in a manuscript</note> in the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>referred to as GRO</note> after line iv. 253; a gap easily explained by the fact that the Hunting ends as the Courtship begins with a Homecoming from the Chase, and easily filled by the Trebizond version <note>TRE</note> lines 1027-1109 or the Andros version <note>AND</note> lines 1566-1674.

Now there was a famous general in that part of the Byzantine Empire <note>original: Rômania; the medieval Greek term for the Roman/Byzantine lands</note> called Doukas, and he had a lovely daughter called Evdokia. (Here there seems to be a curious repetition <note>original: doublet</note> of the Emir’s courtship of Eirene, also the daughter of a Doukas, a repetition which the poet has tried to explain by making Evdokia remark (GRO iv. 325; AND 1740; TRE 1179) that Digenes, through the Doukas family, must be a relation.)

One day when he was hunting in that country, Digenes sang under her window, and she looked out and saw him, and sent her Nurse with a ring and a message of warning; her father the General had a cruel way with suitors, however noble and brave they were. He rode home, praying for the sun to set and the moon to rise, and told his groom to have his black horse ready at nightfall with double girths and double martingales and sword and mace at the saddle; and at dinner he could not eat, so that his mother was anxious, and asked him what was the matter,¹ even quoting Homer to him (*Iliad* i. 363 <note>“Speak out, hide it not in thy mind, that we both may know it”</note>, in the Trebizond and Andros versions but not in the Grottaferrata), and began to pray. But he only went up to his room and put on his riding-boots and tuned his lute as he liked it, and as he rode through the night he sang a song.

It is a real folk-song with rather magical words about loving far and roving late which do not make much sense (Escorial manuscript <note>ESC</note> 839; AND 1844; GRO iv. 401; TRE 1249 has a gap as it often does where a song is indicated, and also omits the whole following scene at the window corresponding to AND 1839-2057). The words are slightly and suspiciously less obscure in the Grottaferrata manuscript than in the Andros version.

¹ There seems to be an echo of this in an Epeirotic folk-tale in Jean Pio’s *Popular Greek Tales* <note>original: Contes pop. grecques</note> (Copenhagen, 1879), no. 4, p. 14.

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s narrative summary of the epic, moving from Digenes’ initial interest in Evdokia to their elopement, the battle with her father’s retainers, and the eventual reconciliation through marriage.</meta>

->### STORY<-

xxxix

When he arrived at the castle it was nearly daybreak and Evdokia had fallen asleep waiting for him, and when he played under her window to wake her up she is rather cross at first and frightened—“if they do see thee they will murder thee.” But they talk at the window and exchange many vows—“if the intent of your love is honorable, and your purpose marriage” <note>The author here quotes Shakespeare’s *Romeo and Juliet* to illustrate the parallels in the lovers' dialogue</note>—and “all my fortunes at your feet I will lay and follow you, dear lord, throughout the world”—and then at last he stands up in the saddle and catches her as she jumps down from the window.

He would not let it be thought that he had stolen her secretly, but shouted and awakened the whole castle. One of the leading pursuers whom Digenes <note>The "Twice-Born" hero</note> crushes with a single blow is named as Soudales the Saracen <note>References to the Andros (AND) and Escorial (ESC) manuscript versions</note>.

It seems strange that a Saracen <note>A medieval term for Arabs or Muslims</note> should have been fighting among the retainers of Doukas; and this appears to be the only occasion in the poem on which Digenes kills an opponent who is specified as an Arab. But Soudales is not an Arab name. It is actually the name of a Byzantine general sent with Andronikos Doukas into Asia Minor by the Empress Theodora in 855 against the Paulicians <note>A medieval Christian sect deemed heretical by the Byzantine Church</note>, whom he persecuted with a ferocity which provoked their subsequent rebellions under Karbeas and Chrysocheir <note>Historical leaders of the Paulician state</note> (see the introduction to the Trebizond manuscript <note>TRE</note> p. lxxx, and Kyriakides p. 85 and below). So the death of Soudales here may be a trace and apparently the only trace of Paulician prejudice in the story.

So Digenes easily defeated all the pursuers: he was careful not to hurt her three brothers <note>Mentioned in the Trebizond (1275) and Andros (2075) versions; however, the Grottaferrata (GRO) and Pashley (PAS) versions mention only two brothers</note> but only knocked them off their horses. He shook hands with the General and congratulated him on having found such a fine son-in-law. But he insisted on taking the Girl—she is usually called simply <term>the Girl</term> <note>original: *Kore*</note> and, in the Escorial manuscript, <term>the Maiden</term> <note>original: *Korasion*</note>—back to his own father’s house for the wedding. The celebrations lasted three months, and there is a long list of remarkable wedding presents from the bride’s father, which included hawks, leopards, embroidered tents, maid-servants, two icons of the two saints named Theodore, the sword of Khosrow <note>original: *Chosroes*; likely referring to a legendary Persian King</note>, a tame lion, and (only in the Pashley and Grottaferrata versions) twelve eunuchs.

Thereafter Digenes rode out with Evdokia in the borders and he destroyed many reivers <note>Border raiders or bandits</note> and freebooters and made peace on all the

<meta>This page continues the narrative summary of the epic, detailing Digenes’ solitary life in the wilderness with his bride and his diplomatic meeting with the Byzantine Emperor, which establishes his legal authority over the frontiers.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

xl

Roman limits. He loved to ride about alone in the wilderness. He had one tent for himself and the Girl <note>Evdokia, the daughter of the Strategos whom he eloped with</note>, and for his men and the maid-servants he had two separate tents at a distance. These details about his servants are in all primary versions (see AND 2312 ff., TRE 1448 ff., GRO iv. 960 ff. repeated AND 3737, TRE 2555, GRO vi. 725); but the story of how Digenes blinded one of his cooks is only in AND (2324) and TRE (1460). This is a reflection of Alexander’s <note>Alexander the Great, whose legendary "Romance" heavily influenced medieval Greek literature</note> quarrel with his cook Andreas who found, and lost, the Water of Life. The birth in captivity of Digenes (not in the epic but in some of the Akritic ballads) may also be a reflection from the *Shah Nameh* <note>The "Book of Kings," a Persian epic by Ferdowsi</note> version of the birth of Alexander.

And the Emperor of the Romans hearing his fame and being then in Cappadocia¹ wrote that he was anxious to see him. Digenes answered that if the Emperor wanted to see his worthless servant he should ride down to the Euphrates with only a few men—‘I am afraid that if you were to come with a large army your men might find fault with me, and I am so young and foolish I might pay them with my fists’ (TRE 1501-4). So the Emperor came to the river with only a hundred men and greeted Digenes affectionately and asked him to name his reward. Digenes replied with a short lecture on the duties of empire—*to spare the lowly and to subdue the proud* <note>original Latin: *parcere subjectis et debellare superbos*</note>—among which GRO (iv. 1037), but not AND or TRE, includes the task of ‘scattering the heretics and fortifying the orthodox’—which perhaps might be taken as a reference to the Paulicians <note>A dualist Christian sect that lived on the Byzantine-Arab frontier and was often at odds with the central government</note>.

The Emperor, wishing he had four such men in the empire (GRO iv. 1025), restores to him all the possessions which had been confiscated from his grandfather (an obvious reference to the mysterious disgrace and exile of Eirene’s father Doukas, to which there have been some allusions: e.g. GRO iv. 55; and which explained his absence from home when Eirene was carried off by the Emir (GRO i. 63) where the word <term>exile</term> <note>original Greek: *exoria*</note> seems to have been misunderstood by the redactor of AND 290, as equivalent to <term>expedition</term> <note>original Greek: *taxeidi*</note>); and confirmed with a Golden Bull <note>A formal imperial decree authenticated with a gold seal</note> his authority over the Border.

The GRO version here introduces a new incident not found in

---

¹ According to TRE 1476 ff. the Emperor was campaigning in Cappadocia <note>A region in central Anatolia</note> and Digenes was at the frontiers on the Euphrates. According to GRO iv. 971 ff. the Emperor was campaigning against the ‘Persians’ when Digenes invited him to come to the Euphrates.
