--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the epic of Digenes Akrites, specifically focusing on the hero's legendary meeting with the Emperor and the conflicting manuscript evidence regarding which Emperor is being described.</meta>

# STORY

...any other version. Digenes entertains the Emperor by an exhibition of strength: he catches and, on foot, turns and throws with his hands at the Emperor’s feet, a wild unbroken horse; and a second item on the programme is provided by a lion which at that moment jumps unrehearsed out of the undergrowth. Digenes seizes it by one leg, dashes it on the ground, and presents it to the delighted Emperor. From that time, concludes the incident (GRO iv. 1087), ‘they called the lad Basil Akrites the Borderer because of the Golden Bull <note>A "chrysobull," or formal imperial decree authenticated with a golden seal.</note> which appointed him to rule over the Borders’.

Whether or not this implies that he was given the name Basil after the Emperor Basil (who is himself named at the beginning of this book (iv. 56) as the Emperor who had banished the grandfather of Digenes), it certainly states that his name Akrites was taken from the appointment officially conferred by the Emperor on this occasion, which is in direct contradiction to the statement at the beginning of the book (iv. 49–53); and this seems to make it more probable than it already appears that this whole passage of the demonstration of strength before the Emperor is a later interpolation <note>An entry or passage added into a text later, not part of the original composition.</note> (whether or not we believe in the general theory of a Basilian recension <term>Basilian recension<note>A hypothesized version of the poem revised during the reign of Emperor Basil II to serve imperial interests.</note></term>), and that the interpolator, as Grégoire points out (*Byzantion*, vi. 491), appears to have copied from the chronicle of Theophanes Continuatus the description of Basil the Macedonian exhibiting his prowess in order to gain the favour of the Emperor Michael III.

In this remarkable incident the name of the Emperor concerned is given in four versions (Andros, Trebizond, Oxford, Paschalis) as Romanos—presumably Romanos Lekapenos (919–44). But in one version, and that perhaps the best and oldest (Grottaferrata), as well as in the Russian version (Speransky), the Emperor’s name is given as Basil. The Grottaferrata version also has Basil in the passage of recapitulatory introduction at the beginning of Book IV which refers to the banishment of Doukas the grandfather of Digenes (GRO iv. 55). Here, where both the Trebizond (836) and Andros (1369) manuscripts speak of *Romanos the all-fortunate* <note>original: Rômanou tou paneutyhous</note>, the Grottaferrata manuscript has *Basil the fortunate, the great border-guard* <note>original: Basileiou tou eutyhous akritou tou megalou</note>. This Emperor, who is here also called the *Chief Warden of the Frontiers*, must be, says Grégoire, Basil I, the Macedonian (867–86), himself an athlete of prodigious strength, who might well have been chosen by a court poet to banish his hero’s grandfather as well as to honour his hero. Two of the three Russian manuscripts go on to tell how Digenes, or as he there appears...

<summary>This page analyzes the hero's feats of strength before the Emperor and the scholarly debate over whether the monarch was Romanos or Basil. It highlights how the title "Akrites" was officially granted by imperial decree (Golden Bull) according to specific manuscript versions.</summary>

<keywords>Digenes Akrites, Emperor Basil, Romanos Lekapenos, Golden Bull, Basilian recension, Byzantine manuscripts, border-guard, imperial decree</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the Digenes Akrites epic, specifically comparing the Russian version (where the hero is known as Deugeny) with the Greek versions. It examines Henri Grégoire’s theory that the poem originated as a revolutionary anti-imperial manifesto before being sanitized into a loyalist text.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>xlii</page-num>

Deugeny <note>The name for the hero Digenes in the medieval Russian versions of the epic.</note>, instead of greeting the Emperor Basil as a respectful vassal, proceeds to fight him, defeats him, and seizes his throne. Grégoire, as noted above, accepts this with delight, and argues that in this Russian version we have fragments of the earliest nucleus of the poem, which was nothing less than a revolutionary manifesto, issued by the Paulician <note>A dualist Christian sect on the Byzantine frontier, often regarded as heretical and frequently allied with Muslim forces against the Emperor.</note> heretics of the border, and their Arab allies, against their arch enemy the Emperor Basil the Macedonian. He believes that after the defeat of the Paulicians the imperial government produced a revised and loyalist version of the poem, suppressing, naturally, the wish-fulfilment scene of the Emperor’s defeat, giving Digenes the name of Basil in honour of the victorious Emperor—he is never called Basil in the Russian version—and representing him as a loyal vassal devoted to peace and conciliation. We are to suppose that all extant Greek versions are descended from this loyalist revision; and that the versions in which Romanos is named, that is, all versions except GRO <note>The Grottaferrata manuscript, considered one of the most important Greek versions of the poem.</note>, including the three versions which in the ninth book, in a death-bed recapitulation of his achievements, *also* mention as having honoured him the Emperor Nikêforos (presumably Nikêforos Fôkas, 963–9) (TRE 3107; AND 4344; PAS, p. 405) <note>These abbreviations—TRE, AND, and PAS—refer to the Trebizond, Andros, and Paschalis manuscripts of the poem.</note>, are all later rehandlings intended to bring up to date the first loyalist edition which has reached us as the Basilian version of GRO.

Without going into all the details of Grégoire’s conclusions it may first of all be accepted as axiomatic that the poem reflects the Arabian alliances during the ninth century of the Paulician heretics on the Armenian border. Even if we could accept Grégoire’s argument that the original poem must have been produced *after* certain battles of the ninth century to which it seems to refer, and perhaps after certain Arabian conversions of 928, but *before* 944 because the Sacred Towel <note>Also known as the Mandylion or the Image of Edessa, a famous cloth believed to bear the miraculous likeness of Jesus’s face.</note> seems to be still at Edessa, and this famous Christian relic, the ‘Image of Edessa’, was surrendered to Kourkouas <note>John Kourkouas, a prominent Byzantine general who recaptured Edessa.</note> and transferred to Constantinople in that year; even if we accept all this, and accept, too, the supposition, which is extremely probable for other reasons, of which those of language and style may be thought conclusive, that GRO is the earliest of the Greek versions; accepting all this it is still not possible to be convinced by Grégoire’s arguments that the Russian version (as it exists in the two manuscripts published by Speransky in 1922) represented an archetypal form of the poem preceding any of the existing Greek versions.

(1) It is conceivable that the fact that the Emperor is named

The author critiques Henri Grégoire's claim that the Russian version represents the oldest, "un-sanitized" form of the story.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the identity of the Emperor in the Digenes Akrites epic. It specifically explores how potential linguistic confusion between Greek titles and proper names might have led to the different historical figures appearing in the Greek and Russian versions of the poem.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>xlii</page-num>

Basil <note>Referring to the hero, whose given name is Basil, though he is better known by his title Digenes Akrites.</note> in the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>GRO; one of the oldest and most complete Greek versions of the poem, found in a monastery near Rome.</note> (book 4, line 973) and in no other Greek version may be due to a simple error or misunderstanding. (The Grottaferrata manuscript does not mention any emperor at all where other versions mention Nikephoros <note>original: Nikeforos, likely referring to Emperor Nikephoros II Phokas (reigned 963–969).</note>, for it omits the death-bed summary altogether and treats the conclusion with refreshing brevity.) It may be noticed that both the Trebizond manuscript <note>TRE (1483)</note> and the Andros manuscript <note>AND (2349)</note>, in the middle of the passage describing the arrival of the Emperor Romanos in Cappadocia <note>A region in central Asia Minor (modern Turkey) that served as a vital Byzantine military frontier.</note>, have a line saying that the "wonderful Basil" was on the border; this line refers of course to Digenes, but from its position a careless copyist might easily suppose that it referred to the Emperor, and that therefore the Emperor was not really Romanos but Basil. Although this ambiguous line does not occur in the Grottaferrata manuscript, which in fact is careful to inform us that Digenes Akrites was only called Basil after the Emperor Basil's visitation, yet the possibility of an error of this sort should warn us against assigning any overwhelming significance to the variation of the Emperor's name in one version out of five. (No Emperor is mentioned in the Escorial manuscript <note>ESC</note>, perhaps another sign of the lateness of this version.)

(2) It is also possible that in the Russian version the name Basil is the result of a misunderstanding. In the Greek versions the Emperor is always called by his usual title of Emperor <note>original: *Basileus*</note> which in the course of translation into Russian may easily have been mistaken for the proper name Basil <note>original: *Basileios*</note>: and the proper name Romanos <note>original: *Rômanos*</note> may even have been mistaken for the adjective Roman <note>original: *Rômaios*, which the Byzantines used to describe themselves as citizens of the Roman Empire</note>, meaning "Roman" or "Greek"; so that "Romanos the Emperor" passed into the Russian tale as "Basil the Roman (King)": although in the fragments extant he is only referred to as a "certain Tsar <note>A title for a king or emperor in Slavic languages.</note> called Basil" who held "all the country of Cappadocia" (Speransky's edition, p. 331).

It is quite another point, to which we may return, that the definite placing of this incident in Cappadocia suggests a time when the frontiers of the empire were in Cappadocia, whereas later incidents imply a time when the frontiers of the empire were on the Euphrates river, so that two distinct historical layers may be traced here.

(3) The Russian version contains so many evidences of folk-story and popular transmission that it is not necessary to see in the defeat of the Emperor by Digenes, who has just jumped over the river Euphrates on his lance,¹ anything more than a climax of popular exaggeration. (Speransky, p. 332; Kalonaros, volume 2, p. 290.)

---

¹ Professor W. J. Entwistle told me that the Dnieper river is jumped in a Russian ballad; but such pole-jumping is also to be found in the Byzantine romance *Callimachus and Chrysorrhoe* <note>original: *Kallim. and Khrysorroe*</note>, line 2532.

<page-num>xliii</page-num>

<summary>This page analyzes how linguistic errors and copyist mistakes likely caused the confusion over which Byzantine Emperor (Basil, Romanos, or Nikephoros) appears in the Digenes Akrites epic. It also highlights the blending of historical frontier reality with legendary folk elements, such as the hero's superhuman feats.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the origins of the Digenes Akrites epic. It contrasts the Russian version of the story with various Greek manuscripts, debating whether the poem's changes over time were the result of natural oral storytelling or deliberate government censorship.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION

<page-num>xliv</page-num>

(4) Over the Euphrates? <note>Referring to a geographical detail in a study by scholarly source SPE, page 332.</note> But what is the Euphrates river doing in <term>Cappadocia</term> <note>original: Kappadokia; a region in central Turkey that was a key military frontier for the Byzantine Empire.</note>? Surely this combination in the Russian version—placing the Euphrates frontier and the Cappadocian frontier in the same incident—can only mean that the Russian text was translated from a Greek version where these two different frontier periods were already mixed together. That is to say, it must have come from a late and complex blending of stories rather than from an original and rebellious political statement. The <term>Trebizond</term> <note>TRE; a manuscript of the poem from 1483.</note> and <term>Andros</term> <note>AND; a Greek manuscript version identified as number 2349.</note> manuscripts combine Cappadocia with the Euphrates, but the <term>Grottaferrata</term> <note>GRO; one of the oldest and most complete Greek versions of the poem.</note> manuscript does not.

(5) It may be true, as the scholar <term>Grégoire</term> <note>Henri Grégoire (1881–1964), a prominent historian of Byzantine literature.</note> argues, that the Russian version is closer to the original folk songs than any other. But this means little more than that both show the same "unpolished" errors that naturally occur when a story is passed down by word of mouth (<term>oral transmission</term>).

(6) If a "loyalist" or "cleaned-up" revision of the poem was officially put into circulation, it seems very unlikely that the editors would have left in the hero's disrespectful answer. In this supposedly "safe" version, <term>Digenes</term> <note>The hero of the epic; his name means "of two races," referring to his Greek and Arab parentage.</note> still gives a defiant reply to the Emperor’s invitation; nor, one might think, would they have allowed him to lecture the Emperor on how to do his job properly.

(7) It is difficult to imagine how an Imperial propaganda office could actually work or be effective in this situation. They would have had to track down every handwritten copy of a heroic <term>saga</term> <note>A long story of heroic achievements.</note> that was being sung in rebellious areas along a mostly illiterate frontier, and then replace them with a sophisticated literary epic that only the educated people in the capital city would enjoy.

We come now in Book VI (Book V in the <term>Grottaferrata</term> version) to a part of the poem that is even more fragmented than usual. The poet seems to have realized that he had nothing left in his book but a collection of short stories (<term>anecdotes</term>). He could not simply throw them away because he was running low on material and needed more romantic subplots. To give these stories a better framework, he decided to write them in the "first person"—presenting them as if the hero himself were telling the stories to his friends after dinner. Accordingly, the next two books (six and seven in the <term>Trebizond</term> and <term>Andros</term> versions, five and six in the <term>Grottaferrata</term>) are spoken by <term>Digenes</term> in his own voice;¹ and although it was stated earlier in the story that he had no friends, he is now shown describing the temptations a young man faces while talking to his friends at the table.

---

¹ The first-person narrative also appears suddenly in the <term>Escorial</term> <note>ESC; a Greek manuscript of the poem known for its more "popular" or folk-like style.</note> manuscript at line 1115.

It also notes a shift in narrative style where the hero begins telling his own life story in the first person.</summary>

<page-num>xlv</page-num>

->STORY<-

some of his own youthful experiences. The episodes in fact become examples of formal boasting, which is a feature of much heroic poetry of every age, like the ‘ritual boasts’ <note>original: "Gabs"; a term for formal, competitive boasting common in medieval French literature.</note> of Charlemagne and his knights (Chadwick, *Heroic Age*, p. 326; see, for example, *Iliad* xx. 83 ff.; and *Beowulf* 480, 636). The opening of the <term>Grottaferrata manuscript</term> <note>GRO; the oldest and most complete Greek version of the poem.</note> Book V, is careful to add that it was not for the sake of boasting but as a manner of repentance that Digenes once told the following story to a passing <term>Cappadocian</term> <note>original: Kappadokian; a person from Cappadocia, a central region of the Byzantine Empire.</note>.

He begins with the strange tale of the daughter of the great Emir <term>Haplorrabdes</term> <note>An Arab leader whose name likely means "the one with the simple staff."</note>, who lived with her father and her mother Melanthia at the city of Meferkeh (GRO v. 66; TRE 1665).

Digenes was fifteen years old at the time (GRO v. 24), or eighteen (TRE 1610), and living with his love on the borders. [The Grottaferrata manuscript, although it retains the detail at the beginning of Book V that he was living apart from his parents on the frontiers, omits the detail that he was living with his beloved Evdokia. It might have been thought that the editor of the Grottaferrata text had omitted his marriage deliberately, in order to minimize his fall from Christian chastity; and had then perhaps been obliged to reduce his age from eighteen to fifteen in order to be more consistent with his celibacy; but a little farther on (GRO v. 57) he has inadvertently allowed Digenes to say that the daughter of Haplorrabdes seemed *second to my own [beloved]* <note>original: δευτέρα τῆς ἐμῆς</note>; and he records the return of Digenes to his own love at the end of the book (GRO v. 281).]

The daughter of Haplorrabdes had the common fortune to fall in love with one of her father’s captives (Eudoxios he is called in the Oxford manuscript <note>OXF 2233; a later, 17th-century Greek version of the poem.</note>: the Oxford manuscript also gives the girl an oriental name, Aïssé, and calls her mother Fatouma instead of Melanthia), son of the Roman general Antiochus. They eloped together with horses and treasure but at their first encampment beside a well he deserted her and rode away. He was, however, attacked by the highwayman Mousour, who would have killed him if Digenes had not just then ridden over the border on his black charger. ‘I killed Mousour’, says Digenes, ‘and gave the young man into the keeping of my friends.’ (Mousour is the only person killed by Digenes who is given an Arab name; and he is not a <term>frontier raider</term> <note>original: ἀπελάτης (apelates); a term for the cattle-rustling warriors of the borderlands.</note> but is described as a *road-blocking robber* <note>original: λῃστὴς ὁδοστάτης (lestes hodostates).</note>.) ‘I rode on and soon came to the Emir’s daughter weeping beside the water under the tree. I was at first frightened and thought I saw a ghost’ (these two lines TRE 1641-2 (AND 2528-9) seem to be imitated according to

# INTRODUCTION

Legrand <note>Émile Legrand (1841–1903), a pioneering French scholar of Greek literature.</note> in the allegorical poem of Meliteniotes¹ 141-2), ‘but she asked me to stay and rest and told me her story.’ She repeats, without much verbal repetition, in greater detail and in character, the account which has already been given of her parentage, her elopement, and her desertion, after she and her lover had been three nights at the well. She had been here alone in the desert for ten days. The day before, an old man crossing the desert on his way to Arabia had told her how he had five days before seen her husband rescued from the brigand Mousour <note>An Arab name, likely a variant of Mansur.</note> at a place called *Blattolibadi* <note>original: "Blattolibadi"; likely meaning "Silk Meadow," a reference to the lushness of the area.</note>, by the young Akrites.

At this moment a band of a hundred Arabs attacked them and attempted to carry her off, but Digenes soon scattered them. (The incident is omitted in the <term>Trebizond manuscript</term> <note>TRE; a 16th-century manuscript found in the Sumela Monastery.</note> 1774 but the lacuna can be filled by the <term>Andros manuscript</term> <note>AND; a prose version of the epic found on the island of Andros.</note> 2671-2720 or the <term>Grottaferrata manuscript</term> <note>GRO; the oldest poetic version.</note> v. 177-97.) She recognized him as the Akrites who had rescued her lover and he offered to lead her back to him if she would renounce ‘the faith of the Aethiopians’ <note>A common Byzantine term for the non-Christian faiths of the East, often used for Saracens or pagans.</note>. She replied that she had already been converted by her husband, and he took her on his own horse to take her back to her husband at Halkogourna <note>original: "Halkogourna"; likely meaning "Bronze Basins."</note> (TRE 1810; but GRO v. 238 Halkourgia; AND 2768 Hohlakoura). But on the road he was inflamed by her beauty, and did not resist the temptation. (*This is the incident which deeply shocked W. P. Ker in his work The Dark Ages, page 345. Perhaps more shocking to modern sentiment are the confessional phrases of the narrator.*) He returned her to her husband—whose father had been killed long ago in subduing the Persians—and no more was said about it; but he gave him plenty of good advice; ‘and still bitterly ashamed and repentant I returned to my own beloved in the middle of April.’

The narration of this incident which fills nearly the whole of the sixth book in the Andros and Trebizond manuscripts and of the fifth in the Grottaferrata is carried through with skill and liveliness. It should be noted that the episode

<meta>This page continues the summary of the narrative themes in the Digenes Akrites epic, transitioning from the story of the abandoned Arabian bride to the beginning of the next book, which focuses on Digenes and his wife, Evdokia, in a pastoral setting.</meta>

<page-num>xlvii</page-num>

<header>STORY</header>

is yet another treatment of the major theme of the poem, that of a runaway marriage between an Arabian and a Greek, a treatment in which it is the Greek who appears in an unfavourable light; while one incident, the desertion of the bride, may be regarded as a pale reflection of the Emir’s return to Syria; and another incident is a doublet of another favourite theme, that of a young couple at an oasis attacked by a band of robbers, a theme the major treatment of which we shall find in the next book. The spring under a palm-tree is a commonplace which becomes an obsession in <term>ESC</term> <note>The Escorial manuscript, a 15th-century version of the poem known for its more "popular" or folk-like style.</note>.

After this April repentance, Book VII (VI in <term>GRO</term> <note>The Grottaferrata manuscript, generally considered the oldest and most "aristocratic" poetic version of the epic.</note>) opens in the month of May, the year’s pleasant King, the loveliness of which moves Akrites, who is still the narrator, to set up his tent in a flowery meadow by a running pool. In the heat of the day he lay down to sleep while Evdokia, not less lovely than the flowers, went down to the water.

The set piece on the month of May (<term>TRE</term> <note>The Trebizond manuscript, a 16th-century version found in the Sumela Monastery.</note> 1860–1920) is in a lifeless and artificial style and is almost certainly a redactor’s embroidery. Several phrases in it seem to be copied in the still more elaborate picture of May at the beginning of the allegorical poem of Meliteniotes <note>Constantine Meliteniotes, a 14th-century Byzantine poet.</note> (e.g. Mel. 37, 38 = TRE 1866, 1867—one of a large number of parallels, to some of which Sathas <note>Konstantinos Sathas (1842–1914), a Greek historian and researcher.</note> and Legrand <note>Émile Legrand (1841–1903), a French Byzantinist.</note> first called attention in their introduction to TRE, introd. pp. cxli ff., and of which a fairly complete list will be found in Appendix D). The month of May has a proverbial quality in all European languages, and in Greece came to be known as *Kalomênas* <note>original: "Kalomênas"; literally "the Good Month."</note>; ‘tears falling like Goodmonth hail’ will be found in the Kerasund version of the *Sons of Andronikos* ballad (Polites <note>Nikolaos Polites (1852–1921), the father of modern Greek folklore studies.</note>, *Eklogai* <note>original: "Eklogai"; Selections from the songs of the Greek people.</note>, p. 298).

And so the Girl—she is always referred to simply as *Kore* <note>original: "Kore"; meaning "the Maiden" or "the Girl."</note> (the name is used almost in a mystical sense; so Meliteniotes always calls the prophetess of his allegory ‘the Maiden’ *Kore*)—after sprinkling him with rosewater, and he asleep with the nightingales singing, went down to the water to drink and paddle. There she was attacked by a serpent in the form of a beautiful youth. She screamed, and Digenes sprang up and seized his sword and had soon slain the monster which now had three fire-breathing heads—(which ESC 1110 characteristically fantasticates into an old man’s head, a young man’s head, and in between them the head of a serpent).

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Book VII (or VI), moving from the description of May and the pastoral setting to the specific challenges Digenes faces: a supernatural serpent, a lion, and finally, a band of human attackers.</meta>

<page-num>xlviii</page-num>

<header>INTRODUCTION</header>

This seductive dragon is remarkable as being the only instance in the poem of a definitely supernatural appearance: there is a sense in which the defeat of three hundred armed men by one unarmed hero is a natural occurrence when compared with the appearance of a fire-breathing dragon or a talking bird. Messieurs Grégoire and Goossens <note>Henri Grégoire and Roger Goossens, prominent 20th-century scholars of Byzantine epic poetry.</note> note that serpents in human form are definitely an Indo-Iranian theme. I cannot help thinking that for the suggestion of such an incident the author need not have gone farther than the garden of Eden. A serpent with four human heads also appears in the Russian version <note>SPE refers to the 'Skazanie o podvigakh Digenisa', the medieval Russian translation and adaptation of the Digenes Akrites story.</note> (SPE, p. 317) by the side of the magic spring in which a light seems to burn; but it is the spring in the forest in which the young Digenes is washed after his first hunting. The Russian version also contains a brief praising of the month of May (SPE, p. 319), but again it is in an entirely different context—in a letter to Digenes from Maximo <note>Maximo, or Maximiana, is an Amazon warrior whom Digenes encounters and later defeats (and in some versions, seduces and kills).</note>.

So the Girl <note>Evdokia, the wife of Digenes.</note> laughed at her fears, and Digenes lay down to sleep again; and the next time she called him from sleep it was to kill a lion which came out of the undergrowth and was preparing to spring at her. He killed it easily with his club without damaging its skin. This time she was cast down by her fears and asked him to play to her to raise her spirits. ‘So I took my lute from the peg. . . . And the sound of the lute and the sound of her voice sent up a pleasant noise into the air and resounded in the mountains’ (<term>TRE</term> 1986). It is almost as if the author were feeling for an effect which was recorded by a greater poet about eight hundred years later:

> O listen! for the Vale profound
> Is overflowing with the sound.
> No nightingale did ever chaunt
> More welcome notes to weary bands
> Of travellers in some shady haunt,
> Among Arabian sands. . . . <note>A quotation from William Wordsworth’s poem "The Solitary Reaper," published in 1807.</note>

After this idyll by the pool—the Serpent and the Lion and the Song—the rest of the book is given up to fighting. First of all a band of three hundred <term>Reivers</term> <note>Translating the Greek 'Apelatai', a class of frontier bandits or border-raiders who appear frequently in the epic as antagonists.</note>, attracted by the sound of the lute and the Girl’s singing, came down from the mountains and attacked them, trying to frighten him away from her: but he drove them away with his club and returned to the tent ‘shaking his [sleeves]’ <note>original: "seiôn ta [manikia]". Shaking one's sleeves or cuffs was a gesture of bravado, triumph, or easy unconcern after a fight.</note>

<summary>The author analyzes the rare supernatural elements of the epic, specifically the dragon and lion encounters, comparing them to Russian variants and English Romantic poetry. It concludes with Digenes successfully defending his wife from a large band of frontier raiders.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the narrative, moving from Digenes's encounter with the wild lion to his confrontation with a group of raiders and his subsequent meeting with three mysterious horsemen on the riverbank.</meta>

<page-num>xlix</page-num>

<header>STORY</header>

sleeves <note>original: *manikia*</note>, TRE 2048, AND 3013; *they shook the sleeve* <note>original: *to manikin eseion*</note>, GRO vi. 158; *I shook out my sleeves* <note>original: *ehysa ta manikia mou*</note>, ESC 118).¹

In OXF 2429 ff. there are only one hundred of them; and in GRO vi. 115 ff. they are only forty-five soldiers ‘passing by a way called Trôsis’: a place also mentioned later, TRE 2289, when Philopappos is reporting to Maximo his encounter with Digenes; and notable as one of the place-names identified by the scholar Grégoire.

Next morning when Digenes went down to the river to wash he met Three Armed Horsemen, who asked him if he had seen the band of Reivers <note>Reivers refers to a band of border raiders or robbers.</note>. (There is a lacuna <note>A gap or missing section in a manuscript.</note> here in TRE 2053, which can be filled by AND 3018–50 or GRO vi. 163–87.) He said they had indeed come along and wanted to carry off his Bride; but he had not even had to get on a horse to deal with them; they should soon know what had happened to the Reivers; for they fell into the pit they themselves had dug. The Three Horsemen whispered together saying, ‘Can this be Digenes the Borderer?’ And to him they said, ‘You must prove your words: choose one of us and fight, and we shall soon know.’ ‘But I only smiled and said, I am an only-begotten son, and I live one and alone—*I am an only-begotten son and I live one and alone* <note>original: "egô monogenés eimi kai monos diatribô"</note>—but with one man alone I have never yet fought.’ (TRE 2069, AND 3066, cf. OXF 2442, but not in GRO which also omits the story of Ankylas which Digenes now tells.) The washing on the banks of the river suggests a reflection of the later scene on the banks of the Euphrates. In fact the scene has suddenly shifted from somewhere near the Kilikian Gates (Blattolivadi, TRE 1763) to the banks of the Euphrates (Trôsis); and the story of Ankylas is placed in Mesopotamia (TRE 2073). The curious emphasis on his being an Only Son and a Lonely Knight suggests that the line which is repeated more than once (AND 3066, 3221; TRE 2223; ESC 1299; and GRO vi. 289, inf.) is meant to have some symbolic significance. It is to be noticed in this connexion that to his great sorrow he had no children (TRE 2950; GRO vii. 180); and that he was thirty-three years old when he died. (Only in AND 1299 and in one of the Death-songs; *Folklore* <note>original: *Laografia*</note>, i (1910), p. 232; also quoted TRE, introd. p. lxiii.) So also were Jesus Christ and Alexander the Great. Digenes proceeds to tell the story of Ankylas which is only found in TRE 2071–2123, AND 3068–3120, OXF 2495 ff., and PAS, pp. 379–81. It is a story within

¹ See *Folklore* <note>original: *Laografia*</note>, iii. 701; iv. 327; and New Testament, *Acts* xviii. 6; *Luke* x. 11; *Mark*, vi. 11, &c.; and cf. the modern proverbial phrase *keep your clothes at a distance* <note>original: *makrya ta rouha sou*; this is a gesture of renunciation or keeping oneself clear of a situation.</note>.

<page-num>1</page-num>

# INTRODUCTION

a story told by Digenes as a warning to the Three Horsemen, and although evidently interpolated <note>Added later by a scribe or editor rather than being part of the original composition.</note> and not included in the text printed below is fairly appropriate to the context.

One day riding down into the plain he had met a young man in Mesopotamia called Ankylas, who had unhorsed and disarmed him, and had written on his club an insulting message—you can tell all the other <term>Reivers</term> <note>The original term used here is *Apelatai*, which refers to the lawless border-raiders or cattle-thieves common in the Byzantine-Arab frontiers.</note> that you have fought with Ankylas and escaped alive. But a year later he says, 'I rode out to his abode and sang a musical challenge to my lute. When Ankylas rode out at me I just tapped him on the head and when I picked him up he was dead.'

So after some more challenging Digenes fights with the Three Horsemen, who are revealed as the *<term>Reiver</term>* <note>original: *Apelate*</note> chiefs, old Philopappos (he is fifty-two years old, TRE 2227), with Kinnamos and Iôannikios who appear to be his sons (GRO vi. 396; TRE 2276; AND 3366). When he had knocked the 'old man' down, the other two attacked him together 'like barking dogs' (TRE 2176), and there is a good long fight before, encouraged by the cries of his Girl, he defeats them, and spares their lives. Then Philopappos asked him to make peace and accept the leadership of all the <term>Cattle-Raiders</term> <note>original: *Abactors*, a Latin-derived term for thieves who drive away entire herds of livestock.</note>. But Digenes replied (nothing about fighting to serve the imperial peace or anything of that sort): 'I do not want to command but to live alone; for I was an only son' (TRE 223; AND 3221; and this time the curious reason is repeated in GRO vi. 289).

At this point there is a <term>lacuna</term> <note>A gap or missing section in a manuscript.</note> in TRE, which can be supplied from AND 3225—3303 or GRO vi. 293-354. The Three Chiefs thank him and depart, but soon begin to wonder and murmur among themselves at their own defeat: fortunately no one had seen it. This Digenes had passed unhurt among their swords, as if he had been a spirit of the place <note>original: *stoiheion tou topou*, suggesting a guardian spirit or supernatural entity associated with a specific location</note> (AND 3273, GRO vi. 326; ESC 1328 *he sees the beast of the place* <note>original: *thêrion ton topon tou blepei*</note>—a characteristic muddle). Not less astonishing had been the beauty of his Girl: she had been like a living statue <note>original: *stêlê empsyhos*</note> (AND 3277, GRO vi. 330; cf. below—TRE 2296, AND 3385, GRO vi. 413—where she is said to be like a breathing image <note>original: *eikon empnous*</note>; cf. Anna Comnena's <note>A Byzantine princess and historian (1083–1153) who wrote the *Alexiad* about her father, Emperor Alexios I.</note> description of her mother Eirene (*Alex. iii. 3*)).

They decide on the advice of Philopappos to summon their friends by lighting beacons, and then attack Digenes again at night. As for the Girl, of whose dazzling loveliness he had never seen the like, she should be given to Iôannikios.

<summary>This page describes Digenes's interaction with three bandit chiefs (Philopappos, Kinnamos, and Iôannikios), including a side story about a youth named Ankylas and the hero's refusal to lead the raiders. The bandits, amazed by Digenes's prowess and his bride's statue-like beauty, plot a nighttime ambush using signal fires.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the Digenes Akrites epic, moving from the hero's victory over the three bandit leaders to the introduction of the Amazon warrior Maximo. <page-num>li</page-num>

# STORY

The whole passage in the Andros manuscript (lines 3234–3353) is muddled and corrupt with numerous repetitions. The offer of the Bride of Digenis to Little Johnnie (Iôannikios) as his share of the spoil is claimed by the scholar Grégoire, as formerly by Polites, to be a trace of an earlier story. This story survives in some of the **Akritic songs** <note>Folk songs belonging to the "Akritic cycle," which celebrate the exploits of the border guards (*akritai*) of the Byzantine Empire.</note>, according to which it was Digenis who carried off the bride of a hero called Iannakos. Grégoire’s arguments are not fully convincing, however eager we may be to discover in the epic some trace of the **ballads** <note>original: *tragoudia*, the Greek word for traditional songs or folk poems.</note> used in its composition. The songs dealing with the *Rape of the Bride*, which are reprinted by Kyriakides, Kalonaros, and Grégoire, may be described as variations of the **Lochinvar theme** <note>A literary motif where a brave man arrives at a wedding to rescue or carry off the bride, popularized in Western literature by Walter Scott's poem "Lochinvar."</note>, with contaminations from numerous other sources; they record a story that Digenis married by capture not the daughter of Doukas but the betrothed of a certain John. It is not true that the Grottaferrata manuscript (VI. 415) means that ‘*the wife of Digenis had been the fiancée of Ioannakes*’. A comparison with the Trebizond manuscript (2298) and the Andros manuscript (3387) shows that Eudokia was wanted for Ioannakes or Ioannikios only in some vague and distant manner: there is no suggestion of betrothal—even if we suppose that the words of Philopappos are meant to have any truth in them at all, in view of the fact that we are definitely told that he was lying in order to interest Maximo (Trebizond 2309, Grottaferrata VI. 425, Andros 3367; compare the Escorial manuscript 1365: **he tells her false words** <note>original: *pseudêis lógous tês légei*</note>). The Grottaferrata version adds the significant detail that Maximo did not even bother to ask who was the present husband of the Girl. It must also be remembered that the natural confusion of the Songs is complicated by the fact that in transmission the names Digenis and Giannes (John) are interchangeable; and that in popular story such a hero as Digenis would have been credited with as many adventures in love as in war. (For the Lochinvar theme see also Polites, *Selections from the Songs of the Greek People*, no. 75, p. 106.)

The lighting of the beacons fails to assemble the expected gathering of **Cattle-Raiders** <term>Abactors</term> <note>A term derived from Latin referring to thieves who drive away entire herds of livestock.</note>, so Philopappos is advised to ‘go and see our kinswoman Maximo’. She was a maiden warrior descended from those Amazons whom King Alexander brought back from India (**from the Brahmans** <note>original: *ek Brahmanôn*</note>, Trebizond 2270). (See the letter from the Amazons to Alexander in the *Pseudo-Callisthenes History of Alexander*—they promise to send him 1,500 picked Amazons to be renewed every year.) Maximo seems to be some relation to Philopappos (Grottaferrata VI. 375; Trebizond 2259. In the Russian version she is his daughter; Speranskij, p. 31). So Philopappos goes

<summary>This page analyzes the literary origins of Digenes's bride and introduces the character Maximo, an Amazon warrior descended from the people encountered by Alexander the Great.</summary>

INTRODUCTION
lii

to Maximo, and tells her that his sons are gone off to the borders to fight the irregulars. (It is not clear why the **apelatai** <term>frontier raiders or bandits</term> should have to fight against **ataktoi** <term>unorganized or irregular troops</term>, unless these ‘irregulars’ were a rival band.) While he was riding along the frontiers, he tells her, he saw in a meadow at a place called Trôsis <note>References to line numbers in the major versions: Trebizond (2289), Andros (3378), and Grottaferrata (vi. 406).</note> the loveliest girl in the world: let Maximo prove her affection and kinship by helping to capture this girl for her dear Iôannikios. Maximo falls into the trap (the suggestion is that she would never willingly have had anything to do with an attack on Digenes if she had known that he was concerned). She called for Melementzes, the leader of her own band of *frontier raiders*, and chose a picked troop of a hundred men. They are joined by Kinnamos with his own band, and come down to the bank of the river. (The scene now and for the rest of the poem is definitely on the banks of the Euphrates.)

On the advice of Philopappos, he alone with Kinnamos and Melementzes advanced along the bank to spy out the position. ‘And I’, says Digenes, still the narrator, ‘was sitting on a rock, holding my grey horse by the bridle, watching for them.’ ‘There he is,’ said Philopappos; ‘we must keep at a safe distance, but find out where the Girl is.’ Melementzes thought this was ridiculous: he had never been afraid of a thousand men; and now was he to run away from one? (There is a **lacuna** <note>A missing section or gap in a manuscript.</note> here in the Trebizond manuscript corresponding to the Andros manuscript lines 3474–3551 and the Grottaferrata manuscript Book VI, lines 492–550.) So Melimentzes advanced alone to the attack (they had crossed the river though their passage is not mentioned). ‘I knocked him off, saddle and all, but while I was watching to see if he would get up, Philopappos came from one side and wounded my horse in the thigh. I chased them only down to the water’s edge, seeing their people all around on the other side and with my horse lamed, and unarmed as I was.’

(It is clear from the passages preceding that Digenes was armed only with sword and mace and dressed in a silk tunic while the others were armed with lances besides wearing body armour and helmets. This is a good example of the fact that in the Greek poem in the standard versions <note>The four main manuscript versions of the story: TRE (Trebizond), AND (Andros), GRO (Grottaferrata), and OXF (Oxford).</note> Digenes is a human warrior, not a giant or a magician as in the Russian version and in the Songs.)

‘I returned to my camp, took arms and a fresh horse, then took the Girl and put her on my horse and hid her in a cave on a hilltop, with provisions, where she could watch without being seen.’ (In the Oxford version, line 2765, she is hidden in a wood where she can *not* see the fighting—a

->STORY<-
liii

(This change is very characteristic of the gentle monk who wrote it: he also silently drops the ‘Amazon’ story: Maximilla <note>The name given to the character Maximo in the Grottaferrata version.</note> is just a modern young woman.)

> ‘When I came down again, there they were on the other side looking for the ford, Maximo in the middle of the four others—Philopappos, Kinnamos, Ioannakes, and Leander.’

(Leander here makes his appearance for the first time and not for long; in the Andros manuscript <note>AND 3552</note> alone five warriors were with her, Melementzes being one of them, the redactor of the Andros version having apparently forgotten that Melementzes had already been disposed of—though he may have been only stunned. Or possibly his reappearance arose from a version from which the first unhorsing of Melementzes was missing owing to a gap in the text, as in the Trebizond manuscript <note>TRE 2384</note>. Melementzes reappears in the final battle in both the Andros and Trebizond versions; the Grottaferrata version alone consistently presents the Big Four, instead of the Big Five, Leander appearing after the unhorsing of Melimitzes.)

Melimentzes, whose name appears as Melimitzes in the Grottaferrata version, and as Melema in the Oxford version, has been plausibly identified by scholars Adontz and Grégoire as the Armenian general Melias or Mleh the Great. This general supported Kourkouas in many of his victories and was granted the frontier fief of Lykandos by Emperor Constantine Porphyrogennetos about 914. (See Constantine Porphyrogennetos, *On the Administration of the Empire* <note>original: "de Adm. Imp."</note> (Budapest, 1949), 50. 135 ff.) They have not noted the curious fact that according to Hasluck (*Christianity and Islam*, pp. 478, 482, quoting Langlois and Grothe) there are still heterodox <term>holding non-traditional religious beliefs</term> tribes in the districts of Adana and Tarsus bearing the name of Melemenji.)

Now Lykantos or Liskantos occurs in the *Digenes* poem as a place name, though not in connection with Melimentzes. It seems to be referred to, in two versions only, in the passage in which Old Doukas, discovering the loss of his daughter—‘It is too true an evil. Gone she is’—calls out the guard to summon his feudal lords and send them in pursuit. From the Escorial manuscript <note>ESC 920</note>:

> Young men from Lykantos, young men from the watchtower, <term>biglan: a military observation post</term>
> help rescue the beautiful girl! He has taken my child.
<note>original: "agouroi apo tou Lykantos agouroi apo tên biglan / boêthêsate eis tên pankopelon epêren to paidin mou."</note>

From the Andros manuscript <note>AND 2006</note>:

> Brave men from Liskantos and youths from the watchtower,
> catch up to the beautiful girl! He has taken my daughter.
<note>original: "andreioi ek tou Liskantos kai neoi ek tês biglês / fthasate ’s ton pankopelon, epêre mou tên korên."</note>

It will be noted that the Andros version ordinarily has a more correct text than the Escorial version; but it can hardly be doubted that here Lykantos is the correct reading and must be the frontier town rebuilt by Melias in the first

# INTRODUCTION

decade of the tenth century. In the second line it is the Andros manuscript <note>AND</note> which preserves the obviously correct reading of the masculine article *the* <note>original: "ton"</note> instead of the feminine *the* <note>original: "tên"</note>. Kyriakides, however, is so much obsessed with ideas of the antiquity and general excellence of the Escorial manuscript <note>ESC</note>, and its capacity for preserving geographical clues, that he prints

->. . . *the [feminine] All-bastard* . . . <note>original: "tên Pankopelon"</note><-

and adds a footnote (*Digenes Akrites* <note>original: "Dig. Akr."</note>, p. 27, n. 3) that a place-name is probably intended. Of course it is the Andros manuscript which preserves the true reading. Although the word *all-bastard* <note>original: "pankopelos"</note> is not in any dictionary, the meaning ‘all-bastard’ is sufficiently obvious, even without investigating the word *bastard* <note>original: "kopelos"</note> in the dictionary of Ducange. 1

Maximo had come forward alone; she was riding a horse black as a swallow, his mane and tail, his ears and his hoofs dyed with scarlet (‘with henna’, in the Escorial manuscript line 1487); and she herself was in cloth of gold with a gold breastplate and a green turban, and she carried a blue and gilded lance.

She asked where Digenes had his men, and when Philopappos told her that he always went about like that, alone with his Girl, she cursed him for an old fool (and very vulgarly in the Escorial manuscript, line 1520) for making all this fuss for one man; she would cross over by herself and bring them back his head. And she started forward; ‘but I’, says Digenes, ‘spoke up and said, “Men ought to come to women, and I will cross over to you.” I rode my horse into the deep water and swam him over to the shallows where she was waiting for me.’ In the fight her lance was broken, and before she could draw her sword Digenes (who seems never to have used a lance himself) had swung his sword and, sparing her, cut off the head of her horse. He left her on the ground crying for mercy, and was immediately surrounded by her men gathering together like eagles; a battle piece of considerable style and dignity describes how he killed or scattered her hundred men-at-arms (Trebizond manuscript <note>TRE</note> 2425–62, Andros manuscript 3590–3629; but in the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>GRO</note> there is only the bare record of their scattering and a protestation that he

---

1 For *bastard* <note>original: "kopelos"</note> see particularly Dawkins, *Mahairas*, ii. 250, 333, showing that this Byzantine sense survives specifically in the Pontic dialect. Although the meaning of *all-bastard* <note>original: "pankopelos"</note> in this context cannot be doubted, it is difficult to produce any formation exactly like it. The nearest seem to be *all-slave* <note>original: "pandoulos"</note> (Anth. Pal. 5. 22. 3); *all-shameful* <note>original: "panaischros"</note> and *utterly-shameful* <note>original: "panaischês"</note> (see Liddell & Scott); *all-beautiful* <note>original: "pankalê"</note> (Escorial manuscript 1738); *all-difficult* <note>original: "panchalepos"</note> (Chariton, vii. 3); and *all-lonely* <note>original: "pantermos"</note> (*Erotokritos* i. 842) which is for *entirely desolate* <note>original: "panerêmos"</note> (Sophocles’ Greek Lexicon, under the entry) and not, as suggested by Xanthoudides, for *five-times-desolate*. The vulgarity of the language here (Escorial manuscript 920) is paralleled by Escorial manuscript 1520, *your backside has grown cold* <note>original: "ho kôlos sou esynkryase"</note>.

# STORY
lv

[The hero] does not want to boast and is indeed only mentioning this occasion (as he said at the beginning of Book V) because he required forgiveness for what followed <note>References to "GRO" refer to the Grottaferrata manuscript version of the poem</note> (GRO vi. 600–8).

And so he was left to deal with the Five—Philopappos, Kinnamos, Ioannikios, Leander, and Melementzes (TRE 2466; AND 3460; but only the first Four in GRO vi. 620) <note>TRE refers to the Trebizond manuscript; AND refers to the Andros manuscript</note>—who tried to cut him off from the river and charged with their lances, but his armour and his sword made them all ineffective. Then Leander alone drew his sword but was knocked into the river, horse and all. The others turned to fly; only Melimentzes in the TRE and AND versions, as well as in the OXF <note>The Oxford manuscript</note> version—which seems to be based mainly on their tradition—tries to turn and fight again but is unhorsed with one blow. He shouted after them but did not pursue them: ‘I always took pity on the fugitive. One must conquer but not over-conquer, and love one’s opponents’—**“To conquer but not to over-conquer, to love one’s opponents”** <note>original Greek: "Nikan kai mē hypernikan, filein tous enantious"</note> (TRE 2510; AND 3679; GRO vi. 642. With these **“to spare the conquered”** <note>original Latin: "parcere subjectis"; a famous Roman sentiment from Virgil's Aeneid regarding imperial duty</note> sentiments, compare the lecture on imperial duties in GRO iv. 1030 ff., TRE 1525 ff., etc.). He goes to send Maximo away with a warning and she thanks him for his mercy and blesses him for his valour, and asks that they may meet again in the morning in single combat. He accepts gladly and sends her home on one of the many horses now riderless. (The GRO manuscript, vi. 685–711, takes the opportunity to insert an explanation that his victory over such a crowd was mainly due to the help of the saints Theodore—both the Recruit and the General—George, and Demetrios.)

So he crosses the river to his tent, changes arms and horses, dresses in a purple silk tunic with a scarlet hood, and as it is already evening he does not visit Evdokia but sends to her two of his serving men (for they had separate tents for menservants and maidservants at some distance from their own and from each other—a domestic detail which is twice repeated: TRE 1453, 2555; AND 2315, 3737; GRO vi. 725, iv. 962). Then he crosses the river Euphrates and waits for the night to pass while his horse rests in the meadow. ‘At dawn I rose up and mounted and rode into the plain and waited: and when the day came, and the daylight, and the sun shone on the heights, then Maximo appeared in the open alone. She was riding a horse white as snow, its hoofs dyed scarlet; and she wore a plated cuirass, and over it a tabard embroidered with pearls, and she carried a gleaming Arabian lance blue and gilded, a sword at her waist and a <term>yataghan</term> <note>A type of short, curved Ottoman sword or knife without a guard</note> at her saddle, a shield of silver with a gilt border, and in...

INTRODUCTION
lvi

the centre the head of a lion in gold studded with precious stones.’

Perhaps the two swords here worn by Maximo are the one-edged and the two-edged sword as worn by Waltharius (*Waltharius* 338) <note>Waltharius is a 10th-century Latin epic about a Germanic hero</note> (for whose connection with Digenes, if any, see below p. lxxiv.)

> And his left thigh he had girt with a double-edged sword,
> And his right with another, according to the custom of the Pannonians,
> Yet he nonetheless deals wounds from one side only.
<note>original Latin: "Et laevum femur ancipiti praecinxerat ense, / Atque alio dextrum, pro ritu Pannoniarum, / Is tamen ex una tantum dat vulnera parte."</note>

After riding round for a little they charged, but neither was unhorsed. When they drew swords, he cut her over the fingers so that her sword fell to the ground; and then to show how completely she was at his mercy he killed her horse, cutting it in two with a single stroke. She begged for mercy and told him that she had kept her virginity for the man who should conquer her. He says that he has a wife of his own; but they sit under the trees by the river where she bathes her wounded hand and puts on it the proper herb which they carried with them in battle. When she took off her armour her body gleamed as if seen in a mirror; the description seems to come mostly from a picture of Europa in Achilles Tatius <note>An ancient Greek novelist known for vivid, sensual descriptions</note>, but there is another maiden in a gossamer singlet in Eustathius Makrembolites (ii. 4. 3). Afterwards he was ashamed of having yielded to a sinful temptation. He rides away saying ‘go in peace and do not forget me’ while Maximo, washing herself in the river, tries to make him turn back. But he rode quickly back to his own true love, his ‘untroubled fountain set apart’¹ (TRE 2641), who questioned him rather sharply about his long absence. He told her that he had had to attend to Maximo’s wounds, and her suspicions were soon allayed. In the Escorial manuscript <note>referred to as ESC</note> (1580 ff.), however, the **maiden** <note>original: "Korasion"; a term often used for Digenes' wife</note> guessed what had happened and seems to have been rather amused.

So ends Book VII to which the Andros manuscript <note>referred to as AND</note> 3853–84 adds a lengthy recapitulation and peroration—and Book VII brings to an end the section which is narrated by Digenes in the first person. In the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>referred to as GRO</note> there is a leaf missing after line vi. 785, and the editor has filled the lacuna by extracting lines 2632–72 from the Trebizond version <note>referred to as TRE</note>. The original redactor of this Grottaferrata version has added to this incident a savage conclusion which occurs in no other version. On thinking it over, Digenes pretends that he is going for a day’s hunting, rides back, and slaughters Maximo for her sin.

# STORY
### lvii

daughter of Philipap, and Devgeny <note>The Russian form of the name Digenes</note>, after pole-jumping over the Euphrates, easily defeats them both and sends them home to his father and mother. But he refuses to join them or marry Maximiana <note>The Russian version of the Amazon warrior Maximo</note>, because the dream book <note>A book used for interpreting omens and predicting the future</note> tells him that if he marries her he will live sixteen years but if he marries the fair Strategovna (the daughter of the General <note>Her name is derived from the Greek "Strategos," meaning General</note>) he would live thirty-six years. So he carried off and, after several bachelor parties, married the General’s daughter (in a passage which is less unlike the Greek versions than the rest of the poem, SPE <note>Refers to Mikhail Speransky’s scholarly edition of the Russian texts</note> pp. 322 ff.) and then settled down to a life of fame and hunting until he was attacked by the ‘Caesar called Basil’ <note>Likely a reference to the Byzantine Emperor Basil II</note> whom he defeated on the Euphrates and reigned in his stead. But the third Russian manuscript, dated 1761, recently discovered, which seems to provide a better and an older text than the two published by Speransky, brings the story to an end with the abduction of Strategovna.¹

There is not much more to tell. Book VIII (VII in GRO <note>Refers to the edition by scholar Henri Grégoire</note>), returning to narrative in the third person, describes with some elaboration the Garden and Palace which Digenes made for himself and Evdokia on the banks of the Euphrates. The description of the garden is to some extent a doublet <note>A literary term for a scene that repeats or mirrors an earlier one</note> of the description of the garden in May at the beginning of Book VII. The architecture of the Palace is described in detail. The tower, square at the base and octagonal above and so high that from the top one could see right over Syria towards Babylon (TRE 2765; AND 3970 <note>These cite the lines from the Trapezuntine and Andros manuscripts</note>), recalls some of the tenth-century Persian towers of brick visited by Robert Byron <note>An early 20th-century travel writer known for his work on Byzantine and Islamic architecture</note>. The decorations of the great hall include mosaics of Samson and the Philistines, David and Goliath, Saul, Achilles, Agamemnon, Penelope and the Suitors, Odysseus and the Cyclops, Bellerophon, Darius, Alexander the Great, Queen Candace (a queen of Tarsus in the Alexander legend, not to be confused with the queen of the Ethiopians of Acts viii. 27),² Moses and the Plagues of Egypt, and the miracles of Joshua the son of Nun.

Half-way through this list in TRE, AND, and PAS <note>Abbreviations for the various primary manuscripts: Trapezuntine, Andros, and the Grottaferrata/Passow edition</note> appear in the company of Kinnamos the mysterious figures of Aldelaga and Olope (TRE 2817; Aldegala and Elope, AND 4022; Aldephaga and Elope, PAS, p. 400), names which are enough to send us hunting through the *Thousand Nights and a Night* <note>Also known as the Arabian Nights</note>, especially if we have been

->INTRODUCTION<-

reading one of Grégoire’s articles on Arabian contacts; inspiring names which caused Krumbacher, speaking of lost Byzantine romances, to give as an instance "an otherwise unknown story of the suffering of the couple Aldelagas and Olope" <note>original German: "eine sonst unbekannte Leidensgeschichte des Paares Aldelagas und Olope"</note> (*History of Byzantine Literature* <note>original: *Byz. Litt.*</note>, p. 855). Once again the relatively direct descent of the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>referred to as GRO</note> from the original source is suggested by the fact that this manuscript alone has preserved in these lines (GRO vii. 86–88) the identity of Agamemnon, Penelope, Odysseus, and the Cyclops.

In the *Allegorical Poem* of Meliteniotes <note>a 14th-century Byzantine poet known for his long, descriptive verse</note>, mentioned above as containing expressions apparently copied from *Digenes*, there is mentioned (line 2218, edited by Miller, p. 105) among other monuments of famous men, one of "Aderaphas"—a name which is said by Grégoire and Goossens (*Classical Antiquity* <note>original: *Ant. Class.*</note> i. 425) to be copied from the "Aldelaga" of the Andros manuscript. On this it may be remarked that this mistake *is not* confined to the Andros manuscript but is also in the Trebizond manuscript <note>TRE</note> 2817 and the Paschalis edition <note>PAS</note>, p. 400. Secondly, the name Aderaphas was certainly not copied from any version of *Digenes*; for Meliteniotes goes on to attribute to "Aderaphas" the "bedstead of iron" of Og the King of Bashan (Deuteronomy 3:11); and it is evident that Aderaphas has taken his name as well as his bedstead from the same passage in the Septuagint <note>the Greek translation of the Old Testament</note>, where the name *Raphaein* occurs (translated "giants" in the Authorized Version). Compare Meliteniotes 2000, "the descendant of Rapha, one of the giants" <note>original Greek: *ton tou Raphan apogonon ton apo tôn gigantôn*</note>.

The corresponding passage in the Escorial manuscript <note>ESC</note> (1630–60) produces only a characteristically muddled account of the gardens, in which the bard tries to incite his memory by casting down for the catchword, and drawing up, twice, a line about water springing up at the foot of a palm-tree (ESC 1633, 1646) which rightly belongs to the beginning of the story of the daughter of Haplorrabdes, the Deserted Bride at the Oasis (TRE 1632; GRO v. 33; AND 2519). He skips the palace with a few vague lines about a building surrounded by golden animals spouting water; and then he makes the quite original contribution that Digenes built a Bridge; a Bridge which crossed the Euphrates in a single span,¹ and on it a four-chambered building of white marble to contain his tomb (ESC 1660–77).

¹ There can be no doubt about the meaning of "single-arched" <note>original Greek: *monokerato*</note> although "horn" <note>original: *keras*</note> is not found elsewhere in the sense of "arch" or "span" or "bow." But Grégoire (*National Herald* <note>original: *Ethnikos Kêryx*</note>, Oct. 1941, and *Digenes Akritas*, New York, 1942, p. 94) prefers the astonishing reading "built with his hands alone" <note>original Greek: *monocherata*</note> which he translates "built with his hands alone."

->STORY<-
lix

Only two lines are given to the building of a Church of Saint Theodore <note>Manuscript references: Trebizond (TRE) 2854; Andros (AND) 4059; Grottaferrata (GRO) vii. 105</note>. The Theodores seem to be the saints most often mentioned; AND 1920 (where he is called the **great border-raider** <note>original Greek: *megas apelates*</note>), 2266, and 4236; with George and Demetrios (GRO vi. 701).

Here by the Euphrates Digenes lived, devoting his wealth to good works and the pacification of the Empire. All the princes and governors of the Byzantine Empire <note>original: Romania, the contemporary term for the lands of the Eastern Roman Empire</note> sent him gifts in gratitude for his services; and the **Emperor** <note>original: *Basileus*</note> every day sent presents to the **Border-Guard** <note>original: Akrites</note>. Romans, Saracens, Persians, or men of Tarsus on the roads, not one of them dared approach without his order or pass without his seal, for the **border-raiders** <note>original: Apelates</note> who served and feared him would have destroyed them.

Grégoire <note>Henri Grégoire (1881–1964), a renowned Byzantinist who sought historical kernels in the epic</note>, having identified the headquarters of Digenes at Trôsis with Trusch near Samosata, clinches the identification and the nature of his activity by adding that on an adjacent hill there still stands a ruined monument of one of the Commagene Kings <note>Kings of a small Hellenistic kingdom located in modern-day south-central Turkey</note> which may well have been known in local legend as the ‘Tomb’ of Digenes; while near by is a village bearing the name of Gömrük which clearly marks the site of a Byzantine **custom house** <note>original: *kommerkyon*</note>.

When his father the Emir died, in Cappadocia, his mother came to live with them (TRE 2880). Curious details are given about his domestic life: his dislike of having servants about him; they all had to keep out of his way; he would ring a bell when he was ready for dinner, and they would all withdraw before he and Evdokia took their places on one couch, and only one little boy waited in the room as a cup-bearer and was allowed to see her. They stood up when his mother came in last and sat in her own armchair; and after dinner they often had music and Evdokia would dance. The oriental seclusion of Evdokia is again referred to in the next book (TRE 3040 ff.; AND 4279 ff.).

The domestic habits of Digenes, which are described several times in the course of the poem, may be intended as models of feudal etiquette. It seems more probable that they were characteristics of a definite historical personage, and were perhaps copied from some lost imperial biography, or history of Alexander the Great (cf. his entertainment of the wife of Darius).

Five years later (GRO vii. 190) his mother dies, and her death is celebrated in the Trebizond and Andros manuscripts with a conventionally elaborate

->INTRODUCTION<-

<term>funeral laments</term> <note>original: *moirologi*</note>. The Grottaferrata manuscript <note>referred to as GRO</note> omits these **funeral laments** but celebrates her as the inspirer of the peace everywhere imposed by Digenes: in fact, her authorship of the policy of peace is so much insisted on (GRO vii. 198 ff.) that we begin to wonder if she was named **Irene** <note>original: *Eirene*, the Greek word for "Peace"</note> with some symbolical intention. In that case, it is curious that the Grottaferrata version does not mention her name at all, and that the name Irene is only given to her in the later versions—the Andros manuscript <note>AND (68)</note> and the Oxford manuscript <note>OXF (67)</note>. The Grottaferrata version concludes Book VII with her death. The Trebizond <note>TRE</note> and Andros manuscripts, closely followed by the Paschales version <note>PAS</note> (but not by the Oxford version, which in the composition of the close is more akin to Grottaferrata), begin their Ninth books with the death and **funeral laments** of his mother. They then fill the rest of the book with a recapitulation of the glory of Digenes and a list of the line of great Arab raiders whose exploits were brought to an end when the Emir Mousour, father of Akrites—called after his baptism **John** <note>original: *Ioannes*; TRE 3071</note>—was baptized and kept his faith and settled down in Cappadocia and fathered the young Akrites. The young hero wonderfully subdued the **Outlaws** and **Irregulars** <note>original: *Apelates*</note> who held the passes and the marches so that they paid a tribute to the Emperor. His successes were suitably rewarded by the Emperor **Nicephorus** <note>original: *Nikeforos*; TRE 3110; AND 4347</note>.

In Book X (Book VIII in the Grottaferrata version), Digenes falls ill. ‘All things in this vain world come to an end’—a line which is found in every Greek version (GRO viii. 1; TRE 3130, as well as 2986, i.e., at the beginning of each of the last two books; AND 4222 and 4368; ESC 1695; OXF 2975; PAS, pp. 403 and 406). The Grottaferrata version is quite brief and rationalistic about it; his illness started with a cramp after bathing. The doctors could do him no good and told him so. He sends them away and calls **Eudokia** <note>original: *Evdokia*</note> and makes her sit near him and, in a fine and moving speech, recalls all their past life together: how fearlessly he had carried her off; their lovely life in the desert at **Blattolivadi** <note>Likely meaning "Clover-meadow"</note>—the Serpent and the Lion and the Well; and how he fought with Robbers and Outlaws; and **Maximo** (the Amazon-like leader whom he afterwards murdered secretly, GRO viii. 120). He tells her she would have to marry again for protection rather than endure the "drought of widowhood." She begins to pray and at the end of her prayer looks up and sees him in the **moment of death** <note>original: "article of death"</note> and herself falls dead; so that he died happy. The Trebizond version comes to an end at line 3182 at the beginning of his last speech; and unfortunately it is not so satisfactory here to fill the gap from the Andros version, because Andros ends as it began with a piece of rather weak and monkish writing. ## 4. DISCUSSIONS

### I

An attempt must be made to summarize the interpretations and estimates of the *Poem of Digenes* put forward by a succession of scholars, from the first editors Sathas and Legrand down to M. Henri Grégoire. Beginning in 1930, Grégoire (assisted on questions regarding the relations between Byzantium and the Arabs by the researches of E. Honigmann, A. A. Vasiliev, and Marius Canard) put these studies on a new foundation. He first entered this field by demonstrating that incidents of Anatolian history not mentioned by any Byzantine historian, but known only from Arab sources, could be traced in *Digenes*. He was soon showing that the author of *Digenes* was equally conversant with Byzantine sources; and in particular that some of the original details for which the Escorial version <note>referred to as ESC</note> had acquired a reputation as a folk-epic of infallible originality were actually borrowed directly from **Genesius** <note>Joseph Genesius, a 10th-century Byzantine historian</note>, and in one detail went back to **Herodotus** <note>The famous 5th-century BC Greek historian known as the "Father of History"</note>. (See *Byzantion*, volume v (1930), pages 327 and following; and page 128; Escorial manuscript, lines 254 and following; Grottaferrata manuscript, book ii, lines 75 and following; Genesius, pages 121-6 (describing the capture of **Ankyra** <note>Modern-day Ankara, Turkey</note> by the Paulicians); and the same author, page 94; *Theophanes Continuatus* <note>A 10th-century collection of Byzantine historical chronicles</note>, page 179.)

### II

It must be remembered to the credit of Sathas and Legrand that they, the first editors, were the first to see that the historical incidents and background against which the hero was set in motion were the rebellions, in the ninth and tenth centuries, of the **Paulician** <note>A Christian sect in the Byzantine Empire often accused of heresy; they were known for their military prowess and defiance of the central government</note> heretics, to whom **Gibbon** <note>Edward Gibbon, author of The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire</note> devoted a chapter and **Bury** <note>J.B. Bury, a prominent historian of the late 19th and early 20th centuries</note> an appendix (*Decline and Fall*, chapter liv; edited by Bury, volume vi, pages 110, 543). In spite of these attentions, their doctrines remain wrapped in considerable obscurity. The publication of an Armenian text *The Key of Truth* (edited by Conybeare, 1898) is said to show that they held the belief, commonly known as <term>Adoptionism</term> <note>The belief that Jesus was born merely human and only became divine at his baptism</note>, that Jesus was only a man until the entrance of a spirit on his baptism in his thirtieth year. They are said to have

<summary>This page analyzes the scholarly history of the Digenes Akrites poem, highlighting Henri Grégoire's discovery of historical parallels in Arab and Byzantine sources and the poem's connection to the Paulician heretical movement.</summary>

### DISCUSSIONS

from all time God's foe and enemy to all good:
In hate of which hell-power so worthy of man’s defiance
they had lost the old fear, and finding internal war
between flesh and spirit in the authentic writings
of <term>Paul of Tarsus</term> <note>The Apostle Paul, whose letters in the New Testament emphasize the struggle between "the spirit" and "the flesh."</note>, him they took for master, and styled
themselves <term>Paulicians</term> <note>A medieval Christian sect often accused of dualism—the belief that the physical world was created by an evil power.</note> the depositories of <term>Christ</term>.
    Their creed—better than others at freeing <term>God</term>
from blame for evil—and their strict self-denial
shamed the half-hearted clergy, whose indulgence in sin
confirmed the group's rigid logic, which concluded
that the visible earthly Church was a device of <term>Satan</term>. . . .

All of this may be accepted as a fair account of the <term>Paulicians</term>, except that they took their name not from <term>Paul of Tarsus</term> but from <term>Paul of Samosata</term> <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Antioch whose teachings on the nature of Christ were declared heretical.</note>, as is sufficiently proved by the passage in <term>Anna Comnena’s</term> *Alexiad* <note>A historical account written by the Byzantine princess Anna Comnena in the 12th century.</note> already referred to. This also suggests that the <term>Paulicians</term> were more deeply influenced than the <term>Bogomils</term> <note>A similar religious sect that rose in the Balkans.</note> by <term>Manichaeanism</term> <note>A dualistic religion teaching a fundamental struggle between light and darkness.</note>. <term>Obolensky</term>, however, insists that their name must be derived from an Armenian word *Polik* (a mocking nickname meaning "Little Paul") and that the connection with <term>Paul of Samosata</term> "cannot be justified either doctrinally or historically" and "must be finally abandoned" (*The Bogomils*, pp. 55, 56).

After the most savage persecution by the imperial armies, especially under the Regency of <term>Theodora</term> (842–856), the <term>Paulicians</term> revolted under the Armenian General <term>Carbeas</term>. They formed an alliance with <term>Omar</term>, the Emir of <term>Melitene</term>, and fortified <term>Tefrik</term> (which appears as *Afrikê* in <term>GRO</term> ii. 78) and other strongholds in the Armenian borderlands. From there, says the historian <term>Gibbon</term>, "in their hostile raids the disciples of <term>St. Paul</term> [sic] joined with those of <term>Mahomet</term> <note>An archaic spelling of Muhammad, referring here to the Islamic forces.</note>." In 859, <term>Carbeas</term> defeated Emperor <term>Michael III</term>, son of <term>Theodora</term>, under the walls of <term>Samosata</term>.

<term>E. W. Brooks</term> (*Cambridge Medieval History*, iv. 133) says that this reading in <term>Genesius</term> should be corrected to <term>Arsmosata</term> because "<term>Omar</term> had nothing to do with <term>Samosata</term>"; however, <term>Samosata</term> was the headquarters of the <term>Paulicians</term>. Under the Regency of <term>Bardas</term> (862–6) and the leadership of his brother <term>Petronas</term>, <term>Omar</term> was defeated and killed at <term>Poson</term> in 863. But the <term>Paulician</term> rebels under <term>Chrysochen</term> continued to plunder <term>Asia Minor</term> until <term>Basil I the Macedonian</term> (867–86) sued for peace, or at least sent an embassy of conciliation which led to a renewal of the war. In a later campaign, <term>Chrysochen</term> was defeated and beheaded (873), and <term>Tefrik</term>, <term>Melitene</term>, and <term>Samosata</term> came definitely under <term>Byzantine</term> rule. Yet, says <term>Gibbon</term>, "the <vocab>Paulicians, Christ, God, Satan, Paul of Tarsus, Paul of Samosata, Anna Comnena, Alexiad, Bogomils, Manichaeanism, Obolensky, Polik, Theodora, Carbeas, Omar, Emir of Melitene, Tefrik, Afrikê, GRO, Gibbon, St. Paul, Mahomet, Michael III, Samosata, E. ### INTRODUCTION

spirit of independence survived in the mountains; the <term>Paulicians<note>A medieval Christian sect, often labeled as heretical, that formed a militant state on the border between the Byzantine Empire and the Arab Caliphate.</note></term> defended above a century their religion and liberty, infested the Roman limits, and maintained their perpetual alliance with the enemies of the empire and the gospel’.

It is impossible to read <note>TRE refers to the Trebizond manuscript version of the poem.</note> TRE 187 ff. without accepting the identification of Chrysocher the Paulician with Chrysocherpes, father of the Emir Mousour and grandfather of Digenes. Chrysocherpes (Chrysoberges in <note>GRO refers to the Grottaferrata manuscript version of the poem.</note> GRO i. 284), marrying Spathia of Rahab-Edessa (called Panthia in GRO i. 284), became son-in-law of Ambrôn who is Omar of Melitene. After the death in battle of Chrysocherpes, his son the Emir Mousour tells us that he was brought up by his ‘Arabian uncles’ (<note>original Greek: theioi Arabitai</note> *Arabian uncles*, TRE 80, 808; GRO i. 287); and these Arabian uncles must be Mousour of Tarsus, and Karôes, for the Emir’s mother, in GRO ii. 75, explicitly calls Karôes her brother. Karôes is to be identified with equal certainty with Carbeas. All these principal identifications, Ambron–Omar, Chrysocherpes–Chrysocher, Karôes–Carbeas, were originally pointed out by Sathas and Legrand in their edition of Trebizond (introd. pp. lxiv ff.) and have been abundantly confirmed by Grégoire. 

But I do not think it has been noticed that if the Emir Mousour is son of Spathia and of the Paulician Christian Chrysocher, he is just as much a <term>Digenes<note>Meaning "of two races" or "two-blooded," typically referring to the hero's mixed Byzantine-Greek and Arab-Muslim parentage.</note></term>, a child of two races, as his own son who is the *Digenes* par excellence of the poem; and this is what he implies when he emphasizes the fact that he was brought up by his Arabian uncles or kinsmen; he means that if his father had not been killed he would have been a Christian (GRO i. 288 is corrupt, but the emendation is obvious); and if Spathia calls Carbeas her own brother (a point which Grégoire seems to have missed), she is definitely naming as an Arab of the true faith the other Paulician leader of Armenian extraction. What is the explanation? It can only be that the author had very little knowledge of the Paulician heresy. This is exactly what we should suppose from the fact that it is impossible to squeeze out of the poem the faintest trace of Paulician doctrine¹ (unless anyone is optimistic enough to suggest that the name of Digenes symbolizes a <term>Manichaean dualism<note>A religious philosophy viewing the universe as a struggle between good/spirit and evil/matter; Paulicians were often incorrectly accused of being Manichaeans.</note></term>!). It follows that the poem is not and never can have been a Paulician pamphlet, as Grégoire suggests, of which fragments have survived in the Slavonic—
