# INTRODUCTION

...very soon corrupted to *Giánnes* <note>A common Greek name, equivalent to John</note> (for example, the ballads on the death of Digenes found in the journal *Folklore* <note>original: "Laografia"</note>, volume i (1910), numbers 27, 31, 38, 39).

Finally, it must be said that even if Diogènes were a more convincing historical figure, it is apparent that the epic is not about a good officer or even about a successful general of the imperial government, but about a lonely hero of romance; a hero who somehow crystallized social and political emotions and perhaps—like King Arthur, Robin Hood, or Piers Plowman <note>A central character in a major Middle English allegorical poem</note>—was not a direct reflection of any single real person.

Grégoire’s <note>Henri Grégoire (1881–1964), a prominent scholar of Byzantine history and literature</note> most original and substantial contribution to research on Digenes is his dissection and exhibition of the Arabic element in the poem. Digenes has an Arabic counterpart, an historical character named Abd Allah Abu-’l Husain el Antaki el Battal, commonly known as Sidi Battal, who was killed during an Arab raid at Akroenos in 740 (see Hasluck, *Christianity and Islam*, page 709). His legendary adventures are recorded in the Turkish romance of *Sidi Battal* (which itself dates no earlier than the fourteenth century and is most familiar in Ethé’s German translation), and his tomb near Eskishehr was still a place of pilgrimage in Asia Minor in the twentieth century. He also married a Christian princess and, in fact, had several Christian wives, one of whom was the daughter of his vizier Akrates—so named, presumably, after Akrites himself. (Hasluck (pages 706 and following), from whom most of these details are taken, makes a strange error when he remarks that "Digenes Akritas elopes with an emir’s daughter".) This Turkish romance acquires some historical importance when the scholar Marius Canard discovers that it incorporates the substance of a tenth-century song of the Emir of Melitene, an Arabic *epic tale* <note>original: "Gest"</note> of Omar of Melitene. Grégoire had suspected the existence of such a tale must underlie the earlier part of the Digenes epic, as it is largely occupied with Arabian <term>andragathy</term> <note>From the Greek "andragathia," meaning "manly bravery" or "heroic deeds"</note> and celebrating the gallantry of the Emir (the father of Digenes) and his grandfather Ambron (who is actually Omar of Melitene, although the city of Melitene is never mentioned). This Arabic epic material appears most clearly in the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>referred to here as GRO</note>; more clearly than in the Escorial manuscript <note>referred to as ESC</note> which Grégoire usually quotes. It refers openly to the Emir’s harem, as in fact do all versions except the seventeenth-century Oxford manuscript <note>OXF</note>; see the Trebizond <note>TRE</note> version line 215, Grottaferrata <note>GRO</note> book iii line 127, and the Andros <note>AND</note> version line 669—all references are quite as explicit as the Escorial version line 236 regarding the "delightful maidens" <note>original: "terpna korasia"</note> he has left behind him. The Grottaferrata version is the only one which refers to the Muslim sanctuary at Palermo, in book i line 101; although the Escorial version

<meta>This page continues the introduction's analysis of historical figures within the epic of Digenes Akrites, specifically focusing on the identity of the Emperor Basil mentioned in the poem.</meta>

### V

The emperor Basil who delights to honour his namesake *Digenes* in the oldest version <note>Referring to the Grottaferrata manuscript, version IV, line 972</note> is clearly Basil II, the <term>Bulgar-Slayer<note>original Greek: "Bulgaroctone", a title earned by Basil II (976–1025) for his decisive victories over the Bulgarian Empire.</note></term> (976–1025), ‘who at that time was managing the empire of the Romans, Basil the blessed, the great conqueror, who indeed buried with himself the imperial glory; for he happened to be making his expedition against the Persians in those parts where the Boy was to be found’. His Persian campaign may quite permissibly refer to Basil’s Georgian campaign of 1021, although Kyriakides, while accepting the manifest reference to Basil II, decides that *Persians* cannot here mean Persians—‘for he happened to be making his expedition against the Persians’ <note>original: *etyhe gar kata Persôn poiôn tên ekstrateian*</note>—but is an anachronism put in in the eleventh century as a compliment to Nikeforos Botaneiates who was proud of his descent from Basil II. (*Folklore* <note>original: *Laografia*</note>, x. 654.) It is noteworthy that in the Grottaferrata version iv. 56 ‘Basil the Blessed the Great Borderer’ is also given as the name of the emperor who had banished the grandfather of Digenes; and that owing to the length of his reign Basil II is one of the few emperors who might really have lived to honour the grandson after banishing the grandfather. (The corresponding passages in the Trebizond version, line 836, and the Andros version, line 1369, mention Romanos as the emperor who banished the grandfather, evidently because in those versions it is Romanos who honours the grandson Digenes.) Sathas and Legrand connect the disgracing of the grandfather with the conspiracy against Leo VI in 908 led by the real Andronikos Doukas who strangely enough went over to the Arabs (Trebizond version, introduction page 95 <note>original: "introd. p. xcv"</note>). All of which goes to show again that the history of *Digenes* while often recognizable is not real history. It is ‘typical’ history presenting a generalized or abstract picture of the Eastern frontier.

The elaborate theories of Grégoire and Kyriakides, who suppose that successive revisers made genealogical interpolations in order to do honour to various living individuals, require us to think that the author or reviser possessed a mentality which is not to be found in other parts of the poem; and ask us to believe, if we can, that a Doukas at any period within the author’s range would have been highly honoured on being related, by an anonymous monk, to the grandmother of a legendary hero. The author undoubtedly took what names he could from the histories available, and he would not have had to look very far to find the names of Doukas or Mouselês: and

<summary>This page analyzes the historical identification of the Emperor Basil in the Digenes Akrites epic, arguing that while he represents Basil II, the poem conflates various historical events and figures into a "typical" frontier history.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the historical figures mentioned in the epic poem *Digenes Akrites*, moving from the identity of the Emperor Basil to the specific historical generals and the chronological markers that suggest when the poem was written.</meta>

<page-num>lxxxiii</page-num>
->CONCLUSIONS<-

Regarding the young women, there are fourteen women named Evdokia and twenty-one named Eirene in the index of Du Cange's <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a founding scholar of Byzantine studies.</note> *Byzantine Families* <note>original Latin: *Familiae Byzantinae*</note>. It must have been from a history book that the author took the name of Soudales the "Saracen" <note>A medieval term for Arabs or Muslims.</note> who fought for Evdokia's father on the night of the elopement. Soudales, as has been said, was one of three generals sent against the <term>Paulicians<note>A dualist Christian sect on the eastern frontier of the Byzantine Empire who were often persecuted as heretics.</note></term> by the Empress Theodora in 855; in the *Continuation of Theophanes* <note>A collection of historical chronicles covering the years 813 to 961.</note> their names are given as "the son of Argyros, and the son of Doukas, and Soudales"; but in the transcription of this chronicle by Skylitzes <note>John Skylitzes, a late 11th-century historian.</note> the names are given as "Leon Argyros, and Andronikos Doukas, and Soudales." The appearance of Soudales alongside Andronikos Doukas suggests that it was from this specific passage that the author of the *Digenes* poem took both these names. However, this pairing is found not in the original historical record but only in the later transcription by Skylitzes; and Skylitzes was a contemporary of Michael Psellos writing in the middle of the eleventh century. Therefore, the pairing of Soudales and Andronikos Doukas in the poem is another detail that hints at this later date of composition.¹

## VI

Digenes is a symbolic hero; he must accordingly be placed in a symbolical setting. For this reason, the poem gives us no literal history but rather a composite arrangement of history, in which fragments or aspects of many actual facts are rearranged to create a universalized image of the conflict on the eastern frontier, combined with elements of pure romance. This legendary or at least typical period of conflict, far from being contemporary with the time the poem was written, is separated from it by a gap. The existence of this gap is indicated by the word <term>at that time<note>original Greek: *tênikauta*; used in the Grottaferrata manuscript, version iv. line 972.</note></term>, a detail which has often been overlooked by scholars.

For such a legendary period, it was necessary to provide "typical" emperors. We may suppose that the author chose as his ideal emperor the recently deceased Basil II; and that the first person to rewrite the poem preferred the prestigious names of Romanos Lekapenos and Nikephoros Phokas, without considering or caring whether both these emperors could have realistically honored a hero who died at the young age of thirty-three. Of one thing we may be quite certain: the emperors named were not the author's contemporaries. Basil II died in 1025.

***

¹ See Bury, *Eastern Roman Empire*, p. 278; Gibbon, v. 507. Andronikos (or Aaron) Doukas is named as the father of Eirene in the Trebizond version, line 844, and the Andros version, line 20; see Appendix A.

<summary>The author argues that the poem *Digenes Akrites* uses names from historical chronicles (specifically Skylitzes) to create a legendary setting, suggesting the work was composed in the mid-11th century or later. He characterizes the setting as a "composite" of history and romance rather than a literal record of events.</summary>

While the previous page examined 11th-century historical sources like Skylitzes to date the work, this section considers the geopolitical climate of the mid-11th century—specifically the brief window of peace before the Seljuq invasions—as the most likely setting for the poem’s creation.</meta>

## INTRODUCTION

What are we to regard as a sufficient interval in order that his achievements may be represented as legendary? Shall we guess that the *<term>Digenes<note>The hero Digenes Akrites, whose name means "Two-Blood Border Lord," reflecting his mixed Arab and Greek parentage.</note></term>* was written during the reign of <term>Constantine IX Monomachos<note>Byzantine Emperor who ruled from 1042 to 1055; his reign was a period of cultural flourishing but also the beginning of military decline.</note></term> (1042–54) during which ‘almost complete peace reigned on the frontier of <term>Syria<note>The region around Antioch and the northern Levant, which served as a buffer between the Byzantines and the Arab caliphates.</note></term> and <term>Mesopotamia<note>Not the ancient civilization, but the Byzantine "theme" or military province located in what is now eastern Turkey and northern Iraq.</note></term>’? It was not only the first period of complete peace but also the latest. In 1048 the <term>Seljuq Turks<note>A rising power of nomadic Turkic tribes from Central Asia who converted to Islam and began a rapid expansion into the Middle East and Anatolia.</note></term> were already gathering on the <term>Armenian<note>Referring to the borderlands of the Byzantine Empire near the Caucasus, which had recently been annexed by the Empire but were now under threat.</note></term> border.

<summary>This page argues that the epic of Digenes Akrites was likely composed during the mid-11th century reign of Constantine IX Monomachos, a brief period of peace on the eastern frontier just before the arrival of the Seljuq Turks.</summary>

<keywords>Digenes Akrites, Constantine IX Monomachos, Byzantine frontier, Seljuq Turks, dating of the poem, Syria, Mesopotamia</keywords>

There was an Emir of noble birth, exceedingly wealthy,
possessed of wisdom and bravery to the highest degree,
not dark-skinned like the Ethiopians, but fair and lovely,
already in his prime with a comely, curly beard.

<column-break/>

**Goulabioi**. See the scholar Henri Grégoire (Royal Academy of Belgium, *Bulletin of the Class of Letters and Moral and Political Sciences*, 5th series, volume 17, number 12, December 7, 1931, pages 463-493, "The Byzantine Epic and its relationships with the Turkish and Romance epics"), who shows, on page 482, that this is a variant for **Goulamians** (from the Arabic *ghulam*, meaning a youth or slave-soldier). The term *Magoulioi* found in other manuscripts of the poem is a scrambled form of this same word.

Line 48: **adnoumiatas**. See Sophocles’ *Lexicon of the Byzantine Period* under the entry *adnoumion*, derived from the Latin *ad-nomen*, meaning "to enroll by name" or a military muster.

The scholarly footnotes analyze the historical and linguistic origins of his troops, such as the 'ghulam' slave-soldiers and the 'Troglodytes'.</summary>

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO I

Having sped through <term>Charziane</term> <note>A Byzantine administrative district (theme) in eastern Asia Minor.</note>, he reached <term>Cappadocia</term>,
and suddenly fell upon the house of the <term>Strategos</term> <note>A high-ranking Byzantine military governor or general.</note>.
Who has the power to tell of the deeds done there? <margin>60</margin>
For he put to death everyone found there,
took great wealth, plundered the house,
and took captive a most beautiful girl,
the daughter of the general, who was a virgin.
The general happened to be in exile at that time, <margin>65</margin>
and the girl’s brothers were stationed at the <term>akras</term> <note>The frontiers or "edge" territories of the Empire where soldiers defended against raids.</note>;
but the mother, escaping the hands of the <term>gentiles</term> <note>original: "ethnon." In this context, it refers to the non-Christian Arab forces.</note>,
immediately wrote to her sons everything that happened:
the arrival of the heathens, the abduction of the girl,
the separation from their dearest sister, and the multitude of calamities. <margin>70</margin>
And she added these words to her letter with lamentations:
"O most beloved children, take pity on a mother
who has a wretched soul and is about to die;
remember the love of your own sister,
and hasten to free your sister and your mother— <margin>75</margin>
her from bitter slavery, and me from death. <margin>2 v<sup>o</sup>.</margin>
We will give all our possessions for the sake of our dearest one;
do not value your lives above your sister's,
but have mercy on your own sister, my children;
go with haste to her rescue; <margin>80</margin>
otherwise, you shall see your mother dead for the sake of her child,
and you shall receive my curse and your father's curse
if you do not do this as I have charged you."
When they heard this, they groaned from the depths of their hearts,
and the five of them became drenched in tears; <margin>85</margin>
they urged one another to set out with haste,
saying, "Let us go and be slaughtered for her sake."
Once again they mounted their horses and sped on their way,
having with them a few soldiers following;
and neglecting nothing, not even taking their fill of sleep, <margin>90</margin>
in a few short days they reached the <term>phoussata</term> <note>original: "phoussata." A term derived from the Latin "fossatum" (ditch/trench), which evolved to mean a military camp or an entire army.</note>,
at the terrible mountain pass which they call <term>Dyskolon</term> <note>literally "The Difficult One."</note>;
and dismounting from afar, where they found the scouts <note>original: "viglas." Lookout posts or sentries.</note>,

# DIGENES AKRITES

Having sped through <term>Charziane</term> <note>A Byzantine administrative district (theme) in eastern Asia Minor, near the border.</note>, he reached <term>Cappadocia</term>,
and fell with great force upon the residence of the <term>Strategos</term> <note>A high-ranking Byzantine military governor or general.</note>.
Who can describe the deeds that were done there?
For he killed everyone he found, <margin>60</margin>
seized great wealth, plundered the house,
and took captive a most beautiful girl,
the daughter of the general, who was still a maiden.
The general himself happened to be in exile at that time,
and the girl’s brothers were away guarding the frontiers.
The mother, having escaped the hands of the <term>foreigners</term> <note>original: "ethnon." In this context, it refers to the non-Christian Arab forces.</note>,
immediately wrote to her sons about everything that had happened:
the arrival of the foreigners, the abduction of the girl,
the separation from her dearest child, and a multitude of sorrows.
To this she added a letter written with great lamentation: <margin>70</margin>
"O my dear children, take pity on your mother,
whose soul is in misery and who is close to death.
Remember the love you have for your sister;
hurry to free your sister and your mother—
her from captivity, and me from death.
We would give our very existence for her sake.
Do not value your own lives more than your sister’s;
have mercy, my children, on your own sister.
Go out in haste for her rescue,
otherwise you shall see me, your mother, dead for the sake of my child, <margin>80</margin>
and you shall receive your father’s curse and mine
if you do not do as I have proposed."
When they heard these words, they sighed deeply—
all five of them—and were soaked with tears.
Encouraging each other to set out in haste,
they said, "Let us go and be killed for her sake."
With that, they mounted their horses and took to the road,
with a few soldiers following behind them.
Neglecting nothing and taking no time for sleep,
in a few days they reached the <term>encampment</term> <note>original: "fossatum." From the Latin for "ditch," this refers to a fortified army camp.</note> <margin>90</margin>
at the terrifying pass which they call "The Difficult."
Dismounting far off where they found the <term>sentries</term> <note>original: "viglas." These were military watchmen or outposts stationed to guard the roads and mountain passes.</note>,

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO I
->8<-

By means of their written petition,
they were brought before the Emir by his command.
He was sitting upon a high throne,
<margin>95</margin> gilded and formidable, outside the tent;
around him stood crowds of armed men.
And as they drew near, he heard their words;
having prostrated themselves before him three times to the ground,
with tears they spoke to the Emir as follows:
<margin>100</margin> “Emir, servant of God and first among the leaders of <term>Syria</term> <note>At this time, "Syria" referred generally to the Arab-controlled territories bordering the Byzantine Empire.</note>,
may you reach <term>Panormos</term> <note>Modern-day Palermo, Sicily, which was a significant center of Islamic culture during this period.</note> and see the <term>mosque</term> <note>original: "masgidion" — a small mosque or place of prayer.</note>,
<margin>3 r<sup>o</sup>.</margin> that you might worship, O Emir, the hanging stone,
and be deemed worthy to kiss the tomb of the Prophet,
and to hear the consecrated prayer!
<margin>105</margin> You have seized a delightful girl, our own sister;
sell her back to us, servant of God most high,
and in exchange for her we will give you as much wealth as you command.
For her father laments as one who has no other child,
and her mother wishes to die, not being able to look upon her;
<margin>110</margin> and we, possessing an infinite love for her,
have all sworn by the most fearsome oaths
that if we do not bring her back, we shall all be slaughtered.”
Hearing these things, the Emir marvelled at their daring;
truly wishing to learn for certain if they happened to be brave men
<margin>115</margin> (for he understood well the language of the <term>Romans</term> <note>original: "Rhomaion" — the Byzantines referred to themselves as Romans and their language, Greek, as the Roman tongue.</note>),
he answered them calmly, saying these things:
“If you desire to make your sister free,
let one among you whom you consider noble be chosen,
<margin>120</margin> and let us both mount our horses,
and let us engage in <term>single combat</term> <note>A formal duel between two champions to settle a dispute without full-scale battle.</note>, he and I.
And if I should turn him back, I shall have you as my slaves;
but if he should turn me back, then without any further word,
you shall take your sister, suffering no loss at all.”

By means of their written petition,
they were brought before the Emir by his command.
He sat there upon a high throne,
gilded and <term>formidable</term> <note>original: "awful" — in the older sense of inspiring awe or terror.</note>, outside the tent;

<margin>100</margin> “Emir, servant of God and prince of <term>Syria</term> <note>The poem uses "Syria" to refer to the various Arab-controlled territories on the Byzantine frontier.</note>,
may you reach <term>Palermo</term> <note>original: "Panormos" — A major city in Sicily that was under Islamic rule at the time this version of the poem was composed.</note> and see the mosque,

her mother wishes to die, not being able to see her.
<margin>110</margin> We ourselves, possessed of boundless love for her,
have all sworn with fearsome oaths
to be killed if we do not bring her back.”

and in order to learn for certain if they were brave
(for he was well-acquainted with the <term>Roman tongue</term> <note>original: "Rhomaion" — The Byzantines called themselves Romans, and their language (Greek) was the "Roman tongue."</note>),

and let us mount our horses together, he and I,

you shall receive your sister, losing nothing,

# DIGENES AKRITES
## II

“...and the other captives who are found with me.
Otherwise, I will not consent to surrender your sister,
even if you offer all the wealth of <term>Romania</term> <note>original: "Rhomania" — The term the Byzantines used to describe their empire, as they considered themselves the true continuation of the Roman state.</note>.
Go now and consider where your best interest lies.”
Forthwith they all went out, filled with hope;
and so they would not fight over who should be the one to contend,
<margin>130</margin> they decided to cast lots, and thus ended their disagreement.
The lot fell to the youngest, young Constantine,
the twin brother of their sister.
The eldest, preparing him for the fight, gave him counsel,
saying: “In no way, brother, let their shouts terrify you,
nor ever shrink away, nor let their blows appal you;
if you see a naked sword, do not give way,
nor fly from anything even more terrible.
Do not heed your youth, but only your mother’s curse—
whose prayers, supporting you, will ensure you prevail.
<margin>140</margin> God shall not allow us ever to be made slaves.
Go, child, be of good heart, and do not fear at all.”
And standing toward the East, they called upon God:
“O Lord, never allow us to become slaves.”
Having embraced him, they sent him forth, saying:
“May our parents' prayer be your helper.”
He, mounting a black and noble horse,
and having girt on his sword, took up his lance;
he carried his mace in the <term>mace-holder</term> <note>A specialized holster or ring attached to the saddle for carrying a heavy crushing weapon.</note>,
and protected himself on all sides with the sign of the cross.
<margin>150</margin> He spurred his horse and rode out into the plain,
brandishing first the sword and then likewise the lance.
Some of the <term>Saracens</term> <note>A common medieval term for Arabs or Muslims.</note> mocked the youth:
“Look what kind of champion is sent out to fight
him who has won great triumphs in Syria!”
But one of them, a <term>Dilemite</term> <note>original: "Dilemites" — A fierce warrior from the Daylam region of modern-day Iran, known for serving as elite infantry or border guards.</note> borderer,
spoke softly to the Emir a word such as this:

130 <note>original: "λαχνοὺς θεῖναι" — To cast lots. The commentator suggests reading "λαχμούς" (lachmous), referencing Sophocles' Lexicon.</note>
135 <note>original: "μικρόν τι δειλιάσωσι" — "To be a little afraid." The commentator notes this phrasing is awkward and likely a corrupted part of the manuscript.</note>
150 <note>original: "τὸν ἵππον ἐπελάλησεν" — "He addressed the horse." The term "epilalo" was a technical riding term meaning to command or spur a horse, though not necessarily with spoken words. In later literature, it appears in a corrupted form as "ippilalo." See also line 226.</note>

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO I

> “See how skillful the tripping maneuver is,
> the reception of the sword, the turn of the spear;
> all these show both experience and courage;
<margin>160</margin>
> see then that you do not strike the youth carelessly.”

The <term>Emir</term> <note>original: "amyras" — a title for a Muslim military commander or ruler, here referring to the leader of the raiding party.</note> also went out, mounted upon a <term>war-horse</term> <note>original: "pharas" — a loanword from Arabic *faras*, used to describe a high-quality Arabian charger.</note>;
for he was most bold and fearsome to behold,
his armor flashed back the rays of the sun;
he deftly brandished a <term>blue</term> <note>original: "beneton" — a deep blue color, often associated with the "Blue" chariot-racing faction in Byzantium.</note> spear, adorned with gold;
<margin>165</margin>
and everyone gathered together to witness the battle.
The horse played its part delightfully, to the amazement of all;
for drawing its four feet together as one,
as if by a mechanical device, it stood poised there;
at other times it seemed to move with such swift, light steps,
<margin>170</margin>
that it appeared not to walk, but to fly across the ground.
The Emir, rejoicing, laughed aloud,
and immediately spurred his horse and rode out into the plain,
<margin>4 v°.</margin>
shouting like an eagle and hissing like a dragon,
roaring like a lion about to swallow up the youth.
<margin>175</margin>
The boy received his charge straightway and with speed,
and they delivered spear-thrusts until both their lances shattered,
neither being strong enough to unhorse the other;
then drawing their swords and closing the distance,
they hacked at one another for many hours;
<margin>180</margin>
the mountains echoed back, and the hills seemed to thunder;
blood flowed down and covered all that earth;
the horses grew weary, and everyone was seized with amazement;
they were covered in wounds, yet neither side would turn away;
and as the <term>Saracens</term> <note>A term used by medieval Christians to describe Arab or Muslim people.</note> witnessed this extraordinary sight,
<margin>185</margin>
marveling greatly at the eagerness of the youth,
his immense endurance and his noble daring,
they all spoke to the Emir with one voice:

> “Seek a peaceful settlement, and leave off the war,
> the <term>Roman</term> <note>original: "Rhomaios" — the Byzantines called themselves Romans, as they were the continuation of the Roman Empire.</note> is formidable, lest he bring you to a bitter end.”

<margin>190</margin>
And the Emir immediately turned himself toward flight.

‘You see him spurring, and how cleverly,
His sword's parry, the turning of his lance.
All this exhibits skill as well as courage;
<margin>160</margin>See then you meet the child not carelessly.’
Forth came the <term>Emir</term> <note>A title for a high-ranking Muslim commander or ruler.</note> riding upon a horse,
Most bold he was and terrible to view,
His arms were glittering with sunny rays;
The lance he wielded was of blue and gold.
<margin>165</margin>And all came out at once to watch the battle.
The charger amazed all with pretty sporting,
For gathering his four hoofs to one spot,
As if caught in a trap <note>original: "hos en mechane" — literally "as if by a machine," implying the horse moved with the precision of a mechanical device.</note>, he there would stay;
Or again showed so quick and delicate a trot
<margin>170</margin>That he seemed not to tread but skim the ground.
The Emir delighting seemed to smile on him,
Straightway impelled him, rode into the plain,
An eagle shrieking, and a hissing serpent <note>The original Greek "drakon" can mean a large serpent or a dragon.</note>,
A lion roaring to devour the youth.
<margin>175</margin>The other soon and swiftly there received him.
They gave play with their spears, and both were broken,
Strength neither having to unseat the other;
Then having drawn their swords within arm's length <note>original: "esothen cheiras dontes" — literally "giving hands from within," an idiom for engaging in close-quarters combat.</note>
Together smote each other many hours.
<margin>180</margin>The mountains echoed, thunders held the hills,
The blood ran down there over all the ground,
The indignant horses raged, all were amazed;
All wounded <note>original: "oloplegoi" — a compound word meaning "struck all over."</note> were they both, yet neither winning.

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO I
### Page 16

“Yesterday in this very place we slaughtered noble and beautiful women,
<margin>225</margin> because they would not submit to what we told them.”
They spurred their horses <note>original: "elalēsan" — literally 'they spoke to', a common Byzantine idiom for urging horses forward.</note> and went off to the stream;
<margin>5 vº.</margin> there they found many slaughtered women, bathed in blood.
Some were missing hands, others their skulls or feet;
for others, all their limbs were severed and their vitals spilled out,
<margin>230</margin> so that they could not be recognized by anyone at all.
When they witnessed this, a great shock took hold of them;
taking dust from the earth, they sprinkled it over their heads <note>A traditional gesture of extreme grief and mourning found in both ancient Greek and Biblical traditions.</note>,
and they began to wail and offer laments from the heart:
“Which hand shall we cut off for burial? Which head shall we weep over?
<margin>235</margin> Which limb, once recognized, shall we bring back to our mother?
O most beautiful sister, how unjustly you were slaughtered!
O our sweetest soul, how did this happen to you?
How did you set <note>The poet uses the metaphor of a setting sun or star.</note> before your time and extinguish our light?
How were you cut limb from limb by the hands of barbarians?
<margin>240</margin> Why did the hand of the heartless murderer not go numb?
He who did not take pity on your delightful youth,
he who did not show mercy to your beautiful voice?
Truly, a noble soul, because of this worthlessness <note>original: "parà tēn achreiosunēn" — the translator notes a variant reading here; see scholarly notes below.</note>,
you chose death and a disastrous slaughter.
<margin>245</margin> But, O most beautiful sister, O soul and heart,
how shall we distinguish you from the rest of the bodies?
Shall we have even this small bit of comfort?
O most terrible hour and treacherous day,
may you never see the sun, may no light ever rise for you!
<margin>250</margin> May God fill you with darkness, for when our sister
was being mercilessly and unjustly butchered by the lawless!
What message shall we bring to our wretched mother?
<margin>6 rº.</margin> O Sun, why did you envy our beautiful sister?”

“There yesterday we killed some lovely ladies,

231 The Trebizond version <note>A different manuscript tradition of the Digenes Akrites epic.</note> begins with the line corresponding to this one.
234 <term>syngopsomen</term> <note>original: "συγκόψομεν"</note>, ‘join in mourning for’. The middle voice, not the active, would be more usual in this sense. One might struggle to believe that <term>syngoptein</term> <note>original: "συγκόπτειν"</note> could ever have such a meaning, as the ritual of beating the breast (and pouring dust on the head) was largely unknown in England at the time of this translation.

<column-break/>

251 <term>katekopton</term> <note>original: "κατέκοπτον"</note> literally means ‘were cutting up’; but the translator chose to avoid the overly literal rendering here to maintain the poetic flow.
253 <term>to adelphin</term> <note>original: "τὸ ἀδέλφιν"</note>. This is a neuter form of the word for 'sister', whereas the ordinary feminine form is used in lines 250 and 255.

<note>The bottom section contains scholarly commentary in two columns.</note>

**292** The manuscript reads *koufe* which Legrand has emended to *koufer* from TRE ii. 86 and AND 524. But see Grégoire, *in the work cited*, page 492, and *Byzantion*, xii, page 699, who shows that this refers to Kufah, the old capital of southern Mesopotamia, a sacred city which usurpers of the ninth century always began by visiting. (There was also a famous school of

<column-break/>

grammarians there: see Nicholson, *Literary History of the Arabs*, page 189; and *Cambridge Medieval History* volume 4, page 291.)
**295** *as far as Iconium*. Iconium is not ‘as far as’ Amorium. I think it probable that these words are corrupt and conceal the name of Akroinon in Phrygia which was the scene of much fighting in the Arab wars of the early eighth century, and...

# DIGENES AKRITES

Seeing me fortunate in all my wars
<margin>290</margin>They made me ruler over all Syria,

All Syria I subdued and captured Kufah,
<note>original: "Kufah." A historic city in Iraq that was a center of Islamic culture and power.</note>
Little I boast in telling you the truth,
Soon afterwards I wiped out Herakleia;
<note>original: "Hêrakleia." This likely refers to the Byzantine city of Heraclea Cybistra, a site of frequent conflict on the frontier.</note>

<note>original: "Amorion" and "Ikonion." Amorium was a major Byzantine stronghold; Iconium is modern-day Konya.</note>

A woman though has conquered me, most lovely,
Whose beauties burn me and whose tears consume;
<margin>300</margin>Her sighs enflame; I know not what to do.
For her sake, but to know for sure, I tried you;
She never ceases, ever weeps for you.
Now wholly I confess and speak the truth:
If you deign have me as your sister's husband,
<margin>305</margin>For the sweet beauty of your own dear sister
I will become a Christian in Romania.
<term>Romania: The Byzantine name for their empire, literally "the land of the Romans."</term>
And, listen to the truth, by the great Prophet,
<note>The Emir swears by the Prophet Muhammad to emphasize his sincerity before his conversion.</note>
She never kissed me, never spoke to me.
Come then into my tent: see whom you seek.'
<margin>310</margin>They, when they heard these words, were overjoyed
And lifted up the tent and went within.
They found a gold-laid bed, the girl reclining;
Christ, there she lay, and seemed to shine with light;
Her eyes were overwatered with her tears.
<margin>315</margin>Whom seeing, her brothers gravely raised her up,
And each began to kiss her, with surprise,
For unexpected joy coming unhoped

<note>Historical Note: This refers to the location where the Turkish hero Sayyid Battal was killed in the year 740. (See the *Cambridge Medieval History* volume 4, pages 120-121, and Henri Grégoire's edition of *Digenes Akrites*, pages 35-38.) For more on Ikonion, see the note for line 2122.</note>

<note>Linguistic Note on line 306: The phrase <note>original: "νὰ γένωμαι" (na genomai)</note>, meaning "to become." Grammatical constructions using the word "na" are used throughout this poem to indicate the future tense, as seen here. The ancient Greek future tense was falling out of use at this time, but the modern Greek future "tha"—or its original form <note>original: "θέλω νά" (thelo na)</note>, meaning "I want to"—is not yet common in this text. However, see line 348 below <note>original: "ἔχειν θέλομεν" (echein thelomen)</note>, meaning "we shall have," and line 357 <note>original: "ὄψομαι" (opsomai)</note>, "I shall see."</note>

<column-break/>

<note>Linguistic Note on line 315: The word <note>original: "σπουδαίως" (spoudaíos)</note> can be translated as "gravely," "busily," "carefully," or "losing no time." It carries a little of all these meanings at once.</note>

<note>Literary Note on line 317: The maxim [gnome] that "unexpected joy causes tears" is expressed here more fully, more clumsily, and with less poetic grace than in the Trebizond manuscript version (TRE ii. 111, 112). The passage is not helped by the editor/redactor ending lines 317 and 318 with the repetitive phrases <note>original: "παρ' ἐλπίδα" (par' elpida)</note>, meaning "beyond hope," and <note>original: "ἀνελπίστως" (anelpistos)</note>, meaning "unexpectedly." There is no doubt that rather than avoiding such repetitions, the author of this poem deliberately seeks to repeat the same ideas.</note>

All those who found her unexpectedly rejoiced;

For beauty makes even brigands gentle,
And leads enemies to spare youth and loveliness.”

<margin>7 v°.</margin>
<note>This refers to the 7th leaf (verso side) of the Grottaferrata manuscript.</note>

<term>Romania: The name Byzantines used for their empire, "the land of the Romans."</term>

<note>original: "boukina." These were straight military trumpets used for signals in the Byzantine army.</note>

That a most noble maiden, with her delightful charms,
Had destroyed the famous armies of Syria.

# CONCERNING THE BIRTH OF THE AKRITES
<note>Digenes Akrites is the "Twice-Born Border Guard," the hero of this Byzantine epic. This section explains the circumstances of his parents' marriage.</note>

## BOOK TWO

Since they had exchanged oaths to take him as a son-in-law,
the Emir immediately took his young men <note>original: "agourous," referring to his military retinue or "boys"</note>,
and returned to Romania for the sake of his beloved.
When he arrived within the borders of Romania,
<margin>5</margin> he set free all those he held as captives,
giving each of them enough supplies for their journey.
The girl’s brothers wrote everything to their mother:
the discovery of their sister, the Emir’s great love,
and how he had renounced his faith, his kindred, and his homeland.
<margin>10</margin> And they wrote: “O most beloved mother, do not have any grief;
for we shall have a son-in-law who is most excellent and handsome;
so prepare everything necessary for the wedding.”
When she heard these things, she gave thanks to God,
saying, “Glory to You, my Christ, for Your love for mankind;
<margin>15</margin> glory to Your power, the hope of the hopeless,
<margin>folio 8 recto</margin> for You can do all You wish, and nothing is impossible for You;
for You have made this very enemy gentle,
and have rescued my daughter from death.
But, O beloved daughter, light of my eyes,
<margin>20</margin> when shall I see you alive and hear your voice?
Behold, I have prepared your wedding and all that is needed;
will your bridegroom truly be your equal in beauty?
Will he possess the character <term>gnome: the inner nature or disposition of a person</term> of the noble Romans?
I fear, my dear child, that he may be unloving,
<margin>25</margin> or wrathful like a pagan <note>original: "ethnikos," meaning a non-Christian foreigner</note>, and it would not be good for me to live.”
The General’s wife sang these things joyfully.

# THE BIRTH OF THE BORDER GUARD
<note>This title refers to the hero Digenes Akrites, whose name means "Twice-Born Borderer," signifying his dual Arab and Greek heritage.</note>

## BOOK TWO

Since they had vowed that he should wed their sister
The Emir straightway taking his company <note>His "agourous" or military retinue.</note>
<margin>340</margin>Returned for his beloved to Romania <note>The contemporary name for the Byzantine Empire, the land of the Romans.</note>.
And when he came into the Roman country
Began to free all those whom he held captive,
Giving each one provision for the way.
The girl’s brothers wrote all things to their mother,
The finding of their sister, the Emir’s love,
How he denied his faith, his kin and country,
And, ‘O beloved mother, have no grief,
For we shall have a bridegroom fine and fair;
Make ready all things needful for the wedding.’
<margin>350</margin>She having heard these things gave thanks to God,
Saying, ‘My Christ, praise to thy loving-kindness,
Hope of the hopeless, praise be to thy power,
All thy will doing, nothing impossible,
For and this enemy thou hast made tame,
And hast delivered my daughter from death.
But, O my daughter dear, light of my eyes,
When shall I see thee living, hear thy voice?
For lo thy wedding and thy needs prepared,
But shall a groom be found to match thy beauty?
<margin>360</margin>His be the nature of the noble Romans?
I fear, my good child, that he may be loveless,
Fierce as a pagan, and I not care to live.’
Thus in her joy the General’s wife did sing.
Meanwhile the Emir and with him the girl’s brothers

---

*sings* <note>original: "adei"</note> can hardly refer to the doubts
and fears she has just uttered. But
possibly the editor departed from
his original model <note>original: "archetype"</note> and rewrote a speech
which in the original was either a

shining clear,
Last night I lost a girl, a daughter
dear.’

<summary>The Emir enters Byzantine territory and frees his captives as a gesture of goodwill before his marriage, while the girl's mother oscillates between religious gratitude and fear of the Emir's foreign nature. The scholarly notes suggest the mother's speech may have been adapted from traditional Greek folk laments.</summary>

### DIGENES AKRITES GRO II

Both rejoicing, they began their journey with much toil;
and when they drew near to their own house,
<margin>30</margin> a great multitude and many kinsmen came out to meet them,
and then the General's wife followed with great glory.
The infinite joy that occurred then,
who could ever describe it, or to what could it be compared?
For the children embraced their mother with great longing,
<margin>35</margin> and the mother truly rejoiced over her children;
and seeing her son-in-law <note>The Emir, who is now joining the family.</note> truly full of joy,
she sent up thanks to God from her whole heart:
> "Lord Christ," she said, "no one who hopes in You
> has ever failed to obtain their heart's desire."
<margin>40</margin> Having arrived at the house, they celebrated the wedding,
<margin>8 vº.</margin> and they perfected the son-in-law through holy baptism <note>The "perfection" refers to the completion of his conversion to Christianity.</note>;
and that public joy continued to grow;
for the Emir rejoiced, having won his beloved;
for there is no joy greater than the love of the heart;
<margin>45</margin> as much as a lover burns when he meets with failure,
so much does he rejoice when he wins the one he loves.
After their union, the young woman conceived,
and she gave birth to Digenes Basil Akrites <note>The hero’s name "Digenes" means "Twice-Born," referring to his dual Arab and Greek parentage. "Akrites" means "Border-Guard."</note>;
and the Emir's devotion increased even more.
<margin>50</margin> But the Emir’s mother sent a letter from Syria <note>The Emir’s original home in the Arab Caliphate.</note>
full of lamentation, reproach, and blame:
> “You have blinded my eyes and extinguished my light,
> O most beloved child, how could you forget your mother?
> How did you deny your kinsmen, your faith, and your fatherland,
<margin>55</margin> > and become a disgrace to all of Syria?
> We have become an abomination to every man,
> as deniers of the faith and transgressors of the law,
> as those who have not kept the words of the Prophet <note>A reference to Muhammad.</note>.
> What has happened to you, my child? How did you forget them?
<margin>60</margin> > How did you not remember the great deeds of your father?”

Together they gladly took the weary road,
And as they approached their own home,
A great crowd of kinsmen came out to meet them,
Followed by the General’s wife in a grand procession.
<margin>370</margin>Who could describe or imagine the boundless joy that followed?
The children embraced their mother with love;
She was truly a joyful mother of children. <note>This is a reference to Psalm 113:9, "He makes the barren woman to keep house, and to be a joyful mother of children."</note>
And seeing the bridegroom <note>The Emir, who has now joined the family.</note> so happy,
She thanked God from her heart, saying:
"Lord Christ, no one who hopes in You
Has ever failed to find what they desired."
And inside the house, they celebrated the wedding,
And the groom was fulfilled through holy baptism. <note>The "fulfillment" refers to the completion of his conversion from Islam to Christianity.</note>
And so that universal joy increased;
<margin>380</margin>For the Emir rejoiced to possess his love,
And no joy is greater than a deep passion;
For just as a lover burns when he meets with failure,
So much does he rejoice when he wins the one he loves.
After their union, the young woman conceived,
And gave birth to <term>Digenes Basil Akrites</term>; <note>The hero’s name "Digenes" means "born of two races," referring to his dual Arab and Greek heritage. "Akrites" refers to the "Frontier-Guards" who patrolled the edges of the Byzantine Empire.</note>
And the Emir’s love grew stronger still.
But the Emir’s mother sent a letter from Syria <note>Syria here refers to the territories of the Arab Caliphate.</note>
Filled with sorrow, blame, and bitter reproach:
"You have extinguished my light and blinded my eyes;
<margin>390</margin>Dear child, how have you forgotten your mother,
Denied your family, your faith, and your country,
And become a disgrace throughout Syria?
We have become loathsome to every man,
As deniers of the faith and law-breakers
Who failed to keep the words of the Prophet. <note>Referring to the Prophet Muhammad.</note>
What happened, child? How could you forget them?
How could you not remember your father’s deeds?"

<note>Note on line 373: The scholar Émile Legrand suggests reading the Greek word for "beautiful" (*perikalle*) instead of "overjoyed" (*perichare*), following other manuscript versions. However, it may be simpler to read it as the mother-in-law herself being "overjoyed" (*perichares*).</note>

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO II

How many Romans did he slay, how many did he lead into slavery?
Did he not fill the prisons with generals and local rulers?
Did he not raid many themes <note>Administrative provinces of the Byzantine Empire.</note> of Romania <note>The term "Romania" was used by the Byzantines to refer to their own Empire.</note>,
And bring back noble and beautiful captives?
<margin>65</margin> Did he lose his way and become a transgressor like you?
For when the Roman armies surrounded him,
<margin>9 r°.</margin> The generals swore the most terrifying oaths to him:
That he would be honored as a Patrician <note>A high-ranking title in the Byzantine court.</note> by the Emperor,
And become the First Equerry <term>Protostrator</term> <note>A high military position, originally the official in charge of the Emperor's horses.</note>, if he would only throw down his sword.
<margin>70</margin> But he, keeping the commands of the Prophet <note>The Prophet Muhammad.</note>,
Despised that glory and paid no mind to wealth;
And they cut him limb from limb, and took his sword.
But you, facing no such compulsion, have cast everything aside at once:
Your faith, your kin, and even me, your mother.
<margin>75</margin> My brother—your uncle—Mourses Karoes,
Traveled to the coastal city of Smyrna,
He raided Ankara and the city of Abydos,
Africa, Terenta <note>Scholars suggest this refers to Tephrike, a major frontier fortress.</note>, and the Six Villages,
And after conquering these, he returned to Syria;
<margin>80</margin> But you, most wretched one, have made a journey of shame.
While you could have been glorified throughout all Syria,
You lost everything for the love of a "pig-woman" <note>original: "chanzyrissa." This is a derogatory term derived from the Arabic "khinzir" (pig) with a Greek feminine ending, mocking the girl's Christian background.</note>
And you have become cursed in every mosque <term>Masgidion</term>.
For if you do not depart quickly and come to Syria,
<margin>85</margin> The Emirs intend to throw me into the river,
To slaughter your children as the offspring of a rebel father,
To hand your lovely daughters over to others—
They who sigh for you and have no more patience.
Oh, my sweetest child, have mercy on your mother;
<margin>90</margin> Do not bring my old age down to Hades <note>The realm of the dead; the mother uses the Greek term even while speaking from an Islamic context in the poem.</note> with grief,
And do not let your children be slaughtered for no cause;
<margin>9 v°.</margin> Do not disregard the tears of your lovely daughters.

# DIGENES AKRITES

How many Romans did he slay, how many did he enslave?
Did he not fill the prisons with generals and captains?
<margin>400</margin>
Did he not ravage many Roman themes <note>The military and administrative provinces of the Byzantine Empire.</note>,
And bring back many noble beauties as captives?
Was he led, like you, toward transgression?
For when the Roman armies surrounded him,
The generals swore terrifying oaths to him
That the Emperor would make him a patrician <note>A high-ranking title in the Byzantine court.</note>,
The Master of Horse <term>Protostrator</term>, if he would only throw down his sword.
But he, keeping the commandments of the Prophet <note>The Prophet Muhammad.</note>,
Despised all such honor and gave no heed to wealth;
They cut him limb from limb and took his sword.
<margin>410</margin>
But you, facing no such compulsion, have given up everything:
Your faith, your kinsmen, and even me, your mother.
My brother and your uncle, Mourses Karoes,
Led his army to Smyrna by the sea,
He ravaged Ankara, the city of Abydos,
Tephrike, Taranta, and the Six Towns,
And from these victories he returned to Syria.
But you have made a most unblessed expedition;
When you might have had the praise of all Syria,
You have instead forfeited everything for the love of a swine-eater <note>A derogatory term for a Christian, referencing the consumption of pork forbidden in Islam.</note>
<margin>420</margin>
And have become accursed in every mosque.
If you do not return soon to Syria,
The emirs will intend to drown me,
To kill your children whose father was a renegade,
And give your beautiful daughters to other men;
They weep for you and cannot endure this.
O my sweetest child, have pity on your mother.
Do not bring my old age to the grave in sorrow,
Do not let your children be unjustly slain,
Nor ignore the tears of your beautiful daughters.

“I have letters from my mother in Syria;
she is in danger because of me, and I must go.

<margin>470</margin>
“do not worry about that; I will go wherever you command.”
But God, intending to perform strange wonders here,
brought the secret plan to light through a dream;
for the girl’s youngest brother had a vision in a dream,
and waking from sleep and calling his brothers,
he told them the dream he had seen during the night.
“I was sitting,” he said, “up in the house,
and watching hawks over the Hollow Stone,
and a warlike falcon that was chasing a dove;
and following it as he brought the chase to its end,
<margin>480</margin>

where the bridegroom lives with our own sister;

my soul was in great distress, and I awoke.”

“Hawks, as they say, represent predatory men,
and the falcon that you saw—I fear our brother-in-law
may do some injury to the dove, our sister.
But let us go to the place where you saw the dream,
and where you were watching the hawks in flight.”
<margin>490</margin>
Mounting at once, they rode off to the Stone,
and found the Arabians there with their horses;
amazed at the sight, they wondered at the vision,
saying, “Welcome, companions of our brother-in-law,
why are you camped here, so far from our house?”
And they, unable to deny it, confessed the truth.

The scholar Dawkins suggests a connection with the Turkish name *köroğlu* <note>original: "köroğlu", meaning 'son of the blind man'</note>, a hero of folk tales. I believe it may be possible to trace a connection with the ancient Greek word *kirkos* <note>original: "κίρκος", referring to a kind of hawk or falcon</note>, as seen in the *Iliad* (Book 22, line 139) and the *Odyssey* (Book 13, line 87); however, more evidence is

<column-break/>

required. Kalonaros (commenting on manuscript AND 2943) suggests that it is an affectionate, shortened form of *kyr* or *kyres* <note>original: "κύρ" or "κύρης", titles meaning 'lord' or 'sir'</note>.

And what was certain was declared; nothing remained hidden.
(For unexpected fear brings the truth to light,
While fear that is expected often produces excuses.)
They immediately seized the guards and went to the groom,
<margin>500</margin> Reviling him as a man with evil intentions;
Thereupon the youngest brother, being especially bold,
Said: "This was your plan; do not deny it.
Know this, <term>Saracen</term> <note>original: "Σαρακηνέ"; a term used by the Byzantines for Arabs or Muslims</note>, you shall not see <term>Syria</term> again.

  Hearing this, the <term>Emir</term> <note>A title for a Muslim military commander or local ruler</note>, seeing those who accused him,
Was unable to reply and remained completely silent,
Being filled with shame, fear, and grief;
<margin>510</margin> Ashamed to be detected, and frightened because he was a stranger,
Pained at the thought of being parted from his beloved,
Not knowing what to do, he went to the girl;
In her alone he hoped to find some comfort,
Not knowing what God had revealed to her in a dream.
"Why did you do this?" he cried out in tears,
"Is this your love? Is this what you promised me?
Did I not place all my trust in you?
Did you not gladly agree to come with me?
Did I use force or do you any violence?
<margin>520</margin> Rather, you insisted that I should take you with me
And that we should rejoice together on the way and back again;
But with no fear of God before your eyes,
You urged your brothers to murder me.
Do you not remember what I first did for you?
I took you captive, yet I honored you.
I wanted you as my slave, but I became yours;
I renounced my parents and my faith for you,
And for your love I came to <term>Romania</term> <note>The "land of the Romans," referring to the Byzantine Empire</note>.

And all the little nobles <note>original: "ἀρχοντόπουλα"; refers to the sons of the nobility or young aristocrats</note> shall revile you,
Because you could not keep your husband’s secret,
But like <term>Delilah</term> <note>The biblical figure who betrayed the hero Samson by revealing the secret of his strength</note> gave him to be slain.’
   So spoke the <term>Emir</term> <note>A title for a high-ranking Muslim leader, here used for the converted father of the hero Digenes</note> all weeping to the girl,
For he supposed she had revealed his plan—
<margin>540</margin> (And love being dishonoured brings on chiding).
The girl when that she heard these words was dumb,
In no wise could she utter any word,
But many hours remained in silent gloom—
(For who is guilty has excuses ready,
Who guiltless silent, with no word to say).
She scarcely at length coming to herself
‘Why vainly scold me,’ she exclaimed with tears,
‘Why, husband, charge the one who loves you so?
Not mine, nor ever be, to tell your plan,
<margin>550</margin> For if I did, earth swallow me alive,
That I be made a warning to the world
As one who has made known her husband’s secrets.’
And seeing the Emir’s increasing grief,
Who nearly was become insane with sorrow—
(For the excess of sorrow madness breeds,
And many therefrom venture what is lawless)—
He with his sword she feared might kill himself,
She went out to her brothers tearing her hair.
‘Sweetest brothers, why are you vainly vexing
<margin>560</margin> One who has done no wrong? Here he is dying,
Here he will kill himself in frenzy-stroke.
Brothers, in God’s name, let not a guest be wronged
Who for my sake denied his kin and faith.

---

635 <note>original Greek: "μέχρι τριῶν μιλλίων"</note> up to three miles. See also lines 2272 and 1879. What was the significance of the three-mile limit? It was still apparently observed in Persia in recent times in connection with the <term>Istiqbal</term> <note>A traditional Persian ceremony where a guest is formally met or escorted by a delegation.</note> or ceremonial meeting of distinguished visitors. See James Morier’s *Journey to Persia*, p. 97: ‘An *Istiqbal* of fifty horsemen of our <term>Mihmandar’s</term> <note>A designated official host or guide for foreign dignitaries in Persia.</note> tribe met us about three miles from our encampment. . . . Then came two of the principal merchants of Shiraz. . . . They how-
<column-break/>
ever incurred the Envoy’s displeasure by not dismounting from their horses, a form always observed in Persia by those of lower rank when they meet a superior.’ (Quoted in Atkinson, *Shah Nameh*, p. 522.) See also the fourth-century <term>Peregrinatio ad Loca Sancta</term> <note>Latin: "Pilgrimage to the Holy Places." This travelogue is attributed to a woman named Egeria (or Silvia).</note> of the nun Sylvia, (quoted in Muller and Taylor, p. 127) where the Persians at Edessa advance <note>original Latin: "ita ut usque tertium miliarium de civitate essent," meaning "so that they were as far as the third mile from the city."</note>.

<summary>The Emir bids farewell to his kinsmen after a three-mile ceremonial escort, a distance the commentary identifies as a long-standing tradition of diplomatic etiquette in the Near East. The scholarly notes compare this Byzantine practice to historical Persian customs and early Christian pilgrimage accounts.</summary>
<keywords>Emir, departure, three-mile limit, Istiqbal, Persian etiquette, Edessa, James Morier, ceremonial escort</keywords>

# THE RETURN FROM SYRIA WITH HIS MOTHER

<margin>14 r°.</margin>Thus every lover is a slave to **Love** <note>original: "ἔρωτος" (Eros). The poet personifies Love here as a powerful, almost tyrannical force.</note>;
for he is a judge who tortures the hearts
of those who do not strictly follow the paths of affection;
he sends his bolts with good aim and shoots at hearts,
<margin>5</margin>and stands with fire, setting the mind aflame.
And everyone who is possessed by him cannot escape,
whether he is among the noble or the wealthiest,
for rising up <note>The translator suggests this implies "hovering" or "flying over" the victim.</note> he quickly overtakes them.
In this way, that marvelous **Emir** <term>Emir: an Arabic title for a high-ranking noble or military commander</term>

forgetting his kinsmen, his parents, and his fatherland;
he even denied his faith for the love of a girl
who was truly delightful and most noble.

<margin>15</margin>and dwelt in **Romania** <note>original: "‘Ρωμανίαν". To the Byzantines, this was the land of the Romans, referring to the heart of the Byzantine Empire.</note> for the sake of his beloved.

# THE RETURN FROM SYRIA AND HIS MOTHER’S ARRIVAL

## BOOK THREE

Thus every lover is a slave to **Love**,
for he acts as a judge who tortures the hearts
<margin>640</margin>of those who do not strictly follow love's paths;
he aims his arrows well and shoots at hearts,
and stands with fire to set the mind aflame.
Whoever is caught by him can no longer escape,
even if they are famous or very wealthy,
for he rises up <note>The poet suggests Love hovers above like a bird of prey.</note> and quickly captures them.
And so it happened that this wonderful **Emir** <term>Emir: A title for a high-ranking Muslim commander or prince.</term>,
despising his own fame and great authority,
forgot his family, his parents, and his country;
<margin>650</margin>he even denied his faith for the love of a girl,
one who was truly beautiful and very noble.
This former enemy was seen to be a slave of Love;
for the sake of his beloved, he lived in **Romania** <note>original: "Romany" or "Ρωμανία". This refers to the Byzantine Empire, the land of the "Romans."</note>.
But he received a letter from his mother in Syria,
and resolved to go away, fearing her curse—
(for it is righteous not to anger one's parents).
When a council was held and vows were made between them,
everyone sent him on his way with joy.
Then he began a song to comfort the girl:
> “Young men, be strong! Horses, do not be idle!
<margin>660</margin>> Hurry by day, and do not sleep at night!
> Think nothing of the rains, the snows, and the frosts,
> so that I am not late for the appointed hour,
> and return to scolding, and care no more to live.”

***

The word for ‘to take’ <note>original: "παίρνω" (pairno)</note> is shown by the Andros manuscript, Book 1, line 185: “and if he flees, the swift one with wings catches him.” Note that the first eight lines of this third book are the foundation of the elaborate description of the **God of Love** in the Andros version, Book 1; also note that the first 37 lines of this third
<column-break/>
book are a repetition rather than a summary of the end of the previous book.
Lines 653–657: This part of the summary can be traced in the Trebizond manuscript, lines 451–456.
Line 662: The phrase “at all” <note>original: "τὸ σύνολον" (to synolon)</note> was omitted in the main translation.

<summary>Book Three begins with a philosophical reflection on the absolute power of Love (Eros) over all men, regardless of rank, using the Emir's conversion and devotion to his Byzantine bride as the primary example. The Emir begins his journey to Syria, spurred by a letter from his mother and the fear of her maternal curse.</summary>

# THE RETURN FROM SYRIA AND HIS MOTHER’S ARRIVAL

## BOOK THREE

Then, having said, “Farewell, kinsmen and friends,”
<margin>14 v⁰. 30</margin>and having embraced everyone, he asked them to pray for him.
And they immediately did so, with one voice:
> “May the God who loves mankind grant you a successful journey,
> and may we be deemed worthy to see you again soon.”
From there, everyone returned to their homes,
<margin>35</margin>gloomy and downcast, as if they had suffered something terrible.
For such is the separation of all those who love:
because it burns the soul and conquers the heart,
a complete separation disturbs even one's very thoughts.
But the **Emir** <term>Emir: A title for a high-ranking Muslim commander or prince.</term> took to the road with great haste;
<margin>40</margin>every day he sent letters to his beloved:
> “Do not be grieved, I pray, but rather pray for this journey.”
Out of longing, he pleaded with his own **agouroi** <term>Agouroi: Young soldiers, squires, or attendants who served the nobility.</term>:
> “Lords,” he said, “young men, my friends and brothers,
> stay awake for my sake and endure this toil,
<margin>45</margin>> for you made pacts and many promises,
> which you swore, saying you would die for me;
> but this is not unto death—this labor is for love.
> For my soul is on fire and my heart is burning,
> when I consider the immense distance of this road.
<margin>50</margin>> When shall we cross the fearsome plains, young men,
> and the fearsome mountains and the narrow, dangerous passes,
<margin>15 r⁰.</margin>> and when shall I behold **Rachab** <note>original: "Ῥαχὰβ". Likely referring to Rahba, a fortress town on the Euphrates in Syria.</note> and see my mother?
> And when shall I cross all these lands once more,
> to return to the regions of beautiful **Romania** <note>original: "Ῥωμανία". The Byzantine Empire.</note>,
<margin>55</margin>> and behold my beautiful **partridge** <note>The poet uses "partridge" as a term of endearment for the Emir's wife.</note>,
> and that most noble flower, my wonderful son?
> Who will give me wings to fly, my dearest,
> and to rest for an hour in your embrace?
How much will she herself sigh, lying awake for me,
<margin>60</margin>and watching the roads every single day?
For many anxieties are natural to those who are loved,
with constant worries, dangers, and fears.
But, O good young men, my noble attendants,
shake off sleep and all laziness!”

# THE RETURN FROM SYRIA AND HIS MOTHER’S ARRIVAL

## BOOK THREE

Then, having said, “Farewell, kinsmen and friends,”
and having embraced everyone, he asked them to pray for him.
They immediately did so with one accord:
->“May the God of compassion grant you a successful journey,<-
->and may we be permitted to see you again soon.”<-
From there, everyone returned toward the house,
<margin>670</margin>feeling sorrowful and dejected, as if they were suffering a great wound—
(For such is the parting of all those who love;
their souls are set on fire and their hearts are overcome,
as their very thoughts are troubled by the total separation).
The **Emir** <term>Emir: A high-ranking title for a Muslim military commander or prince; in this context, the father of the hero Digenes Akrites.</term> set out in great haste upon the road,
and every day he sent letters back to his beloved:
“Do not be grieved, I beg you, but rather pray for me.”
He pleaded with his own companions out of deep affection:
“Princes,” he said, “companions, friends, and brothers,
<margin>680</margin>stay awake now for my sake and endure this hardship,
for you undertook a strict discipline and many promises,
swearing that you would be willing to die for me;
but this task is not a matter of death—this labor is for love.
My soul is flaming and my heart is burning
when I consider the boundless distance of this road.
When shall we cross the fearsome plains, companions,
the fearsome mountains and the terrifying passes,
and when shall I behold **Rahab** <note>original: "τὴν Ῥαχάβ". This likely refers to the city of Edessa (modern-day Şanlıurfa), an important city in northern Mesopotamia.</note> and see my mother?
When shall I traverse all these roads back again
to reach the regions of beautiful **Romania** <term>Romania: The name used by the inhabitants of the Byzantine Empire to refer to their own territory (the land of the Romans).</term>,
<margin>690</margin>and behold my beautiful one, my pet <note>original: "πέρδικαν τὴν ὡραίαν", literally "the beautiful partridge." In Byzantine poetry, the partridge was a common and tender metaphor for a beautiful woman.</note>,
and that most noble flower, my excellent son?
Who will provide me with wings and flight, my dearest,
to give me just one hour’s rest in your arms?
How often will she also sigh, lying awake for me,
and watching the roads every day for my return?
(For many anxieties arise for one who is loved—
endless worries, fears, and dangers.)
But, O good young men, my noble companions,
now shake off sleep and all laziness.”

# THE RETURN FROM SYRIA AND HIS MOTHER’S ARRIVAL

## BOOK THREE

> “As soon as we reach the castle of **Rachab** <note>original: ‘Ῥαχὰβ’. As noted previously, this refers to Edessa.</note>,
> then, having settled things there, let us return again to **Romania** <note>original: ‘Ῥωμανίαν’. The medieval Greek term for the Byzantine Empire, the ‘Land of the Romans.’</note>;
<margin>65</margin> > for there you were often rescued from dangers because of me.
> And, leaving aside many things, I will remind you of one,
> which happened recently at **Mellokopia** <note>original: ‘Μελλοκοπία’. Likely modern Derinkuyu in Cappadocia, known for its underground cities.</note>,
> when the generals surrounded us all at once
<margin>70</margin> > and set their armies around us like a wall,
> and you were led into the despair of death,
> shut in from within like a grave,
> none of you hoping to escape from that place.
> But I spurred my horse and entered their midst;
<margin>75</margin> > how many I sent to **Hades** <note>original: ‘ᾅδην’. The realm of the dead.</note> even you cannot ignore.
> Alone, I turned them all back and made them fugitives,
> and we were saved unharmed along with our captives.
<margin>15 vº.</margin> > But now this is not a war; this is a labor of love,
> and in this I ask you to be my helpers.”

These things and many more the **Emir** <term>Emir: An aristocratic title for a Muslim leader, here Digenes' father.</term> spoke to those with him
<margin>80</margin> on the road, with a heart full of pain;
for Love so inflames those subject to him,
that they despise all other things to follow his command.
And upon hearing this, the **young warriors** <term>young warriors: The Greek ‘ἄγουροι’ (agouroi) refers to the Emir’s youthful soldiers or squires.</term> said at once:

> “Wherever you wish, my master, let your camps be pitched,
<margin>85</margin> > for you shall find no cause for laziness in us.”

And it was a fearsome wonder to see, though not unbelievable to any
(for Love himself was the one serving and helping in all things),
for they covered **three days' journey** <note>original: ‘τρεῖς μονὰς’. A ‘moné’ was a standard day’s march or station.</note> in a single day.
Whenever they reached uninhabited mountain passes,
<margin>90</margin> he would ride around them, guarding his men.
Once, while traveling through a most terrible pass,
they found a fearsome lion holding a hind;
as the young warriors looked upon it, they were
immediately terrified and all ran up the mountain.
<margin>95</margin> Feeling aggrieved, the Emir said to the lion:

> “How did you dare this, most terrible beast,
> to block the path of love’s friendship?
> But I shall give you the reward you deserve.”

With his staff he struck the beast full in its middle,
<margin>100</margin> and immediately it lay still, stretched out upon the earth.

# THE ADVENTURE IN THE MOUNTAIN PASSES

## BOOK THREE

<margin>700</margin> “That we may quickly reach the castle of **Rahab** <note>original: 'Ῥαχὰβ'. A city often identified as Edessa, a key stronghold in northern Mesopotamia.</note>,
and having done so, return once more to **Romania** <note>original: 'Ῥωμανία'. The medieval Greek name for the Byzantine Empire, the land of the 'Romans.'</note>.
Often you have been saved from great dangers through my actions;
leaving aside many instances, I will recall one for you
which happened recently at **Mellokopia** <note>original: 'Μελλοκοπία'. Located in Cappadocia, likely modern-day Derinkuyu, known for its strategic importance.</note>,
when the generals surrounded us on all sides,
and their armies stood around us like a high wall,
and you were all driven to the very despair of death,
shut up within their ranks as if in a tomb,
none of you hoping to escape from that place alive.
<margin>710</margin> But I spurred onward <note>original: 'ἐπελάλησα' (epelalesa). The poet uses the verb for 'spurring' here without explicitly mentioning the 'horse' (hippon), though it is implied.</note> and entered their midst;
you know well how many I sent to **Hades** <note>original: 'ᾅδην'. The ancient Greek name for the underworld or the realm of death.</note>.
Alone, I beat them back and turned them all to flight,
and we were saved, unhurt, along with our prizes of war.
But now this is not a labor of war, but of love,
and in this, I pray you will be my companions.”
The **Emir** <term>Emir: A high-ranking title for a Muslim leader or commander, used here for Digenes' father.</term> spoke these things and many others
to those traveling with him along the road, his heart aching with longing.
(For Love so inflames those who are his subjects
that they despise everything else to acknowledge him.)
<margin>720</margin> And upon hearing him, his young warriors said at once:
“Wherever you wish, master, to set your encampments,
you shall find no cause for delay in us.”
It was a terrifying wonder, which some may doubt—
(for Love was working for them and alongside them all)—
that they covered three stages of the journey every single day.
And when he reached the uninhabited mountain passes,
he would ride around the company to guard them;
once, as they traveled through a fearsome pass,
they came upon a fierce lion holding a doe.
<margin>730</margin> When they saw the beast, the Emir's companions
were struck with such fright that they ran up into the hills;
but the Emir, grieving his own heart's trial, said to the lion:
“How did you dare, you most dreadful beast,
to stand across the road of a man in passionate love?
I shall give you the recompense you deserve.”
He struck the lion in the middle of its body with his staff,
and immediately the creature lay stretched out, dead upon the ground <note>In line 728, the text should be read as 'in one' (original: 'ἐν μίᾳ'), likely referring to the group traveling together or the speed of their movement.</note>.

<margin>folio 16 verso, line 130</margin> and they did not wish to be parted from him at all.

and the mother of the Emir began to speak in this way:

---

### Commentary on the Text
Regarding the special **red boots** of the Byzantine emperors. These three words *on foot with boots* <note>original: 'πεζὸς μετὰ τζαγγίων'</note> occur in the poems of **Ptochoprodromos** <note>A 12th-century writer known for satirical "begging poems" written in the vernacular language.</note> (see Maurofrydes, *Selection of Monuments* <note>original: 'Ἐκλογὴ Μνημείων'</note> (Athens, 1866), p. 39, line 67; compare Legrand, *Library of Vulgar Greek* <note>original: 'Bibl. Gr. Vulg.'</note>, vol. i (Paris, 1880), p. 78, line 69), where, however, the boots are certainly not a mark of honor.

<column-break/>

**750-60** In the corresponding passage found in the Trebizond and Andros manuscripts, there is a quotation from the *Iliad*, Book 2, line 489.

**761** Compare with the text above, lines 317 and following.

**764** *truly with children* <note>original: 'ἀληθῶς μετὰ τέκνων'</note>. This seems to be an implied and rather inappropriate reference to the quotation from the **Psalms** mentioned earlier in line 372; otherwise, the insertion of the word "truly" is pointless.

The accompanying scholarly notes discuss the significance of his footwear and literary parallels in the Iliad and the Psalms.</summary>

The mother refers to a king of the Assyrians, likely the Biblical Naaman, though the text blends various traditions.</note>

> but you became a hinderer of your own fortune,

> while I did not yet partake of the light, I truly honored
> things that are indeed worthy of darkness and utter destruction;
> but when God in the highest was well pleased,”

769 "light of my eyes." Note that
*light* (phōs) means not only the state of being

*drunkenness* (methe), 'being drunk' or 'that which makes one drunk'; *step/dais* (bema), 'a pace' or 'a platform';
*good/beautiful* (kalos), 'good' or 'beautiful'; *earnest/worthy* (spoudaios), 'studious' or 'deserving of study.'

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Emir’s return to his homeland. Having been moved by his love for a Byzantine woman and his experiences in "Romania," he is now confronted by his mother. She uses the shared religious and cultural wonders of their own land to guilt him for his long absence and his apparent abandonment of his heritage.</meta>

# DIGENES AKRITES

‘O sweetest child of mine, light of my eyes,
<margin>770</margin>
And consolation of my soul in age,
Pleasant delight and gladness of my heart,
Say why you tarried, child, in **Romania**; <note>original: 'Ῥωμανία'. The medieval Greek term for the Byzantine Empire, the "Land of the Romans."</note>
Not seeing you, I would not see light at all,
Nor sun shining, nor in the world to live.
Do strange wonders happen in Romania
Such as are done, child, at the **Prophet's tomb**, <note>A reference to the tomb of Muhammad. The mother is appealing to their Islamic faith to lure him back.</note>
Where you went with me going to my prayers?
Saw you a wonder strange how in the night,
Without a light, came radiance from on high,
<margin>780</margin>
Filled the whole house with light unspeakable?
Did you see bears and lions, wolves with sheep,
And many kinds of beasts together feeding,
Not hurting one another, not at all,
But all there waiting till the final prayer,
Then how bending the knee they straight retired?
Did you see greater wonder in Romania?
Is there not with us **Naaman's kerchief**, <term>Naaman’s Kerchief: Also known as the Mandylion, a miraculous cloth. Naaman was an ancient Syrian commander cured of leprosy.</term>
Who was a king among the Assyrians,
Thought worthy of wonders for his host of virtues?
<margin>790</margin>
How did you come, child, to transgress all this,
And **power** <note>original: 'ἀρχῆς', meaning authority or sovereign rule.</note> did despise and greatest fame?
They all believed that you would conquer Egypt,
But you turned obstacle of your own fortune,
And gave up everything for a **Roman girl**.’ <note>The Byzantine woman for whom the Emir left his home; she is the mother of the hero Digenes Akrites.</note>
Her wishing still to speak more on this wise
The young man stopped and thus said to his mother:
‘All this, O mother, I have knowledge of.
Before I shared the light, I truly honoured
Things really worthy of darkness and all destruction.
<margin>800</margin>
But when God in the highest did think good
