# DIGENES AKRITES GRO III

Having said these things, the **Emir** <term>Emir: A title of nobility or high rank in the Muslim world; here it refers to the father of the hero Digenes Akrites.</term>, having opened up a path to the blameless faith, spoke to his mother thus:
<margin>200</margin>
> “I, mother, am returning once more to **Romania** <note>original: 'Ῥωμανίαν'. In the middle ages, this term referred to the Byzantine Empire, the land of the 'Romans,' rather than the modern nation of Romania.</note>, 
> confirming my faith in the **Holy Trinity**,
> for the whole world is not worth a single soul;
<margin>205</margin>
> for even if we gain all things, but lose our soul,
> there is absolutely no benefit in that hour
> when God shall come from heaven to judge the world,
> and shall set everyone before Him to give an account;
> when we shall hear a voice saying to depart 
<margin>18 r°. 210</margin>
> into the accursed **outer fire** <note>A reference to the 'outer darkness' and fire mentioned in the Gospel of Matthew 25:41.</note>,
> to remain forever with the devil,
> as we have become disobedient to His commands.
> But those who believe in Christ, as is right,
> and have kept His venerable commandments,
<margin>215</margin>
> shall shine like the sun in that hour,
> and shall hear the voice of the Good Master:
> ‘Come, inherit, you blessed of my Father,
> the kingdom of heaven which I have prepared for you.’
> And these shall go into eternal life,
<margin>220</margin>
> for He is a just judge, rewarding everyone according to their worth.
> And if you wish, my mother, to be worthy of life,
> and to be redeemed from the fire and eternal darkness,
> flee from idle error and contrived myths,
> and acknowledge God in three persons,
<margin>225</margin>
> united without confusion in one **substance** <note>original: 'ὑποστάσει' (hypostasis). In this poetic context, the Emir is emphasizing the unity of the Trinity. While formal theology usually speaks of one 'essence' and three 'hypostases' (persons), the poem uses the term to stress that the three persons are one God.</note>;
> be persuaded by your son and come with me,
> and I shall become your father in the **Holy Spirit**,
> and I shall receive you as you are baptized in the **rebirth** <term>rebirth: referring to the 'laver of regeneration' or the spiritual new birth of Christian baptism.</term>.”

Such things the Emir spoke; as for his mother,
<margin>230</margin>
she did not reject her son’s most excellent counsel,
but, like good soil receiving the seed,
she immediately brought forth fruit, saying these things:
> “I believe, child, through you in God in Trinity,
> and with you I shall go happily to Romania.”

### 58

"...baptized for the forgiveness of my many sins,
<margin>18 vº.</margin> expressing my gratitude for being enlightened through you.” <margin>235</margin>
Likewise, the relatives who happened to be there,
and the others who had come with her—a great multitude—
cried out with one voice, confessing Christ:
“For we are all coming with you to **Romania** <note>original: 'Ῥωμανίαν'. The Byzantine Empire; the inhabitants called themselves 'Romans' (Rhomaioi).</note>,
and through baptism may we find eternal life!” <margin>240</margin>
And the **Emir** <term>Emir: A high-ranking Muslim leader; here, the father of the hero Digenes.</term> marveled at their eagerness:
“Glory to You,” he said, “the only God who loves mankind,
who does not desire the death of a sinner at all,
but mercifully waits for their return to You,
so that You may make everyone a sharer in Your kingdom.” <margin>245</margin>
Then, taking their infinite wealth with them,
they all departed together for the lands of Romania.
When they reached the regions of **Cappadocia** <note>A region in central Asia Minor (modern Turkey), often the setting for Byzantine epic adventures.</note>,
the Emir consulted his **young men** <term>young men: original 'ἀγούρους' (agouroi), referring to his personal retinue or soldiers.</term>:
“A thought has come to me, O good soldiers, <margin>250</margin>
to go on ahead myself and give the **good news** <term>good news: original 'συγχαρίκια' (syncharikia), a traditional reward given to a messenger of glad tidings.</term>;
for if another arrives first, I shall surely be accused
of being sluggish and lazy by my beloved.”
They replied that this was a good thing to do,
for it is worthy to fulfill the needs of romantic love. <margin>255</margin>
Having devised this plan well,
he immediately changed his clothing, putting on Byzantine dress:
a marvelous **surcoat** <term>surcoat: original 'ἐπιλούρικον' (epilourikon), a garment worn over armor or as a fine tunic.</term>, sprinkled with gold,

<margin>19 rº.</margin> and a white head-wrap with gold lettering, of great value; <margin>260</margin>
he mounted a **chestnut** <note>original: 'βάδεαν'. A dark reddish-brown color.</note> mule with a star on its forehead.
Taking three of his own young men with him,
and—as the saying goes—flying, he reached the house,
and immediately let out a voice full of joy:
“My all-delightful dove, receive your hawk, <margin>265</margin>
and comfort him after his time in foreign lands!”
And at this voice, the **servant girls** <note>original: 'βάγιαι'. Nurses or female attendants.</note> looked out from above.

> “Rejoice, rejoice, mistress; our lord has arrived!”
But she considered this an unbelievable thing

> When they beheld him, they spoke thus to their mistress:
> “Rejoice, madam, rejoice, our master has come.”

> And having wound her arms about his neck
> She hung there speechless, nor did she let fall her tears.
> Likewise the **Emir** <term>Emir: (ἀμηρᾶς) A high-ranking title for a Muslim leader; here, the father of the hero Digenes.</term> became as one possessed,
> Clasping the girl, holding her to his breast,
> So they remained entwined for many hours;
> And had not the General’s wife thrown water on them
> <margin>920</margin> They would have fallen straight to the ground in a faint.
> (Love beyond measure often breeds such things,
> And surpassing joy leads on to death,
> Even as they too were nearly suffering it.)
> And with difficulty were they able to sever them;
> For the Emir was kissing the girl’s eyes,
> Embracing her, and asking with delight:
> “How are you, my sweet light, my pretty lamb,
> How are you, dearest soul, my consolation,
> Most pretty dove, and my most lovely tree
> <margin>930</margin> With your own flower, my beloved son?”
> The girl, her love passionately renewing,
> To the Emir spoke sweetly in this way:
> “Welcome, my hope, refreshment of my life,
> My chief supporter, and my soul’s delight,
> All is well with us by the power of God,
> Who has deemed us worthy to behold each other.”

***

<columns>2</columns>
“Lamb” is used here instead of “heifer.” The translator has also shied away from using “partridge” and “turkey” in similar contexts. This term of endearment is not found in any of the other versions; but compare the Byzantine street-song of 600 A.D. (*Krumbacher*, p. 792) <note>original: "εὐρῆκε τὴν δαμάλιδα ἁπαλὴν καὶ τρυφεράν"</note> “he found the heifer soft and tender”; and the *Palatine Anthology* v. 292.

<column-break/>

10 <note>original: "γλυκερὴν δάμαλιν"</note> “sweet heifer”; also below in line 1846.
931 <note>original: "ἐκ πόθου"</note> “from desire” or “longing” again—interpreted as “eagerly” or “passionately”—or perhaps “to one’s heart’s content”?
933 <note>original: "καλῶς ἦλθες"</note> “Welcome.” [literally: “Well have you come”]. The quite modern formula of welcome.
936 <note>original: "ἡμᾶς . . . ἀλλήλους"</note> “us . . . each other.” Correctly used; see note above for line 783.

The accompanying notes explain the specific Byzantine terms of endearment, such as "heifer" and "lamb," used to describe the beloved.</summary>

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO III
## Page 62

<margin>300</margin> “Tell me also, my lord, how things are with you.”
“All is well,” he replied, “by the grace of my Christ,
who enlightened the hearts of my mother and my kin
and guided them toward the light of the knowledge of God;
you shall see them arriving here in a little while.”
<margin>305</margin> Then, taking his child into his arms,
he spoke these words from the depths of his heart:
“When, my fine young hawk, will you spread your wings,
to hunt the partridge and subdue the bandits?”
Thus the **Emir** <note>original: "amiras" — the father of Digenes, a converted Muslim leader.</note> spoke to the child.
<margin>310</margin> When everyone learned of his arrival,
they ran to the houses to deliver the <term>good-news gifts</term><note>original: "sygcharikia" — rewards or tips traditionally given to someone who brings happy tidings.</note>.
And there was great joy, impossible to describe;
forming circles, they began to dance.
Amidst this joy, another joy followed,
<margin>315</margin> for someone arrived announcing the arrival of his mother;
and one could see everyone together, both men and women,
going out to meet her alongside the General’s wife,
so many that one could not easily count them.
Truly, a wondrous miracle, the work of righteous love;
<margin>320</margin> who would not be struck by this? Who would not marvel even more,
and learn accurately the powers of love—
how it united different peoples, bringing them into one faith?
When they came near, they immediately dismounted,
getting to know one another and asking detailed questions.
<margin>325</margin> The bride kissed her mother-in-law and her other new relatives,
while the mother-in-law joyfully kissed her own kin.
The <term>Arabian mares</term><note>original: "pharai" — high-bred horses, a symbol of status and the frontier lifestyle.</note> whinnied and rejoiced with one another,
and the joy for all became greater than what had come before.
When they reached the house, they immediately celebrated the weddings;
<margin>330</margin> and the Emir, having baptized his mother,
acted as her sponsor in her <term>spiritual rebirth</term><note>original: "anagennese" — refers to the rite of Christian baptism.</note>,
likewise for the relatives who had come with her.

950 "Circles... dancing": another verbal echo in the Greek text.
959 "United different peoples," i.e., brought the foreigners into the religious community (communion), not simply "joined different races into one."

“But say, my master, how goes it with you?”
“All is well,” he answered, “by the grace of Christ,
Who enlightened the hearts of my mother and my kin
<margin>940</margin>And guided them to the light of God’s knowledge;
Whom in a little while you shall see arriving here.”
And then taking his child into his arms
He uttered such words from the deep of his heart:
“When, my fine hawk, will you unfold your wings,
And hunt the partridge, and subdue the robbers?”
Thus the **Emir** <note>The father of Digenes, a former Arab leader who converted to Christianity for love.</note> was speaking to the child.
And all the people, having learned of his coming,
Ran to the house to offer their congratulations;
Much joy was there, impossible to tell;
<margin>950</margin>And forming choirs they began to dance.
Into this joy came yet another joy,
For someone came announcing the arrival of his mother.
Then men and women, all were to be seen
Going out to meet her, along with the General’s wife,
So many that it was hardly possible to count them.
(A strange wonder truly, the work of upright love;
Who would not be amazed? Who would not wonder,
And here learn the exact powers of Love,
How he joined foreign races in one faith?)
When they had come near, they straightway dismounted,
<margin>961</margin>Closely questioning, learning all about one another;
The bride embraced her husband’s mother and other kindred,
And she in turn was gladly embracing her kinsmen.
The horses whinnied gladly to each other,
And all had more joy than any that had gone before.
They reached the house and straightway held a wedding feast;
And the Emir, baptizing his own mother,
Acted as her sponsor in her spiritual <term>regeneration</term> <note>The process of being "born again" through the Christian rite of baptism.</note>,
As he did for his kinsmen who had come with her.

# BOOK FOUR
## Page 66

->The brave deeds of Akrites begin from this point,<-
->and how he carried off that most beautiful maiden;<-
->and concerning his marriage, this is the fourth book.<-

->* * *<-

And straightway I remind you of **passion** <note>original: "eros" — often used here to describe the overwhelming, driving force of romantic love.</note>;
for this has been established as the root and beginning of **love**, <margin>5</margin> <note>original: "agape" — here referring to a broader sense of devotion or affection.</note>
from which **friendship** <note>original: "philia"</note> is born, and then **desire** <note>original: "pothos"</note> is generated,
which, having grown little by little, bears such fruit:
perpetual cares, reflections, and worries,
immediate and manifold dangers, and separation from parents.
For youth in its prime uproots the heart, <margin>10</margin>
and then it dares all things that were never before attempted:
to reach even for the sea, and to fear fire not at all;
dragons and lions and all other wild beasts
this desire, once firmly set, considers as nothing;
it counts even the most daring bandits as naught, <margin>15</margin>
it waits through nights and days and views mountain passes <note>original: "kleisouras" — narrow, strategic mountain passes that were vital in the defense of the Byzantine frontiers.</note> as flat plains,
it treats sleeplessness as rest, and far-off things as near;
many even deny their own faith because of desire.
<margin>Leaf 21 recto</margin>
And let no one among you consider this unbelievable,
for I shall place in our midst a praiseworthy witness: <margin>20</margin>
the all-noble **Emir** <note>original: "amiran" — a title for a Muslim leader; here it refers to the hero's father as a prime example of someone driven by love.</note> and first man of Syria,
who possessed all-delightful beauty and fierce daring,
and a most wondrous stature, and most noble strength,
and he was even thought of as a second **Samson** <note>A biblical hero of the Israelites known for his superhuman strength.</note>;
for that man excelled by rending a lion with his hands, <margin>25</margin>
but this man killed an infinite number of lions.
Cease writing of **Homer** and the myths of **Achilles** <note>The central hero of the Iliad; the poet suggests his Christian hero is more "real" or superior to these pagan legends.</note>,
and likewise of **Hector**, for these things are false.
**Alexander the Macedonian** <note>Alexander the Great.</note> was powerful in wisdom,
and having God as a helper, he became ruler of the world. <margin>30</margin> <note>original: "kosmokratōr" — a title implying universal sovereignty.</note>

# FOURTH BOOK

->FROM THIS POINT begin the feats of the **Borderer**,<- <note>original: "Akrites" — referring to the frontiersmen who guarded the edges of the Byzantine Empire.</note>
->And how he carried off that lovely Girl,<-
->And concerning his wedding, this is the Fourth Book.<-

---

Straightway I advise you again concerning **Desire**,
For He is the root and the origin of **Love**,
From which is born **Affection**; then comes **Passion**,
Which, growing by degrees, brings forth such fruit
As constant cares, anxieties, and troubles,
Dangers crowding all at once, and parting from parents.
<margin>990</margin>
For youth, when at its bloom, uproots the heart,
And then dares all things that are unattempted:
To reach even for the sea and to fear fire not at all;
Dragons and lions and all other beasts
Passion, once confirmed, considers not at all;
It reckons as nothing the most undaunted robbers,
It believes that nights are days and the mountains are flat,
That waking is repose and far-off things are near;
And many even deny their faith for the sake of passion.
Let none of you think this is incredible;
<margin>1000</margin>
I will set a worthy witness in your midst:
The most highborn **Emir** <note>A high-ranking commander or prince in the Muslim world.</note> and chief of Syria,
Who possessed the sweetest beauties and a daring like a beast's,
A wondrous stature, and most noble strength;
Indeed, he was made to be a second **Samson**;
He shone as one who tore a lion with his hands;
The other would slay a countless host of lions.
Cease writing of **Homer**, and the fables of **Achilles**,
And likewise those of **Hector**, which are false.
Macedonian **Alexander**, strong in wisdom,
<margin>1010</margin>
With God’s help, was the master of the world;

<note>A satisfactory rendering. For further generalizations about these four sorts of love, see lines 1420 and 1604 following.</note>
989 <term>parting from parents</term> <note>original: "chorismon goneon"</note>, i.e., the act of leaving one's family behind, not the separation of the parents from each other.

<column-break/>

992 <term>to reach for the sea</term> <note>original: "thalattes... ephikesthai"</note>. This evidently refers to the raids of the hero, his father, and his uncles across the coastal regions of Asia Minor. See line 413 above.

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO IV

But he, possessing a steadfast mind, came to know God;
with Him, he also acquired both courage and daring.
As for the aged Philopappos, Kinnamos, and Ioannakis,
it is not at all worth recounting their deeds,
<margin>35</margin> for they boasted without having accomplished anything;
whereas his deeds are all true and witnessed.
Ambron was his grandfather, and his uncle was Karoes;
they gave him three thousand <term>picked spearmen</term> <note>original: "kontaratoi" — elite infantry or cavalry armed with the kontos, a long pike.</note>;
he subdued all of Syria and captured Kufa.
<margin>40</margin> Then in this manner he came into the regions of **Romania**, <note>The Byzantine Empire, the land of those who called themselves Romans.</note>
he raided many castles, the land of Herakleios,
he plundered Charziane and Cappadocia;
he carried off a delightful girl, a noble of the Doukas family,
<margin>21 vº.</margin> for the sake of her wondrous beauty and delightful youth,
<margin>45</margin> renouncing everything, both his faith and his glory together,
and he became an **Orthodox Christian** for her sake;
and he who was once an enemy was seen to be a servant of the Romans.
From these two a child was born, truly beautiful,
and from his very birth he was named **Basil**;
<margin>50</margin> but he is also called **Digenes** <note>Meaning "of two races" or "twice-born," from the Greek 'di' (two) and 'genos' (race/birth).</note> because of his parents:
pagan <note>original: "ethnikos" — usually referring to a non-Christian or Arab in this context.</note> from his father's side, but Roman from his mother's.
Having become fearsome, as the story shall show,
he is named **Akrites** <note>The "Borderer," derived from 'akrai,' the frontiers of the empire.</note> for subduing the borders.
His grandfather was Antakinos of the Kinnamas line,
<margin>55</margin> who died in exile by the command of the Emperor
Basil the Fortunate, the great borderer;
possessing a great inheritance and matchless glory,
he was proclaimed a great General by all.
His grandmother was the General's wife, a noble of the Doukas family;
<margin>60</margin> he had as uncles the wondrous brothers of his mother,
who even fought a duel for their sister's sake
against the wondrous Emir, his own father.
This man sprouted from the race of noble Romans,
and became illustrious for his manly deeds.

<note>Scholar's note: Line 38 requires the accent on 'trischilious' for the meter. Line 39 mentions 'Koufer' (Kufa), a reference previously noted in line 292.</note>

# DIGENES AKRITES

But he, possessing a steadfast mind, came to know his God,
And there possessed both daring and manliness.
As for the aged Philopappos, Kinnamos, and Ioannakes,
Nothing of their deeds is worth telling at all,
Who boasted in vain, for they accomplished nothing.
But all his deeds are true and well-attested.
Ambrôn was his grandfather, and Karoës his uncle;
They granted him three thousand <term>chosen spearmen</term> <note>original: "kontaratoi" — elite soldiers armed with the kontos, a long cavalry pike.</note>,
He subdued all of Syria and seized the city of Koufah;
<margin>1020</margin>
Then he came into the regions of **Romania**, <note>A term for the Byzantine Empire, the land of the Romans.</note>
Plundered many fortresses and the land of Hêrakles,
He ravaged Harzianê and the region of Kappadokia;
He carried off the Doukas’s fair and noble daughter,
For her elegant stature and her wondrous beauty,
Denying everything—both his faith and his fame—
He became an **Orthodox Christian** for her sake.
Their former foe was now seen as a servant of the Romans.
To them a child was born, most fair indeed,
Who from his very birth was named **Basil**,
<margin>1030</margin>
Called also **Twyborn** <note>original: "Digenes" — meaning "born of two races" or "two-blooded."</note> because of his parents:
A pagan father and a Roman mother;
Growing formidable, as the tale shall show,
He quelled the borders and was surnamed **Borderer** <note>original: "Akrites" — a soldier-guard of the imperial frontiers.</note>.
Antakinos was his grandfather, of the Kinnamades line,
Who died in exile by the order of the Emperor
Basil the Blessed, the mighty Borderer.
Great was his inheritance, and unmatched his fame,
Reputed by all to be a great general;
His grandmother was the general's wife, a noble Doukas;
<margin>1040</margin>
His uncles were his mother's wondrous brothers,
Who fought in single combat for their sister's sake
Against the wonderful Emir who was his father.
So he sprang from a line of noble Romans,
And was illustrious for his acts of bravery.

<note>1024 **wondrous** <note>original: "θαυμαστόν" (thaumaston). The poetic meter requires "θαυμάσιον" (thaumasion).</note></note>

<note>1031 **pagan** <note>original: "ἐθνικός" (ethnikos). This can mean "pagan," "heathen," or "gentile."</note></note>

**Vocabulary**
*   **Digenes Akrites:** The "Two-blood Borderer," the hero of the epic.
*   **Philopappos, Kinnamos, Ioannakes:** Rival leaders or traditional heroes mentioned to highlight Basil's superiority.
*   **Ambrôn & Karoës:** Relatives of the Emir (Basil’s father).
*   **Syria & Koufah:** Regions under Arab control that the Emir conquered.
*   **Romania:** The Byzantine Empire.
*   **Hêrakles, Harzianê, Kappadokia:** Imperial territories raided by the Emir before his conversion.
*   **Doukas:** One of the most powerful aristocratic families in Byzantium.
*   **Emir:** A high-ranking military commander or prince in the Islamic world.

<summary>This page recounts the conversion of an Arab Emir to Christianity for the love of a Byzantine noblewoman and the subsequent birth of their son, Basil. It explains his titles "Digenes" (Twyborn) and "Akrites" (Borderer), rooting his legendary status in his dual heritage and noble lineage.</summary>

# DIGENES AKRITES BOOK IV

<margin>65</margin> Now then, let us begin to recount his deeds.
This wondrous Basil the Borderer, then,
Was given to a teacher by his father from childhood;
And having spent three whole years in his studies,
<margin>22 rº.</margin>
<margin>70</margin> Through the sharpness of his mind, he mastered a wealth of learning.
From then on, desiring horsemanship and hunting,
He spent every day in these pursuits with his father.
Then, on one of these days, he says to his father:
"A desire, my lord and father, has entered my soul
<margin>75</margin> To test myself in <term>battles with wild beasts</term> <note>original: "thêriomachia" — the traditional heroic trial of hunting or fighting dangerous animals like lions or bears.</note>;
And if you truly love Basil, your son,
Let us go out to a place where there are wild beasts,
And you shall surely see the thought that is always troubling me."
Hearing such words from his most beloved son,
<margin>80</margin> The father rejoiced in spirit and was glad in his heart;
With much delight, he kissed him tenderly:
"O most beloved child, O my soul and heart,
Your words are wondrous and your intent is sweet,
But the time for beast-fighting is not yet here;
<margin>85</margin> For the war against beasts is most terrible,
And you are a boy of twelve years or <term>nearly thirteen</term> <note>original: "bisextos" — literally "leap year," used here colloquially to denote a child of twelve going on thirteen.</note>,
Quite unworthy to do battle with the beasts.
Do not, my sweetest child, let this enter your mind;
Do not pluck your beautiful roses before their time.
<margin>90</margin> But when, God willing, you reach full manhood,
Then it will be beyond question for you to fight the beasts."
And when the noble boy had heard these things,
He was greatly saddened; his heart was wounded.
And with tears in his eyes, he says to his father:
<margin>22 vº. 95</margin> "If I perform brave deeds after I reach manhood, father,
What is the gain for me in that? Everyone does as much.
Right now I desire to prove myself and bring glory to my race,
And I shall prove it to you, my benefactor."

# DIGENES AKRITES

So let us now begin to tell his story.
This Basil then, the wondrous <term>Borderer</term> <note>original: "Akrites" — a soldier-hero guarding the frontiers (Akra) of the Byzantine Empire.</note>,
Was given by his father to a teacher from childhood,
And by devoting three whole years to his lessons,
He acquired much learning through his keen mind.
<margin>1050</margin> Then, when he desired horsemanship and hunting,
He devoted himself to these each day with his father.
And so one day he says to his father:
"A desire is in my soul, my lord and father,
To test myself in fighting with wild beasts,
And, if you love your son Basil at all,
Let us go out to a place where there are beasts,
And you shall see the purpose that still troubles me."
Hearing such words from his dear one,
The father was glad in spirit and rejoiced in his heart;
With great delight he covered him with kisses:
<margin>1060</margin> "O soul and heart, O my most darling child,
Your words are wonderful, and your will is sweet,
But the time has not yet come for fighting beasts;
The war with beasts is very terrible,

Nor harvest your fair rose before its time;
When, God willing, you are a full-grown man,

“...to see him, how he rejoices, how he fights, how he runs.”
As the father and the uncles were saying these things,
a massive lion emerged from the reed-bed,
and immediately they turned to see the young lad <note>original: "agouritzēn" — a term for a young man or noble youth, common in Byzantine heroic poetry.</note>;
they spotted him in the marsh, dragging the wild beasts.
With his right hand he was dragging the bears he had killed,
and with his left he pulled the doe.
His uncle called to him: “Come here, child;
leave the dead ones behind, for we have another that is living,
by which the sons of the noble are put to the test.”
The boy answered him, saying these words:
<margin>Folio 24 recto</margin>
“If it is the will of God who favors all things,
and if I have the blessing of my father and my good mother,
you shall see him lying dead just like the two bears.”
And he rushed to go toward the lion without a sword.
But his uncle said to him: “Take your sword;
this is not a deer for you to tear down the middle.”
Straightway the youth spoke this word:
“God is not lacking in power, my uncle and lord,
to deliver him into my hands just like that other one.”
And, grasping the sword, he moved toward the beast;
when he drew near, the lion pounced,
and lashing <note>original: "charzanisas" — to lash or strike with a tail.</note> its tail against its sides,
it gave a great roar and came out against the youth.
But the boy, raising his sword high,
struck him on the head, right in the middle,
and the head was split down as far as the shoulders.
And Digenes spoke this word to his uncle:
“Do you see, my dear lord, the wonders of God?
Does he not lie speechless and dead just like the two bears?”
And his father and uncle kissed him fervently;
on his hands and arms, his eyes and his chest.

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative following Digenes Akrites’s successful hunt, where he slew a lion with a single blow. The text now shifts from the action of the hunt to a scene of familial praise and a ritualistic bathing of the hero, emphasizing both his physical beauty and his growing status as a legendary warrior. This transition highlights the blend of heroic epic and domestic affection typical of the Byzantine poem.</meta>

### DIGENES AKRITES BOOK IV

And both, rejoicing, spoke to him these words:
“Every man who sees your graceful <term>youthful stature<note>original: 'emnoston helikian' — referring to the prime of one's youth</note></term> and beauty,
O most beloved, truly shall not doubt,
<margin>195</margin> but will surely accept your acts of bravery.”
For the youth possessed a graceful stature,
<margin>24 vº.</margin> blonde hair, curly at the tips, large eyes,
a white face, rosy-hued, and jet-black eyebrows,
and a chest like crystal, a <term>fathom<note>an 'orgyia' was the distance between the tips of a man's outstretched arms, roughly six feet, used here to emphasize his heroic proportions</note></term> in width.
<margin>200</margin> Beholding him, his father rejoiced greatly,
and rejoicing, he spoke to him with great pleasure:
“Since the heat is great, and it is now midday,
and the wild beasts are hiding for now in the marsh;
come, let us go to the cool water,
<margin>205</margin> and wash your face from the much sweat;
you shall change what you wear, for they are stained
by the foam of the beasts and the blood of the lion;
and thrice-blessed am I to have such a child,
I shall even wash your feet with my own hands;
<margin>210</margin> from now on I shall cast every worry from my soul,
that I may be untroubled wherever I may send you,
whether to the many raids or the scouts of enemies.”
And immediately both went to the spring,
and the water was marvelous, cold as snow;
<margin>215</margin> and sitting around it, some washed his hands,
and others his face, and likewise his feet.
The water flowed out of the spring and they drank greedily,
as if from this they too might become brave.
And the boy changed his clothing,

“Whoever sees that graceful form and beauty
Of yours, darling, truly shall never doubt,
But surely shall accept your deeds of bravery.”
   Indeed the young man had a comely stature,
And fair hair, curling a little, and large eyes,
A white and rosy face, a brow all black,
His breast like crystal was a <term>fathom<note>original: 'orgyia'. A measurement of roughly six feet, representing the span of a man's outstretched arms, used here to emphasize his heroic, broad-chested physique.</note></term> broad.
<margin>1180</margin>
Looking on him, his father was most glad,
Spoke joyfully to him with great delight:
“The heat is great, it is midday,
Now the beasts hide themselves within the marsh;
Come, let us go aside to the cool water,
And you shall wash the heavy sweat from your face;
And you shall change your clothes, for they are soiled
With the beasts’ foaming and the lion’s blood.
Thrice blessed am I that I have such a son,
And I will wash your feet with my own hands.
<margin>1190</margin>

So that I may be heedless wherever I send you,
Into many raids and against posted enemies.”

There the water was wondrous, cold as snow.
Sitting round, some began to wash his hands,
Others his face, and likewise also his feet.
The spring ran over, and thirstily they drank,
So that they too might become brave from it.
And afterwards the boy changed his clothing;

The upper part of his garment was red with golden <term>borders<note>original: 'rhizas'. Literally "roots," used here to mean the hems or decorated edges of a robe.</note></term>,
<margin>folio 25 recto</margin>
and these borders were <term>enameled<note>original: 'chymeutai'. Refers to cloisonné or metalwork where glass or precious stones are fused into the gold.</note></term> with pearls;
his neck-collar was filled with <term>ambergris<note>original: 'abar'. A fragrant substance used in perfumes; some sources suggest it refers to aromatic herbs like marjoram.</note></term> and musk,
and he had large pearls instead of buttons;
<margin>225</margin>
the loops were twisted from pure gold.
He wore famous <term>leggings<note>original: 'toubia'. A type of gaiter or hose worn by Byzantine elites.</note></term> decorated with griffins,
and his spurs were woven with precious stones;
upon the gold-work he had carbuncle gems.
The noble boy was very eager
<margin>230</margin>
to return to his mother so she would not grieve for him,
and he urged everyone to mount their horses.
He mounted a horse as white as a dove;
its <term>forelock<note>original: 'sgordos'. The section of the mane that falls over the horse's forehead.</note></term> was braided with precious stones,
and golden bells were set among the gems—
<margin>235</margin>
very many little bells, and they produced a sound
pleasant and wondrous, amazing everyone.
He had a green and rose-colored <term>silk cloth<note>original: 'blattin'. A high-quality Byzantine silk, often dyed in vibrant colors.</note></term> over the horse's croup,
which covered the saddle so it would not be soiled by dust.
The bridle was braided with golden buckles,
<margin>240</margin>
and all the work was enameled with pearls.
The horse was daring and bold in its prancing,
and the boy was skillful in his riding;
everyone who saw that youth marveled—
how the horse pranced according to the young man’s will,
<margin>245</margin>

And they set off to return to their own home;
<margin>folio 25 verso</margin>
the young men went in front, riding in order,
while his uncle and his father followed behind,
and in the middle was the youth, flashing like the sun.

1057 *answered*. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 1065 *Philopappos*. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 1076 *bring*. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 1078 After *multitude*, the manuscript adds <note>original: 'λοιπὴ'</note> (is missing). There is indeed a considerable gap here.

1268 *above and below* or *all over*. This might just mean ‘under and over’ him, i.e. that he was lying on skins and covered by skins. But it more probably means ‘all over the place’. As a modern colloquialism <note>original: 'ἀπάνω κάτω'</note> means ‘more or less, approximately’, while <note>original: 'ἄνω κάτω'</note> means ‘upside down, in disarray’.

# DIGENES AKRITES

“Talk not of that, old man, I did it as a child;
<margin>1290</margin>Only I tell you, brave Philopappos, thus:
A hare running uphill and I will catch it,
Or stretch and snatch a partridge flying low.”
Thereupon Philopappos told the <term>reivers<note>original: 'ἀπελάται' (apelatai). These were semi-independent mountain warriors and cattle-raiders who lived in the "no-man's-land" between the Byzantine and Arab empires.</note></term>,
They brought a silver chair, Akrites sat,
They spread a wondrous table there before him,
And all with courtesy they ate and drank,
Thereafter each of them began to say
That they could fight with many of the brave.
And hearing Philopappos asked Akrites:
<margin>1300</margin>“And you, good youth, how many can you knock down?”
And straightway Basil answered on this wise:
“Come along, boys, let us take <term>quarterstaffs<note>original: 'ῥαβδία' (rhabdia). While the translator uses the English term for a long pole, the original Greek refers to shorter sticks used for training and sport.</note></term>
And let us all go off into the plain,
And each of us give stick-play with the rest,
Whoever beats a man shall take his staff.”
Thereupon they did take their quarterstaffs—
The wondrous Kinnamos with Ioannikios,
And with them Digenes and many others,
And all of them went down onto the level
<margin>1310</margin>And there began to give each other stick-play.

<column-break/>

<margin>1310a</margin>And thereupon that Basil Digenes
Took up his staff and came into the midst;
Some with his staff he smote, others with his fists,
And soon of all those braves the hands were loosed.
<note>The original Greek phrase 'their hands were loosed' (ὑπελύθησαν αἱ χεῖρες) is a stylistic nod to the epic language of Homer, implying the warriors were suddenly weakened or paralyzed by Basil's strength.</note>
Digenes taking up the staffs of them
Came to the old man saying to him thus:
“Take the staffs, Philopappos, of all your reivers;
If it displease you, I'll do the same to you.”
This then performed, the wondrous <term>Borderer<note>original: 'Akrites'. This title refers to the soldiers who guarded the 'Akra,' the outermost edges of the Byzantine Empire.</note></term>
Returned to the road where his people were.

---

<note>Translator Commentary: The term 'quarterstaff' might be misleading here; according to the Oxford English Dictionary, those were 6–8 feet long. These training sticks (<gloss>original: ῥαβδία / rhabdia</gloss>) must have been more like singlesticks used in fencing.</note>
<margin>1312</margin>The text uses specific terms for the violence: <gloss>blows with a stick (ῥαβδέας / rhabdeas)</gloss> and <gloss>blows with the fist or knuckles (σφονδυλέας / sphondyleas)</gloss>.

<margin>TRE V 1100</margin> Furthermore, O dearest one, I shall tell you this also:
how in that time the handsome Duke,
the wondrous general of a part of <term>Romania<note>original: 'Ῥωμανία' (Romania). The medieval Greek term for the Byzantine Empire, which they considered the continuation of the Roman Empire.</note></term>,
had an all-beautiful daughter named Eudokia,
whose name Akrites heard of constantly,
<margin>1105</margin> for she had infinite beauty and a most distinguished lineage.

<margin>GRO IV 254</margin> There was a house along the way belonging to a great general,
<margin>255</margin> and as he approached it he cried out:
“When a youth loves a most beautiful maiden,
and when he goes there and beholds her charms,
his little heart is mastered; he wishes no longer to live in this world.”
When those present in the house heard this delightful song,
<margin>260</margin> they were amazed, just as that <term>Odysseus<note>The clever hero of Homer's *Odyssey*, who famously had himself tied to his ship's mast to hear the Sirens safely.</note></term> once was,
when he heard the song of the <term>Sirens<note>Mythological sea-creatures who lured sailors to their deaths with irresistible music.</note></term> upon his ship.
But neither did the maiden remain deaf to the youth—
that all-fair, famous, and renowned girl,
whose beauty was beyond compare and her lineage most distinguished,
<margin>265</margin> whose wealth and property and other riches
it is impossible to count or even to imagine.
For no one can sufficiently praise her house alone;
for it was all of gold and marble, entirely decorated with mosaics.

<margin>26 rº.</margin> Now this most wealthy and all-beautiful girl,

---

It is remarkable that this folk-song should have found its way into all three versions of Digenes (Grottaferrata 1692, Trebizond 1128) as well as into the poem of Constantine Manasses. It may have come originally from Heliodorus’s *Aethiopica* (Book 3, Chapter 7): "the origin of love... the passions are shot into the souls through the eyes" <note>original: "τῶν ἐρώτων γένεσις . . . διὰ τῶν ὀφθαλμῶν τὰ πάθη ταῖς ψυχαῖς εἰστοξεύονται"</note>. And also from Achilles Tatius (Book 1, Chapter 4).

1361 <term>sweet young man<note>original: "ἔμνοστον νέον" (emnoston neon). The word suggests someone attractive or pleasing.</note></term>. See above line 1173. <term>Sweet<note>original: "ἔμνοστος" (emnostos)</note></term> was the medieval form of <term>wholesome<note>original: "εὔνοστος" (eunostos)</note></term> and disappeared after trans-

<column-break/>

ferring its meaning to the modern Greek word for <term>tasty or charming<note>original: "νόστιμος" (nostimos)</note></term>. See below line 3284, <term>most sweet<note>original: "πανεύμνοστος" (paneumnostos)</note></term>.

1365 <term>She remained<note>original: "Ἀπέμεινε" (Apemeine)</note></term>, i.e. she stayed as long as she wanted to. For the force of the prefix <term>away/thoroughly<note>original: "ἀπο-" (apo-)</note></term> see below line 3239 <term>having washed thoroughly<note>original: "ἀποπλύσασα" (apoplysasa)</note></term>; and see A. Mirambel, ‘The Determinate Aspect in Mod. Greek’, in *The Link*, No. 1, June 1938.

1366 In the Trebizond manuscript line 1140, which from this point again diverges considerably, Digenes only pretends not to know whose house it is.

> so that I may have him as a father-in-law by his own consent;
> if not, my father, you shall know the outcome after these things.”
> “Many times I have sent word to him, my sweetest son,
> but he is not at all persuaded to consent to this.”
<margin>310</margin> While the father was speaking such things to the boy,
the youth caught sight of the maiden through the opening;
and having beheld her, he did not walk forward,
but amazement and a trembling of the heart seized him;
he spurred his <term>steed<note>original: 'βοῦλχαν' (boulchan). A term for a swift, noble horse, likely of Arabic or Persian origin, fitting the frontier setting of the epic.</note></term> forward, drew near to the girl,
<margin>315</margin> and to her he softly spoke these words:
> “Let me know, maiden, if you hold me in your mind,
> and if you greatly desire for me to take you as a wife;
> but if your mind is elsewhere, I will not entreat you much.”
The girl then entreated her own nurse:
<margin>320</margin> > “Go down, my good nurse, and tell the <term>young man<note>original: 'ἀγουρίτζην' (agouritzin). A term of endearment for a youth.</note></term>;
> I swear to you by God that you have entered into my soul;
> but I do not know, young man, what lineage you might be;
> if you are indeed Basil Digenes Akrites,
> you are of the noble and the wealthiest
<margin>Folio 27 recto</margin> <margin>325</margin> > and a kinsman of ours, being of the <term>Doukas family<note>One of the most powerful and aristocratic families of the Byzantine Empire.</note></term>;
> but my father the General keeps a strict watch,
> for he has heard much of your brave deeds;
> and take care, young man, that you do not come to harm because of me.”

**316** Instead of "if" (*ei*), the manuscript gives "into" (*eis*). **318** One could write "if you have your mind elsewhere," or consider *echeis* ("you have") as an enclitic, which it sometimes is in such cases. **320** young lad. **321** entered.

The word is the accusative of the classical Attic form <note>original: 'ἀνάπλεως' (anapleos)</note>, meaning 'full up' or 'overflowing,' agreeing with 'love.' 402 *neglects*. 405 *it set*. <note>Original French: "I remove 'and' after 'sun', this conjunction making the verse too long."</note> 410 *grew weary*. 414 *grief*. 418 *at a loss*. 420 *here*.

1477 **with his bare hands**. This might mean with his bare hands, i.e. without a plectrum <note>a small pick used for plucking strings</note>, which was ordinarily used (see below 1509), but more probably means here without singing, as the word *psilos* <note>literally "bare" or "stripped"</note> was regularly used for music unaccompanied by the voice (see classical references in Liddell & Scott). In Achilles Tatius (Book 1, chapter 5, line 4) it clearly means ‘without a plectrum’. Compare also the word *epsilotraouei* <note>singing without instruments</note> in an Akritic song from Karpathos (Polites, *Selections*, No. 69)

<column-break/>

which seems to mean ‘sing without accompaniment’, and ‘bare words’ <note>Greek: psilois logois</note>, Plato, *Symposium* 215 c.
1480 This seems to be the literary version of a folk-song, which may have been a rather meaningless jingle; the version in another manuscript is quite meaningless. Hesseling (*Laografia*, iii, p. 547) quotes a similar song from Passow (distich 700, p. 550).

And having tuned it, he struck it, murmuring:
<margin>1480</margin> “Whoever loves nearby shall not be short of sleep,
But whoever loves from afar, let him not waste his nights:
Far away is my love, and so let me go quickly,
That I do not hurt the soul that wakes for me.”
The sun was setting and the moon came up
When he rode out alone holding his lute.
The black horse was swift, the moonlight was like the day,
At dawn he arrived at the Girl’s pavilion. <note>Usually a tent or a small private building within a larger estate.</note>
As she had stayed awake and waited all through the night,
<margin>1490</margin> At the dawn she relaxed and fell into a sleep.
And when he did not see her, the noble youth
Was much distressed and was sorely troubled,
And evil thoughts began to strike at his heart,
With unbearable woe and the greatest pain.
For in himself he said, “Has she perhaps repented?
Is she afraid her parents might notice her?
What then shall I devise? How can I learn the truth?
My mind is confused, I know not what to do:
For if I speak, others will hear me calling,
They will rush out and find me, those who are here on guard.”

---

...ically repeated in line 1487, regarding his arrival at her *pavilion* <note>Original Greek: 'kouboukli'</note>, which was evidently a separate building. The scholar Legrand’s omission of the word *and* <note>Original Greek: 'kai'</note> is quite unnecessary.
1486 *daos*. This mysterious word, used in the romance *Lybistros and Rhodamne* often and as a noun (like the word *black* <note>Original Greek: 'mauros'</note> is used for a horse here) remains obscure. In the *Chronicle of the Morea* 3378, the phrase *horse of the daos* <note>Original Greek: 'alogon tou daou'</note> seems to mean a mountain horse (derived from the Turkish word *dagh* for mountain), but the reading is doubtful. The historian Kyriakides, who characteristically prefers the reading of the Escorial manuscript 844 (in which it is the black horse which shines like the moon), discusses the word at length, but throws no more

<column-break/>

light on it than a "black horse." An Arabic root is probable; and it may be connected with the term *dagalis* <note>A term for a specific horse color, possibly chestnut or spotted.</note> (see below line 3178, and other manuscript references TRE 222, 697, and 2549, which Legrand translated as "chestnut") and AND 673, etc.
1499 *to give* <note>Original Greek: 'dosoun'</note> in this sense is an extension of the meaning "to strike," for which see references in Sophocles' Greek Lexicon. It is also possible to take the pronoun *me* with the verb *to give* as well as with the verb *to suspect* <note>Original Greek: 'hyponoesoun'</note>, in which case it would mean "hit" not in the sense of "strike" but in the sense of "to not miss"; compare *Lybistros and Rhodamne (Scaliger version)* lines 1206, 2432; in this context, it could be translated as "find me."

“And if I am discovered before the deed, without reaching my dearest,
Since I cannot easily see the one I desire,
What benefit is there for me to live in this life?”
As he said these things to himself, being greatly amazed,
<margin>425</margin> He decided that it was necessary to strike his lute, <note>Greek: 'kithara'.</note>
So that he might make a trial of the things that troubled him;
“I will spare myself,” he said, “by testing the girl;
I will set this lute here in our midst as my helper,
<margin>29 rº.</margin> And let God’s will certainly be done.”
<margin>430</margin> And having tuned it and striking it with the plectrum,
He performed a most sweet melody, whispering:
“How have you forgotten, O all-delightful one, our new love,
And fallen into a sweet sleep so unconcernedly and easily?
Arise, all-delightful rose, scented apple;
<margin>435</margin> The morning star has risen, come let us walk.”
And when the girl heard the sweetness of the lute,
She leapt from her bed, tightened her belt, <note>Greek: 'zonarin', a girdle or sash.</note>
And leaning down low, she said to the youth: <note>Greek: 'agouritzin', a term for a young man or brave lad.</note>

<margin>440</margin> Meaning to reproach you always for being hesitant and lazy,
But this lute you are playing—it seems you don't know where you are!
Good sir, if my father notices you, he will judge you harshly,
And if you die because of me—oh, the lawlessness!
For God knows, who is the knower of secrets,
<margin>445</margin> That your love has taken root in my heart,
And I consider your failure to be my own disaster;
Save yourself then, my soul, before the light catches you,
And remember me always, who desires you so much.
For indeed, noble youth, I do not wish to come with you;
<margin>450</margin> I know that desire burns you, and this strange love,
And your reasoning persuades you to die for my sake;
<margin>29 vº.</margin> But if you take me away and I come with you,
And my brothers and my kin find out...”

## BOOK IV

“...And if my father should overtake you with a great host,
<margin>455</margin> how do you have the means to carry me off and save your own soul?”
Then the wondrous youth, grieved, replied to the maiden:
> “I praise your distress, O most noble lady,
> for by looking at the opposite of what is bound to happen,
> you choose the best course, reasoning clearly;
<margin>460</margin> > but you know nothing at all of my character or strength;
> for if you had knowledge of my brave deeds,
> you would not say that your brothers and your kinsmen
> could catch and strike me down, making you grieve on my account;
> but let this be known and certain to you, my soul,
<margin>465</margin> > that I expect to crush whole armies single-handedly,
> to defeat battle arrays and subdue great powers;
> as for your father and his young warriors, <note>Greek: 'agourous', a term for young soldiers or retainers.</note>
> and likewise your brothers and your relatives,
> I consider them all as mere infants and of no account at all;
<margin>470</margin> > this alone I seek to learn from your lips:
> if you are truly eager to follow me,
> so that we may depart through the narrow passes before daybreak;
> for narrow alleys and tight passes can kill even the brave; <note>A military maxim: a hero can be trapped in a small space, but excels in the open.</note>
> in the open plains, however, even the faint-hearted are made bold;
<margin>474</margin> > but if perhaps you have changed your mind, or preferred another,
> and for this reason you offer such excuses—
> by the holy martyrs of Christ, the Theodores <note>Saint Theodore Tiron and Saint Theodore Stratelates, two popular warrior-saints often invoked by soldiers.</note>—
> no other man shall ever be called your husband while I live!”
Then the sun-bright maiden spoke again to the youth:
<margin>30 r°. 480</margin> > “You, O most desired one, were once as inexperienced
> in all matters of love and friendship as you claimed;
> but now you suffer for me, and perhaps you speak the truth,
> as I judge from my own similar feelings;
> for even if it is quite improper for me to speak of my feelings,
<margin>485</margin> > nevertheless I will tell you everything, for I am enslaved by desire;
> many of the noble lords and high-ranking magnates...”

# DIGENES AKRITES

“If my father with a crowd should overtake you,
How could you get me out and save your life?”
And grieved, the hero to the Girl replied:
“Your trouble, noblest lady, I commend,
For looking at the front of things to come,
Reasoning clearly, you forejudge most well;
Yet nothing knowing about me at all—
<margin>1540</margin>For if you were acquainted with my deeds,
You would not say your brothers and your kin
Will catch and cast me down, and you be sorry.
But be this known and sure to you, my soul,
Alone I look to overcome armies,
Subdue their powers, and conquer their arrays;
As for your father and his company,
Likewise your brothers and with them your kinsmen,
I reckon them just babies, being nothing.
Only this I want to learn from your own lips:
<margin>1550</margin>If you are most eager to follow me,
That we may leave the by-ways before dawn
(Brave men are killed by lanes and alley-ways,
And in the open cowards are made brave); <note>This reflects a common medieval military sentiment: a hero's strength is neutralized in narrow city streets or passes, but in an open field, his true power is unstoppable.</note>
But if you have repented, or chosen another,
Therefore put forward this sort of excuses,
Then by the Saints Theodore, Christ's witnesses, <note>Saint Theodore Tiron and Saint Theodore Stratelates were the premier warrior-saints of the Byzantine military.</note>
None other, while I live, shall be called your husband.”
Again spoke to him the sun-lovely Girl:
“You then, dearest of all, untried, as first,
<margin>1560</margin>Are of all love and affection, as you said,
And now suffer for me, and perhaps speak true;
Myself likewise this also testify,
Though to tell you about myself be wrong,
Yet by desire enslaved I will tell you all:
Me many noble princes, many captains...

refers to the possibility of a rival.

# DIGENES AKRITES BOOK IV

"Even the kinsmen of kings and their children sought me, 
bearing a royal escort and royal garments, 
and longing to behold my physical form. 
<margin>490</margin> Frequently they passed very close to my house, 
but my father was not satisfied with any of them at all. 
No one was ever deemed worthy to see even my shadow; 
no one ever heard my voice or any conversation from me, 
nor the smile of my laughter, nor the sound of my footstep. 
<margin>495</margin> I never put my head out of the window; <note>In the elite Byzantine household, high-born women (especially unmarried daughters) were often kept in "gynaikonitis" (women’s quarters) to protect their reputation and family honor.</note>
I kept myself unseen by all strangers. 
For except for my own relatives and close acquaintances, 
no one ever saw the features of my face, 
as I maintained the strict decorum that is fitting for virgins. 
<margin>500</margin> But now I have stepped outside of these things and passed their boundaries, 
and I have become 'shameless' <note>The Greek term *anaides* (shameless) here implies a bold casting off of social constraints and maidenly modesty for the sake of passion.</note> because of my love for you. 
And I, who was never seen by any strange man, 
now share my words with you, feeling no shame at all; 
and my truly free virginal mind 
<margin>30 vº. 505</margin> I see becoming a slave and suddenly bold. 
For from the hour I saw your face, O young man, 
it immediately burned my temperate soul like fire; 
it changed my logic as well as my resolve; 
it enslaved my mind, making me unashamed 
<margin>510</margin> toward you alone, beloved, and toward your love. 
I am now persuaded, and I wish to go with you, 
for whose sake I renounce my kin, I am deprived of my parents, 
I am estranged from my brothers and from untold wealth. 
And I will go with you wherever you may command, 
<margin>515</margin> having God as a witness, who helps everyone, 
and who is a most beautiful avenger—do not lead me astray. 
Love burns you, love incites you, 
and your reason persuades you to die for me, 
which I pray never to see or hear with my ears." 
<margin>520</margin> As the all-lovely girl spoke these things, 
she wept from her eyes, she sighed deeply, 
and she blamed herself for such great shamelessness.

# DIGENES AKRITES

“...They have sought me, even kinsmen and the sons of kings,
having their royal retinue and vesture,
desiring to behold my countenance.
They would pass frequently before my house;
<margin>1570</margin> with none of them at all was my father contented.
Not one was thought worthy even to see my shadow;
none ever heard my voice or my conversation,
my laughing or the sound of my footfall.
At the window I never showed my head,
unseen by other eyes I kept myself
(for except for my kinsmen and my own household,
no one has ever seen my face),
keeping the strictness which is fitting for a maiden. <note>In elite Byzantine society, young women of noble birth were often kept in seclusion—a practice sometimes called "gynaikonitis"—to preserve their reputation and marriageability.</note>
But now I am out and, having overstepped these bounds,
<margin>1580</margin> I have become shameless on account of your love.
And I, who was never seen by a strange man,
now share my talk with you without any shame at all,
while the free purpose of my virginity
I see has become a slave and quite bold. <note>The "shamelessness" the Maiden refers to is not a lack of morals, but rather the act of breaking the rigid social protocols that dictated a noblewoman's behavior.</note>
For from the hour, O youth, that I saw your face,
it was as if fire burnt up my temperate soul,
altered my reason and likewise my nature,
enslaved my purpose, making me immodest
toward you alone, my dear, and toward your love.
<margin>1590</margin> Now I obey, and I wish to go with you,
for whom I deny my kin and am robbed of parents,
estranged from my brothers and from boundless wealth;
I will go with you wherever you may command,
holding God as my witness, the helper of us all,
and the best avenger if you should lead me astray.
For you too, love burns you and love pricks you on,
and reason tells you that you would die for me—
that which I pray never to see or hear.”
While she was speaking thus, the all-lovely Girl
<margin>1600</margin> filled up her eyes with tears and deeply sighed,
and blamed herself for her great shamelessness.

And, wishing to change her mind by a great deal,
the inexperienced longing within her would not allow it.
<margin>525</margin> For such is the power of love, longing, and affection:
while these things strictly maintain their own order,
even if one possesses a temperate mind, longing wars against it.
And for this reason, the one who longs has no sense of order;
he is not ashamed before relatives, nor is he frightened of neighbors,
<margin>530</margin> but is entirely shameless and a slave to affection—
<margin>31 r^o.</margin> just as these most beautiful youths have also suffered.
Then the wonderful youth, seeing that the maiden had begun to weep,
himself replied with tears of his own, saying:
“I, O most beautiful maiden, know all the facts about you:
<margin>535</margin> the infinite wealth which your father possesses,
because of which many noblemen longed to take you as a bride;
I have become aware of all this, having investigated it accurately.
For I, my dearest soul, do not crave wealth;
I do not desire possessions, nor do I yearn for glory.
<margin>540</margin> I consider all such things as common grass <note>original: "χόρτον" (chorton). This is a biblical and poetic metaphor for something that is fleeting or worthless compared to spiritual or emotional truth.</note> while I revel in your beauty.
From the hour, O dark-eyed one, that we looked upon each other,
you have not departed from my soul for a single hour. <note>original: "ὡρίτζαν μίαν." This is a vernacular or diminutive form of the word for "hour," emphasizing the precision of his devotion.</note>
For you were rooted deep within and became intertwined with my soul,
<margin>545</margin> and I imagine you in everything, and I see you even when you are not there.
For I have never before been in love with any kind of beauty,
nor have I ever known the ways of love at all.
Come here, my sweet light, follow your lover;
reveal that love which you hold within.
<margin>550</margin> For the certain proof of love is established by deeds,
and we shall live together by God’s will, both of us rejoicing.
In this, your parents too shall eventually be glad,
once they realize what sort of son-in-law they have obtained;
and no one shall reproach you—rather, everyone will call you blessed.”
<margin>554</margin> These things and many others the youth spoke...

And yet, though wishing much to change her mind,
Boundless desire within would not allow her.
(The power of love, desire and affection,
Things which exactly keep their proper station—
One has a temperate mind, yet desire attacks him,
Wherefore the lover has no discipline,

The hero then, seeing the Girl weeping,
Himself in tears made answer to her thus:
‘O lovely maid, I know your circumstance,
The boundless riches which your father owns,
For which many nobles wanted to take you,
All this I know by careful inquiry;
For I, my dearest soul, aim not at riches,
Want no possessions, reach not after fame,
Count it all grass <note>original: "χόρτον" (chorton). A metaphor for something temporary or worthless compared to true love.</note>, being rich in your beauty;
<margin>1620</margin>For from the hour, dark-eyed one <note>original: "μαυρόμματε" (maurommate)</note>, we saw each other,
You went not from my soul one little hour <note>original: "ὠρίζαν" (orizan)</note>,
Rooted within, you were entwined in it;
My fancy sees you even when you are not there;
For never yet have I loved any beauty,
Nor have I known at all the ways of love.
Come then, my sweet light, follow your lover;
Show forth the affection which you have within,
For a strict demonstration is in deeds,
By God’s assent we will live in joy together.
<margin>1630</margin>Your parents too will be made glad by this,
When they have heard what a son-in-law they have gotten;
And none shall blame but rather call you blessed.’
This and much else the young man having uttered,

“In you is my every beginning,” he said, “and the end,
<margin>555</margin>
commencing with God’s help until my very death;
<margin>31 verso</margin>
and if I should ever wish to grieve you, my soul,
or fail to keep my love for you unclouded <note>original: "ἀθόλωτον" (atholoton). Literally "unstirred" or "without sediment," describing a pure, clear liquid.</note>,
and my desire most pure until my death,
<margin>560</margin>
may I not die a Christian, may I not prosper,
may I not earn the prayers of my parents;
and may you, most noble one, keep the same!”
The girl, these things echoing in her ears, said:
<margin>565</margin>
“Even if it is most unlawful for me to betray myself
(for true nobility is called 'order' <note>original: "τάξις" (taxis). In Byzantine thought, this refers to the proper social and moral order one must maintain.</note>,
which I have set aside, I know not what possessed me),
nevertheless, your pure passion and steadfast love
have persuaded me to prefer your most beautiful affection.”
<margin>570</margin>
Then the girl, speaking an oath of love, said:
“Abandoning parents, brothers, and home,
I entrust myself to you through God, O youth <note>original: "ἄγουρε" (agoure). A term for a young, vigorous man or hero.</note>,
give Him to me as a witness that you will never grieve me,
but will make me your lawful wife until the end;
<margin>575</margin>
for many lovers have set aside their words,
after first appearing to be in love with those they desired.”
And when the boy heard this, he was struck with wonder
at the maiden’s wisdom; and he swore:
“By the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit,
<margin>580</margin>
I will never grieve you, O most noble lady,
but I will make you the lady and mistress of my house,
my wife and companion <note>original: "σύνοικον" (synoikon). One who shares a home.</note> until the end of my life,
<margin>32 recto</margin>
if you keep your passion for me pure,
just as I have told you before, O my beloved soul.”
<margin>585</margin>

the maiden leaned out from the golden window;
the boy stood up on his horse and received her;
the partridge flew out, the hawk received her <note>This is a common Byzantine metaphor where the "partridge" represents the delicate maiden and the "hawk" the swift, powerful hero taking his prize.</note>.

“In you is my every beginning, and my end,” he said,
“Commenced with God’s help, until my passing;
And should I ever wish, my soul, to grieve you,

And most pure passion until my dying day,

This echoing in her ears, the Girl replied:
“Though it be a grave transgression to give myself away
(For true nobility is called ‘order’ <note>original: "τάξις" (taxis). In the Byzantine world, this referred to the divinely ordained social and moral structure; the Maiden acknowledges she is breaking social protocol by eloping.</note>,
Which I have set at naught, I know not how),
Yet sudden passion, and my certain affection,
Made me prefer your beautiful love.”

“Leaving my house, my brothers, and my parents,
<margin>1650</margin>

Take Him to witness never to grieve me at all,

(For many lovers set their words at naught

even while held in the arms of her dearly beloved),

Upon seeing them from afar, the all-lovely girl
(for she was looking back and gazing sharply
while held in the arms of her beloved),

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Basil (Digenes) Akrites's elopement. Having defeated the General's initial forces, Basil is now confronted by the girl's immediate family. ### DIGENES AKRITES
115

“Do not harm my own brothers, my love <note>original: ψυχή (psyche) — literally 'soul', used here as a term of endearment equivalent to 'my life' or 'my darling'</note>;
For those whom you see coming now towards us,
I judge by their horses that they are my brothers,
And the third man who is with them is my father;
Grant them to me safe, and keep them from harm.”

“What you ask shall be done,” the boy said to the girl,
“If nothing else happens unexpectedly;
(For whoever spares his foes in time of war
<margin>1740</margin> Is often ruthlessly struck down by them).” <note>A common sentiment in Byzantine heroic poetry, reflecting the practical dangers of chivalry.</note>

Having said this, he jumped onto his horse
And fell quickly upon those surrounding the General.
The brothers of the girl, filled with great zeal,
Were telling their own men to finish him,
Expecting that his death would come by other hands;
But the boy, keeping the command of his dear one,
Attacking with skill and wisdom, finished all the others. <note>He kills the soldiers/guards to neutralize the threat while sparing the family.</note>
Then, as the brothers rushed madly upon him,
He circled them and threw them from their horses,
<margin>1750</margin> Taking strict care not to harm or wound them at all.
Then he turned to the General, who was further off, and dismounted;
Tightly clasping his own hands and bowing low, <note>A gesture of submission and respect to a superior or elder.</note>
He began to say to him with a cheerful face:

“Forgive me, master, and do not blame me at all:
Your troops were unskilled <note>original: ἄγροικοι (agroikoi) — literally 'boorish' or 'rustic', implying they lacked the noble training of a true warrior</note> at the give and take of battle,
And therefore most of them have gone to the underworld <note>original: Hades — the Greek realm of the dead</note>;
For I do not lack nobility or valor,
And if you ever bid me to do you service,
You shall know everything about your son-in-law;
<margin>1760</margin> And if you judge me strictly by my deeds,
You shall often bless yourself for your good fortune.”

Straightway the General, lifting high his hands
And turning his face to the east <note>The traditional direction for prayer in the Byzantine Christian world</note>, gave thanks to God,
Saying, “Glory to Thee, O God, who rules all things
Well for us in unspeakable wisdom:
Just as I wished, I am granted a son-in-law
Who is noble, fair, temperate, and brave.”

<summary>Digenes Akrites successfully subdues the girl's brothers without harming them and presents himself respectfully to the General. Impressed by Basil's prowess and noble bearing, the General offers a prayer of thanks for such a worthy son-in-law.</summary>
<keywords>Digenes Akrites, mercy, chivalry, General (Strategos), son-in-law, Byzantine warfare, prayer, nobility</keywords>

### DIGENES AKRITES GRO IV
116

“…the likes of which no one has ever encountered in the world.”
<margin>690</margin> Having thanked God for these things from his whole soul,
The General spoke gently to the youth in this way:
“Thanks be to God for all things, O golden son-in-law <note>original: χρυσόγαμβρε (chrysogambre) — a term of high affection and status, literally 'golden-son-in-law'</note>,
To Him who ordains all things for our benefit;
Take her, most handsome one, whom you claimed out of desire;
<margin>695</margin> For if you did not possess an infinite desire for her,
You never would have dared to enter among thousands alone.
Come now, let us depart for my own house,
And do not think you will suffer anything painful from us,
But rather that we may draw up the matching marriage
<margin>700</margin> Contracts in writing, with your father present;
For as soon as he is summoned, he will return to us,
And you shall receive the dowry for my daughter.
I shall make your wedding famous throughout the world,
So that you may receive your dowry from this very day:
<margin>705</margin> Twenty centenaria <note>original: κεντηνάρια (kentenaria) — a Byzantine unit of weight; one centenarium was equal to 100 Roman pounds (litrai) of gold</note> of ancient coinage,
Which I set aside long ago and placed specifically
In the name of my dearest daughter; and silver vessels,
A wardrobe <note>original: βιστιάριον (bistiarion) — referring here to the value of clothing, silks, and textiles</note> worth five hundred pounds,
Many landed estates with thirty-six sources of income,
<margin>710</margin> Seventy maids along with her mother’s house,
Which is truly magnificent and most precious;
Likewise, her mother’s extraordinary jewels,
The world-renowned crown, a work worthy of praise,
Composed of gold and most precious stones;
<margin>715</margin> And along with all these, the livestock to be found:
Four hundred prime animals, eighty grooms,
Fourteen cooks and as many bakers <note>original: μάγκιπας (mankipas) — from the Latin 'manceps', a term for a baker or someone in charge of bread production</note>,
And another hundred and fifty souls <note>original: ψυχάρια (psycharia) — 'little souls', a term for household servants or slaves</note>.”

---

695 *most handsome one*. 706 *ancient*: instead of the usual accent, this reflects a specific medieval Greek pronunciation. 710 *thirty-six sources of income*. 716 The first half of this line is missing a syllable. 717 *grooms*. 719 *fifty*.

---

**1772** *golden son-in-law*. Compare to 'black-eyed one' mentioned previously in line 1620, showing the successful use of local, everyday titles of affection.
**1785** *centenaria*. Legrand translates this as 'quintals', and Constantinides’ dictionary gives 'quintal', while the Oxford English Dictionary defines 'quintal' as a

<column-break/>

hundredweight (derived from the Arabic *qintār*). See Legrand’s note: ‘The quintal or *centenarium* is worth one hundred Byzantine pounds or *litrae*.’ Gibbon, writing on the finances of the Emperor Justinian (chapter 40), calculates a centenary of gold as being the equivalent of four thousand British pounds <note>This refers to 18th-century pound values; in modern terms, 20 centenaria would represent a massive fortune of roughly 1,440 pounds of solid gold.</note>.

<summary>The General formally accepts Basil as his son-in-law, marveling at his bravery and inviting him to his home to sign a marriage contract. The page lists an enormous dowry consisting of gold, estates, luxury goods, and hundreds of servants, reflecting the high aristocratic status of the families.</summary>

### DIGENES AKRITES
116

“…the likes of which no one has ever discovered in the world.”
<margin>1770</margin> Thus, offering gratitude to God with his whole soul,
He spoke these kind words to the youth:
“My golden son-in-law <note>original: χρυσόγαμβρε (chrysogambre) — a term of high affection and status</note>, thanks be to God above all,
Who guides well those things that are for our benefit.
Take her, fair young man, whom you have longed for;
If your desire for her were not so boundless,
You would not have dared to face thousands alone.
Come then, let us go into my house;
And do not think you will receive any harm from us,
But rather that we shall make the marriage agreements,
<margin>1780</margin> Written contracts, in the presence of your father,
And soon, once advised, you shall return to us,
And carry away my daughter’s dowry.
I will make this wedding famous throughout the world,
And from this day you shall receive your dowry:
Twenty centenaria <note>original: κεντηνάρια (kentenaria) — a Byzantine weight of roughly 72 lbs of gold</note> of ancient coins,
Which I set aside and sorted long ago
In the name of my dearest daughter; and silver vessels,
A wardrobe <term>bistiarion (from Latin vestiarium): a place for clothes or treasure</term> valued at five hundred pounds,
Thirty-six estates with their own incomes,
<margin>1790</margin> Seventy handmaids, along with her mother’s house,
Which is magnificent and truly precious,
As well as her mother’s wonderful ornaments <note>original: κόσμια (kosmia)</note>,
Her world-renowned crown, an admirable piece of craftsmanship
Made of fine gold and most precious stones;
And with all these, the livestock found there,
Four hundred prizewinners <note>referring to prize-winning horses</note>, and eighty grooms,
Fourteen cooks, and an equal number of bakers,
And one hundred and fifty other serfs <note>original: ψυχάρια (psycharia) — literally 'little souls'</note>.

---

<columns>2</columns>

1788 *bistiarian*, that is, *vestiarium* <note>Latin: wardrobe</note>. *Vestiarium* can mean, to quote Maigne d'Arnis: “a place where not only clothes are kept, but also treasures, and even the treasury and funds.” So here it may be explanatory of the vessels, “a treasure chest of silver plate worth five hundred pounds.”
1792 *kosmia*: “ornaments.”
1795 This line (TRE 1310) should

<column-break/>

apparently come after 1789. Read: “the things found” <note>original: τὰ εὑρισκόμενα (ta heuriskomena)</note>.
1796 *proteia* <note>literally 'first prizes' or 'excellences'</note> (a favorite word of the scholar Psellos) is difficult here; but see line 2245 below, where it is unmistakably used to refer to horses.
1798 *psycharia* <note>Greek: 'little souls'</note>. *Mancipia* <note>Latin: slaves or serfs</note>, as cited by Ducange from Constantine Porphyrogenitus' *On Administering the Empire* 32 (55): “they gave in exchange... two souls [slaves] and two falcons.” (Also see 9. 52.)

### DIGENES AKRITES BOOK IV
118

“I shall give you preference over my other children, 720
much wealth beyond measure, and no small amount of property;
along with these, I shall provide even more than what I have said.
Before the sacred blessing <note>original: ἱερολογίαν (hierologian) — the religious marriage rite</note> takes place, my child,
I shall also make your wedding famous throughout the world,
so that the young men never cry out that you are a ‘marriage-thief’ <note>original: κλεψίγαμον (klepsigamon) — one who steals a bride without legal or religious sanction</note>, 725
or that you snatched a girl who had no share of possessions—
a thing which is a natural disgrace to all right-thinking people.
And how could you not easily escape these shames
if you would only return home with us now?
This is so that the General’s wife <term>strategissa: the wife of a strategos or military governor</term> may find comfort 730
(for she has no idea at all what sort of man you are),
and so that, rejoicing, she may magnify the Giver of Good.
Be persuaded then, my good son-in-law, and come with me.”
When the General had said these things, flattering the youth,
the younger man immediately replied to the General: 735
“To be persuaded by your advice, since it is excellent,
my lord and father-in-law, is only right for me.
Yet I fear that danger may come upon me from this,
and I shall owe a most piteous death accompanied by shame,
<margin>35 r°.</margin> having acted as an enemy, a conspirator, and a foe toward you. 740
For my conscience persuades me to keep to the opposite course,
and I blush even to look upon the face of the General’s wife.
I, my lord father-in-law, had only one desire:
to take your daughter for the sake of her beauty,
and not for the sake of wealth or possessions. 745
I grant all these things to my brothers-in-law;
her beauty is enough for me in place of many dowries.
God provides wealth and poverty alike;
He humbles and He exalts, He brings down and He raises up.
But as for returning with you, I shall not disobey; 750
only let me first go to my own mother,
so that my father may see the bride he wishes to have,
and so he may glorify God—and then I shall quickly return.
Do not be grieved by this, but rather pray for us,
for we are your children and the servants of your soul.” 755
And the General marveled at the youth’s wisdom.

<note>729: "now" — the word is missing in the manuscript. 732: "magnifies." 743: "my lord." 750: "to return." 753: "that" — missing in the manuscript.</note>

<summary>The General urges Digenes to return home to formalize the marriage and avoid the social stigma of bride-theft, but Digenes respectfully declines the dowry and insists on visiting his own parents first. This exchange highlights the Byzantine values of filial piety, religious legitimacy in marriage, and the hero's disdain for material wealth compared to honor and beauty.</summary>

“I shall give you honor above my other children,
<margin>1800</margin>
much boundless wealth, and not a few estates;

Therefore, good son-in-law, be persuaded and come with me.”

“To obey your counsel, which is excellent,
my lord and father-in-law, is only right for me;
but I fear that danger may come from this,
and I may pay the price of a miserable death in shame,
<margin>1820</margin>
as one who has been your enemy and a traitorous foe.

I, my lord father-in-law, had the desire

not for her riches or her possessions;
I give all of these to my wife’s brothers.

so that my father may see his daughter-in-law-to-be
and may praise God; then I shall quickly return.
Do not regret this, but rather pray for us;
we are your children and servants of your soul.”

Grant you enjoy all the years of your life.”
And having embraced him he mounted his horse;
He to the Girl; the General went home,
