<meta>This page continues the narrative following the Emir’s decision to meet his son, Digenes, and his new bride. It details the lavishness of the welcoming party and the emotional exchange between the young couple as they approach Digenes' family.</meta>

# DIGENES AKRITES
## BOOK IV

<page-num>123</page-num>

Filled with joy, he immediately mounted his horse;
his wife’s five brothers, along with three thousand men, followed.
<margin>1870</margin> They prepared twelve saddles and bridles for the women;
two of them were entirely encrusted with pearls,
the others were made of gold, decorated with animal designs,
and all the saddles were beautifully draped.
The horses were all hung with purple silk <note>original: βλαττία (blattia)—a high-status silk fabric, often dyed imperial purple</note>
by which they were enwrapped, along with much gold;
behind these were trumpets and heavy horns,
and they played drums and organs loudly,
so that at that time the noise was heard from afar.
When they had advanced to within three miles of the house,
<margin>1880</margin> the lovely Girl, seeing them from a distance,
began to tremble all over, not knowing who they were,
and she said to the boy in great distress,
“If they are strangers, they will tear us apart.”
“Fear not, my sweet light,” the youth replied,
“That is your father-in-law; he has taken this trouble for your sake.” <note>original: ἐκοπώθη (ekopōthē)—literally "he has tired himself," implying a gesture of respect and effort</note>
Again the sun-born child <note>A common epithet in Byzantine poetry for someone of extraordinary beauty</note> answered the boy,
“My love, I am ashamed, because I am alone;
if you had turned back with me and heeded my father,
so that I might have my maids and my wedding finery,
<margin>1890</margin> then your father would know whose child you have taken;
but you decided this, so the excuses must be yours.”
“Most noble lady, do not grieve to be alone,
for they all know who you are, even if you are by yourself;
there is no blame to be found in this.”
When they came near, they greeted one another;
the young man then dismounted with his beloved,
and the Emir dismounted and embraced them both.
“May God,” he said, “my child, give you His blessing,
and increase your years in peace and wealth.”

---

<margin>1879</margin> The three-mile limit. See the earlier reference at line 635 and later at line 2272. <note>This "three-mile" distance appears to be a traditional boundary for welcoming or escorting high-status guests in the poem.</note>
<margin>1885</margin> *was weary/took trouble*. “He has taken all this trouble.” The classical verb *kopoo* (to weary) is used here in the sense of the modern Greek *kopiazo* (to toil), for which see Sophocles’ lexicon under *kopiao*, who refers to Theophanes 728. 18. There, it is used in exactly the same sense as this passage: “to take

<column-break/>

the trouble, or have the great kindness, to come.” For a similar use of *kourazomai* (to be tired/to toil), see the *Sacrifice of Abraham* line 766, where the correct reading is *kourasthe* (should he take the trouble).
<margin>1889</margin> *wedding finery*. Read as *exoplisin* (equipment/finery) as found in the Trapezuntine (TRE 1349) and Andros (AND 2212) manuscripts.
<margin>1891</margin> The Grottaferrata manuscript (GRO) noticeably gives the Girl a distinct character and allows her to be critical of the hero's impulsive decisions.

“...and may He show them to be partakers of His kingdom!”
They seated the girl upon a jeweled saddle <note>original: χυμευτὴν (chymeutēn)—referring to metalwork decorated with enamel or "niello" by a goldsmith</note>,
and placed a precious crown upon her head,
and every one of the relatives who were found there
<margin>825</margin> brought her gifts that were truly of highest value;
and, having dressed the boy as was fitting for the youth,
they gave the signal on the horns <note>original: βούκινα (boukina)—from the Latin 'buccina', a curved military horn</note> and turned back immediately.
The trumpets blared, the drums spoke out,
the organs were played, and everything sang in harmony;
<margin>830</margin> harps sent forth their sound with every kind of music,
and they quickly returned to the house amidst singing.
The surpassing joy that occurred then—
who could interpret it or have the strength to tell of it?
For it seemed as if even the very earth on which they walked
<margin>835</margin> was itself delighted as they all processed along;
and every person who happened to be at that celebration
was transformed from one state to another by the gladness. <note>original: ἄλλος ἐξ ἄλλου (allos ex allou)—an idiom meaning they were "beside themselves" or "changed men" with joy</note>
The hills skipped, the rocks danced,
the rivers bubbled up, and the trees rejoiced;
<margin>840</margin> even the air grew bright in that great joy.
As they were about to finally approach the house,
a countless multitude of women met them;
for the general’s wife <note>original: στρατήγισσα (stratēgissa)—referring here to Digenes' grandmother</note> was there to meet the young couple,
and with her was the all-delightful mother of Akrites,
<margin>845</margin> along with elegant handmaidens, brilliantly adorned,
<margin>37 v<sup>o</sup>.</margin> some holding flowers, roses and myrtle,
while the scent of incense perfumed the air.
Others struck hand-cymbals while singing
a most sweet melody, praising the boy
<margin>850</margin> and the maiden with him, and the parents of both.
The floor was strewn with myrtle and laurel,
roses, narcissus, and many fragrant flowers.
The mother-in-law embraced her own daughter-in-law,
bestowing exquisite gifts upon her with great honor.

<margin>1900</margin>“...and may He show you to be partakers in His kingdom!”
They seated the girl upon a saddle adorned with jewels,
and placed a costly crown upon her head;
and every one of the kinsmen who were present there
offered her gifts that were truly of great value.
The boy, too, was dressed in finery as was fitting for the youth.
Then they blew the horns and began at once to return;
the trumpets blared and the drums resounded,
the organs played, and everything broke into song.
The lutes rang out with every kind of music,
<margin>1910</margin>and with singing they returned quickly to the house.
Who could interpret or have the power to describe
the overwhelming joy that existed then?
It seemed as if even the very earth on which they walked
was delighted along with them as they stepped upon it.
Every person who found themselves in that joyful crowd
was completely overcome with happiness;
the hills skipped and the rocks seemed to dance,
the rivers bubbled up, the trees made merry,
and the very air grew radiant with that joy.
<margin>1920</margin>When they were finally about to approach the house,
a boundless crowd of women came out to meet them;
there was the General’s wife to meet the young couple,
and with her was the charming mother of the <term>Borderer</term> <note>original: Akritēs—the title for those who guarded the frontiers of the Byzantine Empire</note>.

some bearing flowers, roses, and branches of myrtle,
perfuming the air with balmy scents;
others were beating cymbals as they sang
a song of exceeding sweetness, praising the boy,
the young virgin with him, and the parents of them both.

with narcissus, roses, and many fragrant flowers.
The mother-in-law fondly embraced the bride,

<columns>2</columns>
**1924** This charming detail of the reception is not found in any other version of the epic; the organized "system of housemaids" and the ritual use of flowers is repeated later in lines 1957 and following.
<column-break/>
**1933** *her exceptional works* <note>original: ἔργα αὐτῆς ἐξαίρετα (erga autēs exaireta)</note>. This refers to needlework, weaving, or embroidery. Such "women's work" is a traditional sign of honor, as seen in Homer’s *Odyssey* (20. 72), the *Iliad* (9. 390), or Theocritus (15. 37): "And into these works I have put my very soul." <note>original: τοῖς δ’ ἔργοις καὶ τὰν ψυχὰν ποτέθηκα</note>

So there was endless pleasure and great delight.
When they came to the house, the <term>Emir</term> <note>A high-ranking military commander; here referring to Digenes’ father, a converted Arab noble.</note> straightway
mustered his own wife’s brothers with a substantial company
and sent three thousand men-at-arms

“Bid my <term>fellow-father-in-law</term> <note>original: sympentheros — a specific Greek term for the relationship between the parents of a married couple.</note> come to the wedding,
<margin>1940</margin>which God has settled, even if it was not by our own will.” <note>A reference to the fact that the marriage began as an abduction before being sanctioned by the parents.</note>
When the General heard them, not neglecting any detail,
taking with him all things fitting for the honor of his dear ones,
in numbers beyond imagining,
on the next day he set forth with his lady.
For they had nothing [negative] to say, nor any room for doubt,
having learned what a [noble] bridegroom they had found;
eagerly then and with the greatest gladness
they sped on their way with the marriage songs,
along with the girl’s brothers and those attending them.
<margin>1950</margin>Hearing then of their arrival, the boy’s
father went out to welcome them with his folk;
and when that <term>wondrous man</term> <note>original: ho thaumastos — a title of high respect.</note> would have dismounted,
the General prevented him, adjuring him [to stay in the saddle]. <note>A gesture of mutual respect; the General refuses to let the Emir perform the humble act of dismounting first.</note>
Having embraced each other as was meet,
they hastened toward the house, and after a little while
met a countless company on foot,
and others with them, an assembly of housemaids;
and as they reached the precincts of the house,
a crowd bearing incense stood in proper array
<margin>1960</margin>with rosewater and all other perfumes.

That wonderful reception by the Emir,
the lovely conversation of his spouse,
the ordered banqueting and due array
of meats, and the endless, varied spectacle,

<column-break/>

1952 The phrase <term>the wondrous man</term> <note>original: ho thaumastos</note>, used absolutely, usually means, as noted elsewhere, "the Hero"—that is, Digenes himself. Here, however, it is used to refer to the Emir, <term>the father of the boy</term> <note>original: ho tou paidos patēr</note> from the preceding lines, who had also been a Hero in his own right, as the scholar Henri Grégoire has reminded us.

1957 “With them others, a group of housemaids.” <note>original: met’ ekeinous heteroi systēma oiketidōn</note>. This is an example of the author's habitual confusion of grammatical genders, just as the three lines following show a confusion of noun cases. <note>The translator is pointing out that the medieval Greek of the manuscript often ignores strict classical grammar.</note>

Three months lasted the marriage celebration

<margin>2020</margin>

<margin>950</margin> The Emir <note>original: "amiras"; Digenes' father, a converted Arab leader.</note> returned to his own house,

who joined imperial glory to his name

and having learned the facts about him, he was greatly amazed.

---

Scholars are determined to find in the Epic some trace of the Akritic Ballad Cycle, in which Digenes is quite a minor character without a wife of his own. It is in fact a commonplace of European balladry, the "Lochinvar" theme of the Stolen Bride.
<margin>2047</margin> "How that" <note>original: "hopos te"</note> suggests that a line has been lost (similar to the Trebizond manuscript, line 1471).
<margin>2048</margin> Black-Snow-dwellers <note>original: "Mavrochionitai"</note>, probably the same as the Black-dwellers <note>original: "Mavronitai"</note> of line 3742, possibly from the Black Mountain <note>original: "Mauron Oros"</note> located between

<column-break/>

Antioch and the sea.
<margin>2050</margin> "King" <note>original: "Basileus"</note> I have translated as ‘King’ and "Majesty" <note>original: "Basileia"</note> as ‘majesty’ throughout—even in line 2106, where ‘kingdom’ might be more suitable.
<margin>2052, 2053</margin> Basil . . . the royal glory <note>original: "Basileios . . . ten basileion doxan"</note>. This is a mild pun in the original Greek, as the name Basil means "kingly." This wordplay is not reproduced in the translation, though it satisfies the editor's preference for verbal repetition. Basil II, known as the "Bulgar-Slayer" <note>original: "Boulgaroktonos"</note>, who reigned from 960–1025, was likely the emperor referred to here.

<margin>40 rº. 980</margin> The Emperor sent a letter to him containing these words:
> “O my child, my Majesty <note>original: "ἡ ἐμὴ βασιλεία" — a formal way the Byzantine Emperor referred to himself.</note>
> having learned of your successes, we have rejoiced in them,
> sending up thanks to God who works alongside you;
> we have been filled with a desire to see you with our own eyes,
<margin>985</margin>> and to grant you rewards worthy of your deeds;
> come then to us, rejoicing and without hesitation,
> not suspecting that you will suffer anything distressing from us.”

But he, having received this, sent back a reply:
> “I happen to be the lowest servant of your power,
<margin>990</margin>> even if I am lacking in all good qualities;
> but what achievement of mine do you wonder at, Master,
> one so humble and base and entirely lacking in daring?
> Nevertheless, all things are possible to the one who trusts in God;
> and since you desire to see your servant,
<margin>995</margin>> come with a few men to the river Euphrates,
> and there you shall see me, holy Master, whenever you wish.
> And do not think I am being disobedient about coming to meet you;
> but rather because you possess some reckless <note>original: "ἀπείρους" — literally "inexperienced," but in this context implying soldiers who lack the self-control to deal with a hero of Digenes' stature.</note> soldiers,
> and if perhaps some should say what is not right,
<margin>1000</margin>> I shall surely cause you to be deprived of such men,
> for such things happen among young men, O Master.”

And as the Emperor went through the letter word for word,
he marveled at the humility of the youth’s speech,
and rejoiced, perceiving the height of his courage.
<margin>40 vº. 1005</margin> Desiring greatly to see the young man,
taking with him one hundred soldiers
and sufficient bodyguards, he came to the Euphrates,
having commanded all of them not to utter a single
reproachful word in the presence of Akrites.
<margin>1010</margin> Those who had been ordered to keep watch for this purpose
quickly announced the arrival
of the Emperor to Digenes, the wondrous Akrites.
And Digenes went out to meet him entirely alone,
bowing his head even to the ground:
<margin>1015</margin>> “Greetings,” he said, “you who received your sovereignty from God,
> and who rules over all because of the impiety of the nations,” <note>original: "δι᾿ ἀσέβειαν ἐθνῶν" — This suggests the Emperor's authority is a divine tool to punish or manage the "unbelieving" nations surrounding the Empire.</note>

<note>2059 The poet uses a formal, stilted style here to mimic the official correspondence of the Byzantine court, where even personal letters followed strict protocols of address.</note>

He sent him a letter containing these words:
> “Concerning you, my child, my Majesty <note>original: "ἡ ἐμὴ βασιλεία" — a standard formal way the Byzantine Emperor referred to himself in the third person.</note>
<margin>2060</margin> having learned of your deeds, we have rejoiced in them,
sending up thanks to God who works alongside you;
it is our purpose to see you with our own eyes
and to give you a reward worthy of your deeds.
Come then towards us gladly and without hesitation,
suspecting nothing distressing from our hands.”

Receiving this, he sent back a reply:
> “I am the lowest servant of your power,
even though I have no share of benefits;
what deed of mine, Master, do you admire,
<margin>2070</margin> in me, who am quite humble, worthless, and lacking in daring?
Yet all things can be done by him who ventures with God;
inasmuch as you wish to see your servant,
in a few days be by the Euphrates <note>The Euphrates River served as the symbolic and often literal boundary between the Byzantine Empire and the Arab Caliphate.</note> river;
there, holy Master, you shall see me when you will.
Think not that I refuse to come before you;
but rather because you have some inexperienced <note>original: "ἀπείρους" — while meaning "inexperienced," it implies soldiers who lack the discipline to show proper respect to a powerful local lord like Digenes.</note> soldiers,
and if some chanced to say what they ought not,
I should for certain make you miss such men;
for with the young, Master, such things occur.”

admired the modest diction of the boy,
and gladly understood his lofty valour.

taking with him a hundred soldiers
and a few spearmen, he came to the Euphrates,

a word of blame before the <term>Borderer</term> <note>The term "Akrites" (Borderer) refers to the military settlers who guarded the frontiers of the Empire.</note>.

Lord of all nations, through their wickedness,” <note>Digenes acknowledges the Emperor’s divine right to rule over the worldly nations.</note>

“Whence has this come to me, that the master of all the earth
has come to visit me, a man set at naught?”
Seeing him, the Emperor was struck with amazement at the sight;
forgetting all the weight of his imperial dignity,
<margin>1020</margin>he stepped a little forward from his throne and greeted him joyfully;
he kissed him with great cheer, marvelling at his stature,
and the great composition of his magnificent beauty.
“You have, my child,” he said, “the proof of your deeds;
for the harmony of your beauty portrays your courage.
<margin>1025</margin>If only Romania <note>original: "Ῥωμανία" — The name the Byzantines used for their empire, literally "the land of the Romans."</note> had four such as you!
Speak then, my child, with all boldness,
and take whatever you desire from my kingdom.”
“Keep all things, Master,” the youth replied,
“for your love alone is sufficient for me.
<margin>1030</margin>It is not right to receive, but rather to give,
and you have countless expenses for your army.
<margin>41 rº.</margin>I ask and I entreat the power of your glory:
love your subjects, show mercy to the poor,
rescue the oppressed and those suffering under injustice,
<margin>1035</margin>grant forgiveness to those who strike unintentionally,
do not listen to slanders, do not accept what is unjust,
drive away heretics, and strengthen the orthodox.
For these things, Master, are the weapons of justice,
with which you shall be able to overcome all your enemies.
<margin>1040</margin>For to hold power and to rule is not of our own strength;
it is the gift of God alone and of the hand of the Most High.
But I, your most humble servant, grant to your power
the tribute that was paid in time past to Ikonion <note>The Sultanate of Rum, centered in Ikonion (modern Konya), which often demanded tribute from border territories.</note>;
I shall see that you receive twice as much from them against their will,
<margin>1045</margin>and I shall make you, Master, free from worry in this regard,
until my soul shall depart from this frame <note>original: "σκήνους" — literally "tent," a common poetic and biblical metaphor for the human body.</note>.”
And the Emperor rejoiced at these words.

Line 1034: "rescue the oppressed and those suffering under injustice." The word *wrongdoers* (adikountas) must be taken to mean here "suffering injustice." Stranger things have happened in manuscript transmissions.

‘Why has this happened to me, that the master of all the earth
comes before me, though I am of no account?’
Seeing him, the Emperor was astonished by the sight;
forgetting all the heavy burden of his imperial majesty,
he stepped down from his throne a little and embraced him gladly.
<margin>2100</margin>He kissed him joyfully, admiring his tall stature
and the great abundance of his mature beauty,
saying: ‘You have, my child, the proof of your deeds in your face,
for beauty so well-formed reveals true valor.
If only Romania <note>original: "Ῥωμανία" — The name the Byzantines used for their empire, literally "the land of the Romans."</note> had four such men as you!
Speak then, my child, fully and with all freedom,
and take from my majesty whatever you desire.’
‘Master, keep everything,’ the youth replied,
‘Your love alone is quite enough for me.
It is not right for me to take, but rather to give;
<margin>2110</margin>you have unmatched expenses in maintaining your army.
I claim and I entreat the power of your glory:
love obedience, show pity to the poor,
deliver the oppressed from injustice,
grant forgiveness for unintentional faults,
do not listen to slanders, and accept no injustice;
scatter the heretics and confirm the faith of the orthodox.
These, master, are the arms of righteousness,
with which you can overcome all your enemies.
For rule and kingship do not belong to human might;
<margin>2120</margin>only God grants power, by the right hand of the Most High.
I, worthless as I am, grant to your power
the tribute that Iconium <note>Iconium (modern Konya) was the capital of the Seljuk Sultanate of Rum. The poem suggests Digenes has forced them to pay taxes to the Emperor.</note> once paid,
and as much again from their unwilling hands;
from this burden, master, I will set you free,
until my soul departs from its earthly dwelling.’
Upon these words of his, the King rejoiced,

---

**2114** *they strike*. <note>original: "παίουσι"</note> Read: *they stumble* or *err*. <note>original: "πταίουσι"</note> Compare the Trebizond (TRE) manuscript line 1528 and the Andros (AND) manuscript line 2394 which use the word *faults*. <note>original: "πταίσματα"</note>

**2122** The reference to the tribute of Iconium is not found in the TRE or AND versions of the poem. It can hardly be linked directly to the historical Emperor Basil II. Could it echo the defeat of Emperor Romanos IV Diogenes at Manzikert in 1071? Regardless, this reference should not be

<column-break/>

taken too seriously as a historical fact. As suggested in the introduction, the poet is providing a romantic and condensed view of the past two centuries, likely writing from a peaceful period in the middle of the eleventh century (during the reign of Constantine IX Monomachos, 1042–1054).

->**Vocabulary**<-

**Digenes Akrites:** The "Two-Blood Border-Lord," the legendary hero of the Byzantine frontier.
**Romania:** The Byzantine Empire.
**Iconium:** A major city in central Asia Minor, often a flashpoint between Greeks and Turks.
**Emperor Basil:** Likely Basil II (the "Bulgar-Slayer"), though the poem blends several historical figures.
**Romanos Diogenes:** A Byzantine Emperor famously defeated and captured by the Seljuk Turks.
**Constantine IX Monomachos:** A mid-11th-century Emperor associated with a flourishing of arts and letters.
**Arms of Righteousness:** A metaphor for the moral virtues required of a Christian king.
**Kingship:** The divine right to rule, portrayed here as a gift from God rather than a result of military strength.

<summary>This page concludes Digenes' moral advice to the Emperor, where the hero emphasizes that true power comes from God and justice rather than wealth. Digenes refuses imperial gifts and instead promises to secure the empire's borders and provide tribute from the conquered city of Iconium.</summary>

And he says: “O marvelous, most handsome youth,
my empire holds you as a <term>patrician</term><note>A high-ranking title in the Byzantine court, originally established by Constantine the Great for those who served the state with distinction.</note>,
gifting to you all the estates of your grandfather,
and I grant you the power to govern the borderlands;
I shall confirm all these securely in a <term>chrysobull</term><note>original: "χρυσόβουλλον" — A formal imperial decree or charter authenticated by a golden seal.</note>,
and I provide you with precious imperial robes.”
When the Emperor had said these things, the youth immediately ordered
one of his horses—one that was wild and untamed—
to be brought before them bound with iron chains;
he told the servants to loosen it, saying, “Let it run.”
And having tucked his robes firmly into his belt,
he began to run after it to catch it;
and in a short distance, having seized the mane,
he turned that wild and great beast back,
even as it kicked and snorted, thinking only of escape;
and coming before the noble Emperor,
he threw it down, spread flat upon the ground.
And all were astonished at the extraordinary sight.
As they were about to withdraw, a lion came out
from the grove and terrified those who were present
(for many lions are found in that place),
and even the Emperor himself turned to flee.
But the boy, running toward the lion at once,
seized it by one of its hind legs,
shook it violently and dashed it to the earth,

**2141** *snorting/kicking.* <note>original: "στροινιάζων"</note> Ducange quotes from a manuscript where a similar word is used for a fish; the Liddell & Scott dictionary gives a version used for elephants trumpeting and for harsh sounds, as well as for "wantonness"; so the unbroken horse here is either ‘kicking and plunging’ or ‘kicking and screaming’ (notice both participles are in the nominative case, agreeing with the horse in the line before). See also in the New Testament (1 Timothy 5:11) for the word used for the "wantonness" of young widows.

# DIGENES AKRITES
### 139

And the Emperor said, “O wondrous, excellent young man,
My majesty appoints you a <term>patrician</term><note>A high-ranking title in the Byzantine court, often granted to those of noble birth or exceptional service.</note>,
Granting you all your grandfather’s estates;
<margin>2130</margin> I assign you authority to rule the borders;
These things I will fast confirm in a <term>Golden Bull</term><note>original: "χρυσόβουλλον" — A formal imperial decree or charter authenticated by a golden seal.</note>,
And precious royal vestments I bestow.”
So spoke the King; straightway the youth commanding
One of his horses fresh from grass, unbroken,
To be brought before them hobbled in irons;
Bade his boys loose him, saying “Let him run”;
And tucking fast his kilts into his girdle,
Began to run behind to catch him up,
And in a little distance seizing the mane
<margin>2140</margin> He turned round backwards the great beast and wild,
Kicking and plunging, all thinking to escape;
And when the brave boy came before the King,
He dashed it down spread out upon the ground.
All were astounded at the marvellous sight.
As he was going, a lion from the grove
Came out and startled those were there with him
(For there are many lions in that place)—
Even the King himself was turned to fly.
The Boy at once running up to the lion
<margin>2150</margin> And snatching hold of one of its hind legs,
Mightily shook and dashed it on the ground,

---

...who “wax wanton against” <note>original: "καταστρηνιάζουσιν"</note> Christ (the scholar Souter gives “exercise youthful vigour against”), and in Revelation 18:3, 7, 9, the words <term>strēnos</term> and <term>strēniaō</term> are used in the senses of “wantonness” or “to live wantonly” (the King James Bible says “live deliciously”). Morosi, in his *Greek Dialects of the Land of Otranto* (1870), gives the terms “strignazo, strionno... I run wild or frolic <note>original Italian: "sbizzarrisco"</note> (used for horses, bulls, etc.)”; and relates <term>stroibiniázō</term> and <term>stroibónō</term> to Ancient Greek forms. The variation from <term>strōiaō</term> to <term>strēniaō</term>, which developed before the onset of <term>itacism</term><note>A shift in Greek pronunciation where several different vowels and diphthongs came to be pronounced like the English "ee".</note>, is, in onomatopoeic words of this sort, no more than the difference between “whine” and “whinny”. Vlasto, in his *Synonyms and Related Words*, lists <term>striniázō</term> under the entry for “to shout.”

<column-break/>

To the New Testament references above should be added from the Greek Old Testament (LXX) 4 Kingdoms 19:28, <term>strēnos</term> (The King James Bible 2 Kings 19:28 translates this as: “Thy tumult is come up into my ears”). The ancient encyclopedia Suidas quotes this and adds “I live wantonly—I am unruly.” The grammarian Phrynichus says the word for “living wantonly” is wrongly used by writers of the New Comedy instead of the word for “living in luxury” <note>original: "τρυφῶ"</note>; and Rutherford, in his edition, adds two references from Middle Comedy, noting: “in neither of these passages is it a synonym for ‘living in luxury’, but expresses the fighting-cock feeling of a man who has just risen from a hearty meal.”

2142 <term>noble-born</term><note>original: "γεννάδα" — a classical term for a high-born or noble man.</note>. A classical term; see the Liddell & Scott dictionary.

See also a dirge from Arahova (Polites, *Selections*, no. 195). . . *fresh youth with crystal arms* <note>original: "ἄγουρε δροσερὲ κρουσταλλοβραχιονᾶτε"</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the physical feats of Digenes Akrites (the Borderer) to a more philosophical and moralizing tone at the start of Book V. It moves from his public recognition by the Emperor to his personal confession of past moral failings, specifically a lapse into adultery during his youth. This serves to humanize the hero and frame his adventures within a framework of Christian repentance.</meta>

# FIFTH DISCOURSE

All youth is truly vanity,
whenever it stretches toward disordered pleasures;
but he who safely guides its reins
remains forever unconquered <note>original: "ἀχείρωτος"</note> by the passions,
and is shown to be an heir of eternal life, <margin>5</margin>
instead of the fleeting, shameful, and profane pleasure.
For he who lives in luxury, it is impossible to attain the eternal;
for just as it is not possible to quench a fire with oil,
so it is not possible for one living in luxury to flee from sin,
through which the fire is fed for all the unchaste. <margin>10</margin>
And indeed this wondrous and noble Akrites <term>Borderer</term>,
who was enriched with all the gifts of God,
having for a short time allowed his youth to grow soft <note>original: "χαυνώσας" — a classical term suggesting being relaxed, enfeebled, or loosened by luxury.</note>
fell carelessly into the crime of adultery;
<margin>Folio 42 verso</margin> but later having become repentant for this, <margin>15</margin>
he announced his error to those who met him,
not for the sake of boasting, but of repentance.
For on one of the days, having met a Cappadocian man,
wishing to humble his own sin,
he recounted to him these things, saying modestly: <margin>20</margin>
> “When I voluntarily separated from my father
> and chose to dwell alone in the borders <term>akrai</term>,
> I wished to travel into inner Syria,
> being in the fifteenth year of my age;
> and having reached the waterless plains of Arabia, <margin>25</margin>
> traversing the road alone, as I was accustomed,
> sitting upon my horse <note>original: "φάραν" — a swift horse; the term is derived from the Arabic 'faras'.</note>, carrying also a spear,
> I became entirely parched with thirst (for the heat was great), <margin>28</margin>
> and I looked everywhere to see where there might be water;

<meta>This page continues the transition into the "Fifth Discourse" of the epic. Having established Digenes’ status as a hero honored by the Emperor, the narrative now shifts to a moralizing reflection on his youth. This page begins the hero’s confession to a Cappadocian man regarding a past moral failing—an act of adultery—which occurred when he was just fifteen years old and traveling alone through the desert.</meta>

# FIFTH BOOK

Truly, all youth is vanity
Whenever it reaches out after unbridled pleasures;
But he who firmly guides its reins
Remains forever unconquered by the passions
And is made an heir of eternal life,
Instead of fleeting, base, and unclean pleasure.
For one who lives in luxury cannot win the eternal;
<margin>2180</margin> Just as fire can never be extinguished with oil,
Neither can the lustful ever escape sin,
By which the fire is fed for all the unchaste.
So this wondrous and noble Akrites <term>Borderer</term>,
Who was enriched with all the gifts of God,
Having for a short time allowed his youth to grow soft <note>original: "χαυνώσας" — meaning loosened or weakened by luxury</note>
Fell heedlessly into the sin of adultery.
And afterwards, being penitent for this,
He would tell of his fault to those who met him,
Not for the sake of boasting, but of penitence.
<margin>2190</margin> So on a day, meeting a Cappadocian man,
Wishing to show the baseness of his sin,
He told this tale to him in humble words:
“When I willingly separated from my father
And chose to live alone on the borders,
I wished to travel into inner Syria,
Then being in my fifteenth year of age.
Reaching the waterless plains of Arabia,
Making my way, as was my habit, alone,
Sitting my horse and carrying a spear,
<margin>2200</margin> I was very thirsty, for the heat was intense,
And I looked everywhere for where water might be.”

# DIGENES AKRITES

I saw a tree far off, near a thick swamp,
Pressed my horse, thinking there was water there,
And was not wrong; a date-palm was the tree
And from its root a wondrous spring gushed up.
When I came near to it I heard moaning
Lamentations with weeping and many tears.
The mourner was a girl most beautiful;
But I, believing that I saw a spirit,
<margin>2210</margin>Was all affrighted and, hair standing up,
Began to draw the steel which always guards me;
The place was desert, pathless, overgrown.
She, when she saw me, jumped up at once,
Wrapping herself about in decent order,
Wiped with her linen the showers from her eyes,
And gladly thus began to speak to me:
“From where do you travel alone, and to where, good young man?
Surely you too are not strayed here for love?
But since, it seems, by God you have been guided
<margin>2220</margin>To save me in my misery from the desert,
Rest yourself here, my lord, a little while,
That rightly as may be you may hear about me,
And I take consolation for my woes
(For grief is lifted from the soul by words).”
I too, when I heard this, was changed to joy,
Rightly understanding the apparition was real;
So straightway with much pleasure I dismounted;
Her beauty unspeakable had touched my soul,
So that I thought her a second to my own <note>The hero refers to his own wife or beloved, suggesting this girl's beauty was an equal match.</note>.
<margin>2230</margin>My horse I tied up to the branching tree,
And set my spear to stand between its roots,
Then took some water and spoke to her thus:

<columns>2</columns>

2229 **"A second to my own"** <note>original Greek: "δευτέραν τῆς ἐμῆς"</note>. Some classical scholars may feel bound by the genitive case to translate this as "Second to [i.e., only surpassed in beauty by] my own beloved." Even if we can supply the word "only," this is a feeble description of the **ineffable beauty** <note>original Greek: "ἀπόρρητον κάλλος" — beauty too great for words</note>, and I believe that this line was intended by the writer to mean "so that I thought she

<column-break/>

was another like my own." The numeral here, as in "A second Adam," connotes not inferiority but a perfect match. Compare **"a second grief"** <note>original Greek: "δεύτερον ἄχος"</note>, from Homer's *Iliad*, book 23, line 46; and Michael Drayton’s "If Time . . . Can show a Second to so pure a Love."
2230 **"The horse"** <note>original Greek: "τὸν φάραν"</note>: masculine as used above in line 166, etc., but feminine in 2203, etc. See index.

# DIGENES AKRITES GRO V

“Tell me, maiden, first, how you spend your time here?
And for what reason did you settle in this desert?
Then you too shall know who I am.”
Then, sitting together on a low seat <note>The Greek term suggests a seat close to the ground, perhaps a rock or a root.</note>,
<margin>65</margin> she began to speak these things, sighing from her depths:
<margin>43 v</margin> “My homeland, young man, happens to be Mepherke <note>Also known as Martyropolis, a significant frontier city between Byzantine and Arab territories.</note>;
You have heard of Haplorrabdes, the emir of all people there—
he is my father, and my mother is Melanthia.
I fell in love with a Roman-born man <note>original: "Ρωμαιογενῆ" (Romaiogenē). To the narrator, "Roman" means a Byzantine Greek.</note> to my own misfortune,
<margin>70</margin> whom my father held prisoner for three years;
for indeed, he claimed to be the son of a glorious general.
I freed him from his bonds, I rescued him from prison,
I gave him swift horses <note>original: "pharia," a term for high-quality Arabian horses.</note>, the best of my father’s,
I made him a renowned ruler of Syria,
<margin>75</margin> with the counsel of my mother while my father was away,
for he was always accustomed to be busy in wars.
He seemed to have a great love for me,
as if he would die if he did not see me for even an hour;
but he was a fraud, as the end has shown.
<margin>80</margin> For one day, having planned an escape,
wishing to depart for Romanía <note>The Byzantine Empire.</note>,
he told me his plan, and the fear he had
concerning my father, lest he should ever return;
and he compelled me to go away with him,
<margin>85</margin> promising with most terrifying oaths
never to deny me, but to have me as his wife.
Believing these things, I agreed to flee with him;
then we both sought to find an opportunity
to take the wealth of my parents.
<margin>90</margin> And indeed, by some bitter and demonic fate,
an illness came upon my mother, bordering on death;

67 **Haplorrabdes.** [The text] "which in any case" [is corrected]; the correction is borrowed from the manuscripts of Trebizond (near verse 1664) and Andros (near verse 2552).

---

**2236 on a low seat.** *Thokos* (Ionic dialect for *thakos*) is a word used by Homer <note>Referring to the ancient Greek poet of the Iliad and Odyssey; the author is using "epic" language here.</note>.
**2238 Mepherke.** Mayyafariqin or Martyropolis, one of the Arab frontier towns like Melitene which were the scenes of much fighting in the tenth century. Haplorrabdes probably con-

<column-break/>

ceals the name of Abu Taghlib of Mepherke, who was an ally of Bardas Skleros in 976 (Honigmann, p. 150).
**2239 the emir of all** is obscure, but occurs in all three versions; probably understand it as **of the people of Mepherke**.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Book Five of the Grottaferrata version of Digenes Akrites. In the previous section, the hero Digenes encountered a distraught woman in the desert. # DIGENES AKRITES

> “First tell me, maiden, how are you living here?
> For what reason have you made your home in this wilderness?
> Then you shall also learn who I am.”

We sat together on a seat near the ground <note>The Greek suggests a humble seat, such as a rock or the roots of a tree.</note>,
And sighing deeply from her heart, she began to speak:
> “My own country, young man, is Mepherke <note>Also known as Martyropolis, a significant city on the frontier between the Byzantine and Arab worlds.</note>.
> You have heard of Haplorrabdes, the Emir of all those people;
> <margin>2240</margin> He is my father, and my mother is Melanthia.
> I fell in love with a Roman-born man <note>original: "Rhōmaiogenē"—to the narrator, "Roman" means a Byzantine Greek.</note> to my own misfortune,
> One whom my father held captive for three years;
> He claimed he was the son of a famous general;
> I loosed his chains and delivered him from prison,
> I gave him horses—my father's finest champions <note>original: "pharia," a term for high-quality Arabian horses.</note>—
> And I made him a renowned ruler in Syria,
> With my mother’s consent while my father was away,
> For he was always occupied with wars.
> Then he appeared to have such great love for me,
> <margin>2250</margin> As if he would die if he did not see me for even a little while;
> But he was false, as the outcome has shown.
> For on a certain day, having planned an escape,
> And wishing to depart for Romania <note>The Byzantine Empire.</note>,
> He told me his desire and the fear he felt
> Because of my father, in case he should return.
> He tried to pressure me <note>The Greek verb implies he was "violently persuading" her rather than successfully forcing her at this stage.</note> to set out with him,
> Promising with the most dreadful vows
> Never to reject me, but to make me his spouse;
> Trusting him, I agreed to flee with him.
> <margin>2260</margin> We both began to look for an opportunity
> So that we might take the riches of my parents.
> Then, by some bitter and fateful misfortune,
> A disease fell upon my mother, bringing her near to death;

***

<columns>2</columns>

2241 *Roman-born*. <note>original: "Rhōmaiogenēs"</note>. The Trebizond manuscript (TRE 1666) and Andros manuscript (AND 2554) use the variant *Rhōmogenēs*.

2240 *Melanthia*. Note another Greek-named wife of an Arabian emir, recalling *Spathia* <note>The name likely derives from the Greek "Spathē" (sword).</note>, mother of the emir Mousour (TRE 488 and following), also called *Panthia* (above line 284, and PAS 323).

2245 *preeminence/first place*. <note>original: "prōteia"</note>. See above line 1796.

2246 *I showed/revealed*. <note>original: "edeixa"</note>. See above line 2177.

2252 *flight/escape*. <note>original: "drasmon"</note>. See below line 2978.

<column-break/>

2256 *he tried to compel*. <note>original: "katēnagkaze"</note>. Exactly as seen below in line 3268; it does not mean 'compelled' (as a completed action), but 'tried to compel' or 'was violently persuading.'

2260, 2266 *opportunity*. <note>original: "adeian"</note>—a word used here with a meaning between the ancient sense of 'security' and the modern Greek sense of 'permission.'

2263 *being near to her mother*. <note>original: "mētri . . . geitniōsa"</note>. A fine example of a grammatical disagreement between the participle and the noun it modifies. However, the Trebizond manuscript (TRE 1694) uses *geitniōsē* (agreeing with "mother").

### DIGENES AKRITES GRO V

changing my appearance into the clothing of a youth;
<margin>44 v^o.</margin> for in such a guise I had left my homeland <note>It was common in medieval literature for women traveling long distances to disguise themselves as men for safety.</note>.

led the other by hand and galloped away.
<margin>125</margin> When I saw this unreasonable and unexpected sight,
I ran after him on foot, just as I was, shouting.

### DIGENES AKRITES

All others mourning who were in the house

Love's changes celebrating without fill,
His inner purpose craftily concealed

### DIGENES AKRITES GRO V

> “Where are you going, my dearest, leaving me here all alone?
> Have you forgotten the kindnesses I showed you?
> Do you not remember from the start those solemn oaths?” <meta>130</meta>
But since he did not turn back, I cried out even louder:
> “Have mercy, take pity, save my wretched soul,
> Do not leave me here to be devoured by wild beasts.”
And I said many other things to him, singing my laments;
But he vanished into the distance without uttering a word;
And then, with my feet already exhausted <meta>135</meta>
From striking against the rocks, bleeding from every side,
I collapsed right there, sprawled out like a corpse.
And when day came, having barely recovered myself, <note>original: "meth’ hēmeras" — usually translated as 'after a day' or 'at daybreak'. See the note below.</note>
I returned to the spring, walking out of necessity,
And I am bereft of everything, possessing no hope; <meta>140</meta>
For I do not dare to return to my parents,
And I am ashamed before my neighbors and those of my own age.
Where to find the one who utterly misled me, I do not know,
And I beg you to give a sword into my hands,
So that I may strike myself down for having acted so foolishly; <meta>145</meta>
For it does not profit me to live, having lost everything.
Oh, my misfortunes! Oh, greatest of calamities!
I have become a stranger to my kin, separated from my parents
To gain my beloved, and even of him I have been deprived.”

I, as much as I could, stayed her from her mourning;
I gently pulled her hands away from her hair,
Comforting her so that she might have better hopes.
And I asked to learn how many days had passed <meta>155</meta>
Since that deceiver had left her here alone.
And she, sighing once more, said: “Ten days
I have completed until now in this wilderness,
Seeing no other human face but yours.”

# DIGENES AKRITES

'Are you going, my dearest? Where are you leaving me alone?
<margin>2300</margin>

I am bereft of everything, without any hope;
For I do not dare return to my parents,

I do not know at all where to find the one who betrayed me.
I beg you, put a knife into my hands,

It is no use for me to live now that all is lost.
O my misfortunes and most great disasters!

2327 'How many—here?' <note>original: πόσαι—ἐνταῦθα;</note> Legrand <note>Emile Legrand, a 19th-century French scholar who edited the first modern editions of the poem</note> omits inverted commas here, making this

quickly mounted while holding my spear,

Some of them, recognizing me, said to one another:

reveal Akrites; we are all lost.”
Hearing this, they fled back into the marsh.

---

...a fault in an otherwise well managed narrative. This shows that the editor <note>referred to as the redactor</note> of the Grottaferrata manuscript <note>GRO</note> was rearranging a version in which—as is currently found in the Trebizond <note>TRE</note> and Andros <note>AND</note> versions—Digenes’s fight with Mousour, the border bandit and highwayman, had been described at the beginning of the book (TRE 1617, AND 2504). The editor of the Grottaferrata version rightly cut out this preliminary to begin at once with the palm-tree and the weeping lady, but forgot to add some ex-

<column-break/>

planation of Mousour here, although he brings it in later, at line 2388.

**2340** *estasiase* <note>original: ἐστασίασε, meaning "he rose up" or "he attacked"</note>. Perhaps read *enstasiase* <note>original: ἐνστασίασε</note>, from the Trebizond manuscript line 1766, *enstasisas* <note>original: ἐνστασιάσας</note>; compare the uses of *enstatikos* <note>original: ἐνστατικός, meaning "resistant" or "opposing"</note> in the Liddell & Scott Greek dictionary, and below of *enstatos* <note>original: ἔνστατος</note> (line 2695) and *enstatikos* <note>original: ἐνστατικῶς</note> (line 3208).

**2349** A typical nominative absolute <note>A grammatical construction where a noun and participle stand apart from the main syntax of the sentence.</note>.

**2353** *polla phthareis* <note>original: πολλὰ φθαρείς, used here for "much startled" or "agitated"</note>. For this use of the verb *phtheiro* <note>original: φθείρω, which usually means "to destroy," but can colloquially mean "to get lost" or "to go away"</note>, compare Euripides' play *Andromache*, line 708: "if you do not get lost... from this house" <note>original: εἰ μὴ φθερῇ . . . τῆσδ᾽ ἀπὸ στέγης</note>.

# DIGENES AKRITES
->157<-

And I shall prepare him to take you as his wife,
If you will renounce the faith of the base Ethiopian. <note>original: "Aethiops." In Byzantine literature, this term was often used to describe Arabs or Muslims, equating dark skin with a perceived "darkness" of the soul.</note>
When she heard this, she was filled with joy,
"My lord," she answered, "and my greatest protector,"
"I had already partaken of holy baptism
Before being joined to this man, at his command;
<margin>2400</margin>For I, enslaved by my desire, could not
Fail to bring to pass what he had said,
For whose sake I considered parents and kin as nothing."
When I heard these words, my friend, from the girl’s mouth,
It was as if a flame rose up into my heart,
Suggesting love and an unlawful union.
At first, I set aside this unrestrained intent,
Wishing, if I could, to escape from sin;
But fire can never remain safely near dry grass. <note>A common medieval metaphor comparing sexual temptation to fire and the human body to flammable hay.</note>
And when I took her up onto my own horse
<margin>2410</margin>And we set out for the place called Coppermines
(Which was a location nearby in Syria),
I no longer knew who I was; I was made of fire,
As passion grew uncontrollably within me;
And when we stopped to rest for our physical needs—
While my eyes took in her beauty and my hands felt her touch,
My mouth her kisses and my ears her words—
I began to commit every lawless act,
And every deed I desired was fully done.
Our journey was defiled by this lawlessness
<margin>2420</margin>Through the help of Satan and my soul’s own neglect,
Even though the woman strongly opposed the act,
Calling upon God and the souls of her parents.
The Adversary, that champion of darkness,
The foe and enemy of all our human race,
Made me ready to forget God Himself
And the judgment of that terrible day.

# BOOK SIX

->The sixth book presented here relates many brave deeds,<-
->recounting the wonders of Digenes Akrites,<-
->as he himself told them to his own friends.<-

->\* \* \*<-

If one wished to appoint a king of the months,
May would reign over all the others; 5
it is the most delightful ornament <note>original: "κόσμος" (kosmos). Usually meaning "world," here used in the sense of "adornment" or "order."</note> of the whole earth,
the eye of all plants and the brightness of flowers,
the blush of the meadows and a gleaming beauty.
It breathes wondrous love and brings forth Aphrodite <note>The name of the Greek goddess is used here to personify physical love and desire.</note>; 9
it prepares the earth itself to mimic heaven, 10
adorning it with blossoms, with roses and narcissi.
In this truly wondrous and most sweet month,
I desired to move away alone with my fair one,
the beautiful daughter of the General, the Duke.
And so, having gone to a certain wonderful meadow, 15
there I pitched the tent and our own bed,
<margin>Folio 48 recto.</margin>
having placed all kinds of plants around it.
Reeds grew there, reaching to a great height,
and cool water bubbled up in the middle of the meadow 19
and ran everywhere across all that land. 20
Many kinds of birds dwelt in the grove:
tame peacocks, parrots, and swans.
The parrots sang while hanging from the branches,
the swans made their feeding grounds in the waters,
and the peacocks, circling their wings among the flowers, 25
reflected the color of the blossoms in their feathers.

**1** The first "the" is missing in the original. **3** Instead of "as," it is not necessary to read "which," as in the Trebizond manuscript (around line 1858). **6** "Delightfulness." **8** "Outshining." **13** "Me." **16** "They pitched." Correction provided by the Trebizond manuscript (around line 1878) and the Andros manuscript (around line 2834). **23** "Branches." Correction provided by the Trebizond manuscript (around line 1896) and the Andros manuscript (around line 2825).

2465 Regarding this set piece on the "King of Months," which appears also in other versions (Trebizond 1861 ff., Andros 2816–23, 2871, 2872), see Achilles Tatius ii. i. 2, and Meliteniotes 32 ff. See also the phrase "On a May morning" at the beginning of

<column-break/>

### THE sixth and present book of many deeds <note>The original uses "gests," an archaic term for heroic deeds or historical exploits.</note>
### Recording the wonders of Digenes the Borderer, <note>Digenes Akrites, the "Borderer," is the legendary hero of the Byzantine-Arab frontiers.</note>
### Even as he himself told them to his own friends.

->*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*<-

‘If any would suppose a king of months,
Of all the months the month of May is king;
He is all earth’s most pleasant ornament,
The eye of all plants and the brilliance of the flowers,
<margin>2470</margin>The blush and beauty of the meadows flashing,
He breathes wondrous passions and brings in love-making;
He sets the earth to imitate the sky,
Decking it with blooms of rose and daffodil.
In this wonderful and sweetest month,
I was pleased to travel, alone with my good wife, <note>The original "καλή" (kalē) literally means "beautiful," but in this context, it is a term of endearment for his wife.</note>
The lovely daughter of the General Doukas.
So, when we came to a wondrous meadow,
There I set up my tent and my own bed,
Having put every sort of plant around it.
Rushes were growing there, rising on high,
<margin>2480</margin>And in the mid-meadow, gushing up cool water
Was running everywhere through all that land.
Several kinds of birds lived in the grove:
Tame peacocks were there, and parrots, and the swans.
The parrots, hanging on the branches, sang;
The swans found their own pasture in the waters;
Circling their wings among the flowers, the peacocks
Flashed back the hue of flowers in their wings;

---

sense so late, but classical; also below 3757.
2469 “flashing” <note>original: ἀπαστράπτων (apastraptōn)</note> agreeing with May; but Legrand unnecessarily reads it as a neuter “flashing.” <note>original: ἀπαστράπτον (apastrapton)</note>
2471 “he prepares it.” <note>original: αὐτὴν παρασκευάζει (autēn paraskeuazei)</note> Legrand reads “he himself” <note>original: αὐτός (autos)</note> from the Trebizond manuscript of 1867.
2474 “with my fair one.” <note>original: μὲ τῆς καλῆς μου (me tēs kalēs mou)</note> See above 2453, etc. I think “fair” <note>original: καλή (kalē)</note> is always used with the connotation of ‘wife,’ as in the modern language.
2486, 2487 “wings,” “with wings”; <note>original: πτέρυγας (pterygas), πτέρυξι (pteryxi)</note> 2488
<column-break/>
“feathers”; <note>original: πτερά (ptera)</note> but there is no distinction of meaning.
2487 “shone back.” <note>original: ἀντέλαμπον (antelampon)</note> At first sight this line would seem to mean ‘outshone the flowers with their wings.’ But “shone back” <note>original: ἀντι-λάμπω (anti-lampō)</note> cannot mean ‘outshine’; and the line must mean rather ‘answered the color of the flowers with the brightness of their wings.’ Cf. below 3349, where the line is repeated with slight variations. Line 2479 is also repeated below 3329.

# DIGENES AKRITES BOOK VI
### 164

And the rest, possessing wings that are free,
Played, perched upon the branches of the trees.
And the beauty of the noble girl, outflashing all,
<margin>30</margin>Shone better than a peacock and than every plant;
For her face imitated the color of the narcissus,
Her cheeks bloomed like a flourishing rose;
Her lips revealed the flower of a newly grown rose,
Just as it begins to sprout from its buds;
<margin>35</margin>Locks of hair resting far down upon her eyebrows
Sent forth golden-delighting rays in great abundance,
And through it all there was an unutterable joy.
Around the bed, confections <note>original: "πέμματα" (pëmmata). While this usually refers to cakes or sweetmeats, the following lines suggest these are incense or solid perfumes being burned.</note> of all kinds were smoking:
Musk, nitai <note>an aromatic substance, identified in the notes below as nutmeg.</note>, and ambergris, camphor, and cassia;
<margin>40</margin>And there was the greatest pleasure and the scent of joy;
Such delight did that paradise possess.
At the midday hour I turned toward sleep,
While the noble girl sprinkled me with rosewater,
<margin>48 vº.</margin>And the nightingales <note>The Greek "ἀδονίδων" (adonidōn) refers to nightingales, a common symbol of romantic longing in Byzantine poetry.</note> and the rest of the birds sang their melodies.
<margin>45</margin>But the girl, becoming thirsty, went off to the spring;
And as she was there, delightedly wetting her feet,
A dragon <note>The "drakon" in Byzantine romance is often a shapeshifting serpent or monster capable of human deception.</note>, having shaped himself into a handsome youth,
Came toward her, wishing to lead her astray;
But she, not at all ignorant of who he was, said:
<margin>50</margin>“Cease, dragon, what you wish; I am not deceived,
My lover has kept watch and now he sleeps
(For she said within herself: this is a dragon,
Never have I seen such a sight here before);
If he wakes and finds you, he will surely bring you to justice.”

**29** outflashing. **32** sprouted. **35-36** Between these two verses, one or more have probably been forgotten. **38** confections. **40** "the" is missing. **44** nightingales. **53** never. here.

The garden possessed such a great delight.

About the midday hour I turned to sleep
While the noble Girl sprinkled me with rosewater,
And nightingales with other birds made music.
The Girl, becoming thirsty, went down to the spring,

> I never saw such a face <note>original: "opsin," which can mean either face or general appearance.</note> here before”—
> “If he wakes up and finds you, he shall do you harm.”

...quoting Psellos, and then a Spanish medical dictionary: ‘Ned, an aromatic composition or *neda*. *Ned* first, a highly aromatic incense <note>original Spanish: "sahumerio muy aromatico"</note>.’ Professor V. 2501 *Pleasure* <note>original Greek: "ἡδονή" (hēdonē)</note> here, especially as combined with *scent* <note>original Greek: "ὀσμή" (osmē)</note>, may mean ‘sound’; see above at line 1338.

(sub voce)</note>.

2508 *Serpent* <note>original Greek: "δράκων" (drakōn)</note>. Like the serpent of the *Iliad* (book 11, line 39 and book 12, line 202), this is no ordinary snake, and is well on the way to becoming the *drakos*, or ogre, of modern Greek folk tales.

2514 *Appearance* <note>original Greek: "ὄψιν" (opsin)</note>, meaning either ‘face’ or ‘appearance’.

But he, leaping forward shamelessly, attempted to violate her; <margin>55</margin>
and the Girl immediately let out a cry, calling me:
> “Wake up, my lord, and save your dearest one!”

When that voice echoed in my heart,
I sat up quickly and saw the molester;
for the spring was conveniently right in front of me, <margin>60</margin>
and drawing my sword <note>original: "spathin," the standard Byzantine straight sword</note>, I reached the spring,
for my feet ran as swiftly as wings.
And when I overtook him, he revealed to me an apparition <note>original: "phantasma"</note>
gruesome and terrifying to men, and massive,
with three enormous heads, entirely blazing with fire; <margin>65</margin>
from each one he sent out a flashing flame.
Moving from his spot, he made a sound like thunder,
so that the earth and all the trees seemed to shake.
Swelling his body, pulling his heads together into one,
<margin>49 r°.</margin> <note>Folio 49 recto of the Grottaferrata manuscript.</note> thinning out behind and sharpening his tail, <margin>70</margin>
sometimes contracting, and then stretching out again,
he made his whole rush upon me.
But I, considering these sights as nothing,
raising my sword high with all my spirit,
brought it down upon the heads of that most terrible beast, <margin>75</margin>
and I took them all off at once; he was stretched out on the earth,
twitching his tail up and down for the last time.
And having wiped the sword and placed it in the scabbard,
I called my servants <note>original: "paidas," literally "boys" or attendants</note> who were some distance away,
and I ordered the dragon <note>original: "drakonta"</note> to be carried away immediately. <margin>80</margin>
When this was done faster than words can tell,
the servants ran back to their own tents,
and I went back to the bed to sleep again,
for the sweet sleep I was having drew me back;
for I had not yet been satisfied with it when I first woke up. <margin>85</margin>
But the Girl, moved to immoderate laughter,
bearing the images of the dragon in her memory
and the swift death of that massive creature,
went out toward a certain tree so as not to wake me,

He sprang and shamelessly tried violence:
The Girl at once cried out calling for me:
“Wake up, my master, and take back your darling.”
And when her voice had sounded in my heart,
<margin>2520</margin>
I sat up quickly and I saw the troubler
(For by design the spring was opposite)—
I drew my sword, and I was at the spring,
For and my feet ran dazzlingly like wings;
And when I reached him he showed me an appearance
Horrible, dreadful among men, and great,
Three well-grown heads he had all fiery-flaming,
From each of them a flashing flame sent out;
Moving from his place he made a noise of thunder,
So the earth seemed to shake and all the trees.
<margin>2530</margin>
His body thick, joining the heads in one,
Behind was slender, tapering to a tail,
Now gathered himself up, and then stretched out,
And right against me all his onset made.
But I counting as nothing what was seen
On high with all my spirit raised my sword,
Down brought it on the dreadful beast his heads,
Took all together; on the ground stretched out
He lashed his tail up and down for the last time.
I wiped my sword and put it in its sheath,
<margin>2540</margin>
Called up my servants who were some way off
And bade them straightway take the serpent up.
When this was done quickly beyond the telling
My boys ran off again to their own tents,
And I too to my bed to sleep again;
Sweet sleep I had been slumbering drew me back,
For I had woken still unsatisfied.
The Girl now moved to measureless laughter,
Remembering the snake’s appearances
And of that magnitude the quick-cut death,
<margin>2550</margin>
So as not to wake me went away to a tree,

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the previous page, where the border-raider Ioannakes and his men were introduced. # DIGENES AKRITES BOOK VI

And as they went along their way, they heard the songs,
<margin>125</margin>being one mile <note>original: μίλλιον; a Roman mile</note> away from us, as I reckon,
and turning out from the road, they came near to us.
And when they caught sight of the celebrated maiden,
their souls were wounded by her beauty as if by an arrow,
and they were all moved to a boundless desire,
<margin>130</margin>being forty and five in number.
Seeing me alone, they hoped to wound me with words:
“Leave the girl,” they said, “and save yourself;
if not, you shall earn death for your disobedience.”
For they did not yet know what kind of man I happen to be.
<margin>135</margin>But the sun-born <note>original: ἡλιογέννητος; a poetic epithet for the heroine, suggesting she was born of the sun's own light</note> girl, suddenly seeing them
all fully armed and seated upon their horses,
believing their words, was struck with great fear,
and covering her face with her veil,
she ran toward the tent, utterly terrified.
<margin>140</margin>But I said to her: “Why do you not speak, my dearest?”
“Because,” she said, “my voice has died before my soul;
for behold, we are being separated, and I cannot bear to live.”
“Cease, my soul,” I said, “to think such things;
what God has joined together, let no man put asunder.” <note>A quotation from the Gospel of Matthew 19:6</note>
<margin>145</margin>Straightway I took up my staff <note>original: ῥάβδον; a heavy mace or club often used by Digenes</note> and my buckler <note>original: χειροσκουτάριν; a small hand-shield</note>,
and like an eagle flying down from the heights upon partridges,
as many as my staff reached to touch,
no remnant of life was left in them at all.
<margin>50 vº.</margin>And many who wished to flee, I overtook them,
<margin>150</margin>for no horse ever defeated me in a race.
And I do not proclaim these things to pride myself,
but so that you may learn of the gifts of the Creator.
Some escaped my notice by hiding in the marshes;
and before delivering them all to death,
<margin>155</margin>I took one alive, from whom I learned who
these senseless and frantic men were.

# DIGENES AKRITES
### Page 171

As they went on the road they heard our songs,
distant one mile from us, as I suppose,
and riding off from it came near to us.
When they beheld the wonderful Girl alone,
their souls wounded as by her beauty's arrow,
they were moved all of them to boundless love,
forty of them in number and five more,
<margin>2590</margin>
saw me alone and hoped to wound with words,
saying, "Give up the girl and save yourself;
or else you shall find death for disobedience."
For yet they did not know me who I am.
The sunborn <note>original: ἡλιογέννητος (heliogennetos); a common epic term for a heroine of radiant beauty</note> maid suddenly seeing them
all of them armed there sitting on their horses,
their words believing feared exceedingly,
and with her veil covering up her face
she ran quite terrified into the tent.
I said to her, "Why, dearest, speak you not?"
<margin>2600</margin>
"Because", she said, "my voice died ere my soul;
look we are parted, I will not bear to live."
I answered, "Cease, my soul, to think such things;
whom God has joined men shall not put asunder." <note>A direct reference to the Gospel of Matthew 19:6</note>
I took at once my club <note>original: ῥάβδον (rabdon); a heavy iron mace or staff</note> and handbuckler,
flew out as eagle on partridges from a height,
as many as my club but only touched
no trace of life was left in them at all;
many wanting to fly, I caught them up,
for no horse ever beat me at running.
<margin>2610</margin>
(This I declare not to exalt myself
but that you fully learn the Creator's gifts.)
A few escaped me hidden in the marshes;
and just before putting them all to death,
took one alone alive, from him learned who
they were, these senseless ones, out of their wits;

# DIGENES AKRITES
## BOOK VI

And boiling over with rage, I spared no one;
then I threw down my sword and my hand-buckler,
I shook out my sleeve <note>original: τὸ μανίκιν ἔσειον (to manikin eseion); a gesture of shaking down a sleeve rolled up for a fight, signaling the end of a task or "good riddance"</note> and went toward the girl.
But the girl, seeing that I alone was left alive,
<margin>160</margin> came out to meet me, filled with joy,
and she sprinkled rose-water <note>original: ῥοδόσταμμα (rodostamma); a traditional Byzantine gesture of celebration or welcome</note> with her own hands,
kissing my right hand and praying for my long life.
And I, wishing to reproach her for her fear,
spoke to her with playful words of love:
<margin>165</margin> "Must I die before I even suffer, just as you did?" <note>Digenes is teasing her for "dying of fright" before the battle even began.</note>
But she, understanding what I meant, smiled sweetly:
"I saw the multitude," she said, "of the horsemen suddenly,
all of them fully armed, and you on foot and alone;
at this, my lord, fear came upon me."
<margin>170</margin> And after a thousand kisses, we went into the tent,
and the next day I went off to the river to wash,
so that I might change my clothing, which was stained with blood;
and I told the girl to bring me another set.
And so, having arrived near the water,
<margin>175</margin> I sat down by a tree and waited for the girl.
And behold, three beautiful horsemen <note>original: καβαλλάροι (kaballaroi); from the Latin 'caballarius', referring to knights or mounted soldiers</note> appeared;
they were wearing distinct types of clothing,
and they came toward me, following the river.
For they saw me sitting at the root of the tree,
<margin>180</margin> and coming near, they all greeted me.
But I did not stand up for them, but remained sitting. <note>In the Byzantine social code, remaining seated when others approach is a sign of superior status or extreme confidence.</note>
"Brother, have you seen any soldiers like those who were here?"
And I answered them, not being afraid at all:
"Yes, I saw them," I said, "brothers, during the day yesterday;
<margin>185</margin> for they wanted to snatch away my wife..."

2619 "I shook out my sleeve" (also found in the Trebizond version 2048, the Andros version 3013, and the Escorial version 1185). This means primarily and literally the shaking down of a loose long sleeve which has been rolled up for the fight; but also connotes a gesture of shaking the fingers, implying "good riddance." See *Laografia* <note>A journal of Greek folklore</note> volume 3, page 701, and volume 4, page 327. It is to be found also, for example, in a Cypriot

<column-break/>

# DIGENES AKRITES
## BOOK VI

And with rage overboiling I spared no one,
Then threw aside my sword and hand-buckler,
And shook my sleeve <note>original: τὸ μανίκιν ἔσειον (to manikin eseion); a gesture of shaking down a sleeve rolled up for a fight, signifying the completion of a task</note>, and came back to the Girl.
<margin>2620</margin>
And the Girl, when she saw me left alone,
Came out to meet me, filled with joyfulness,
With her own hands she threw rose-water <note>original: ῥοδόσταμμα (rodostamma); a traditional Byzantine floral water used in welcoming ceremonies</note> over me,
Kissing my right hand as she wished me long life.
And I, wishing to scold her for her fear,
Began to speak with feigned words of love:
“Do I, like you, die before I am even hurt?” <note>Digenes is playfully teasing her for being "scared to death" before the fight even began.</note>
She, knowing what I spoke of, smiled sweetly,
Saying, “Suddenly I saw the crowd of horsemen,
All of them armed and you on foot alone;
<margin>2630</margin>
That, my lord, was why fear came over me.”
We kissed a thousand times and went into the tent.
The next day I went down to the river to wash,
So that I might change my garment, which was stained with blood,
And I told the Girl to bring me out another.
So then I came down to the water’s edge,
Sat by a tree, and waited for the Girl.
When, behold, there appeared three fine cavaliers <note>original: καβαλλάροι (kaballaroi); meaning knights or mounted soldiers</note>,
All were dressed up in exquisite array,
And they came up to me, hugging the river bank;
<margin>2640</margin>
They saw me sitting there at the tree’s root,
And coming near, they all saluted me.
I did not rise for them, but rather remained seated.
“Have you seen any such soldiers here, brother?”
I answered them with no fear at all:
“Yes,” I said, “brothers, I saw them yesterday,
For they wanted to carry off my wife;”

He came sharply at me, hoping to frighten me;
for he truly possessed a courageous spirit.
He struck a sharp blow with his sword upon my buckler <note>A buckler is a small, round shield used for parrying.</note>,

I struck him on the head with my staff <note>original: ῥάβδῳ (rhabdo); often a heavy mace or wooden club</note>,

not a single bone would have been left whole in his head.

feeling no shame, despite their previous boasting.

It was a chaotic melee and a stubborn fight;
Kinnamos was eager to dodge behind me,
while Ioannakis struck fast and straight.
Then I saw that they were truly tested warriors,
but none of them had the power to overcome me.
<margin>2700</margin>Whenever I brandished my staff around me,
they all fled from me as if from a lion,
watching me from afar like sheep,
and then coming back like barking dogs.

It is never my custom to strike a fallen man.
But, if you wish, stand up and take your weapons,
and we shall face one another as is fitting for brave men;
for striking fallen bodies is the way of the weak."
<margin>270</margin> But he showed his submission through gestures,
for he did not have the strength to speak, gripped by trembling.
Leaving him there, I turned back
and saw Philopappous coming to his senses,
shaking his head and saying these words:
<margin>275</margin> "By the God who created heaven and all the earth,
who has adorned you with every grace,
leave off this war and make peace <note>original: ἀγάπην (agapen); here used in the sense of a peaceful reconciliation or a bond of friendship</note>,
and we shall be your servants, if you yourself command it,
taking orders from you and serving without hesitation."
<margin>53 r^o. 280</margin> When I heard these friendly words, I took pity on them
(for submissive words have a way of softening anger),
and smiling at him with a touch of irony <note>The original Greek text uses "eironeia," suggesting a sophisticated, perhaps slightly mocking, sense of superiority.</note>, I said:
"Philopappous, you have woken up and now you speak of visions;
but since you have turned your old age toward contrition <term>katanyxis: a Byzantine term for a state of spiritual humility or "piercing" of the heart</term>,
<margin>285</margin> stand up, take those who are with you, and go wherever you wish,
having your own eyes as witnesses to what has taken place."

<summary>Digenes decisively defeats Kinnamos by striking his horse with a staff and then shows knightly mercy by refusing to strike a fallen opponent. Philopappous recovers his senses and offers total submission, leading Digenes to grant them their lives with a bit of ironic commentary on their failed ambush.</summary>
<keywords>Kinnamos, Philopappous, chivalry, mercy, horse-combat, submission, lion imagery</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the encounter between the hero Digenes Akrites and the three frontier lords. On the previous page, Digenes had already stunned the elderly Philopappous. Here, the second lord, Kinnamos, attempts an attack but is swiftly defeated, leading to Philopappous’s plea for mercy and Digenes's ironic response.</meta>

# DIGENES AKRITES
## Page 179

Immediately his sword fell to the ground;
A little past me he fell off his horse,
And leaned against a rock clutching the pain.
Kinnamos wanting to play the brave alone,
Now having urged his own horse up and down,
Trusting the wretch that he should scare the lion <note>Digenes often uses the lion as a symbol of his solitary, predatory strength against his enemies.</note>,
Came charging down upon me with his horse;
<margin>2720</margin> My staff struck it a blow between the withers,
From which blood in a river flowed at once,
From its temples and from the nag’s very mouth,
Straightway in a heap it fell with Kinnamos.
Then terror seized on him and fear constrained,
Thinking that I would hit him lying down.
I said to him, “Kinnamos, why are you trembling?
My use has never been to strike the fallen.
But, if you like, rise up and take your weapons,
And we will face to it, as brave men should.
<margin>2730</margin> It is for weaklings to hit carcasses.”
And he by signs was showing his subjection,
For in his fright he had no power to speak.
Leaving him there I turned backwards again
Saw Philopappos coming to himself,
Moving his head and speaking on this wise:
“By God who made the heaven and all the earth,
Who has embellished you with all His favours,
Abandon war and make an understanding,
And we will be your servants, as you bid,
<margin>2740</margin> By you enjoined and working without rest.”
Hearing such friendliness I pitied them
(Anger is softened by submissive words)—
And smiling at him said with irony,
“Awake, Philopappos, telling us your dreams?
Since you have turned your old age to contrition,
Rise, take those with you, and go where you will,
Your proper eyes shall witness what was done;

<summary>This page depicts Digenes Akrites easily defeating Kinnamos and then showing mercy to the defeated Philopappous, who begs for peace. The scene highlights the hero's chivalric code of not striking a fallen opponent and his superiority over the older lords.</summary>
